GOLD WATER V CARTER
Facts of the Case
In 1978, President Jimmy Carter acted to normalize diplomatic relations between the United States and the People's Republic of China (PRC). As a prerequisite for this normalization, the PRC demanded that the U.S. terminate its 1954 Mutual Defense Treaty with the Republic of China (Taiwan).
Article X of that 1954 treaty provided that either party could terminate the agreement upon one year’s notice to the other. President Carter, acting unilaterally (without a vote from Congress), gave the required notice to Taiwan that the treaty would terminate on January 1, 1980.
Senator Barry Goldwater and several other members of the Senate and House filed suit against the President. They argued that because the Constitution requires the "advice and consent" of two-thirds of the Senate to make a treaty, the President must also obtain legislative approval (either from two-thirds of the Senate or a majority of both Houses) to terminate a treaty. They claimed the President’s unilateral action unconstitutionally deprived them of their legislative role.
Issue
Does the President of the United States have the authority under the Constitution to unilaterally terminate a mutual defense treaty, pursuant to a termination clause in the treaty itself, without the approval of the Senate or the Congress?
Rule
The Constitution is explicit in Article II, Section 2, regarding the "making" of treaties (requiring two-thirds Senate concurrence), but it is silent regarding their "unmaking" or termination.
Under the "Executive Power" of Article II, the President is generally recognized as the "sole organ" of the federal government in the field of international relations. Furthermore, the Supremacy Clause (Article VI) makes treaties the "supreme Law of the Land," but it does not specify that they must be repealed in the same manner as statutes.
Application (Court’s Reasoning)
Constitutional Silence: The court noted that the Framers specifically required Senate participation in making treaties to prevent the Executive from binding the nation too easily. However, they did not include a corresponding requirement for ending them.
Executive Authority in Foreign Affairs: The court emphasized that treaty termination is often a "political act" tied to the recognition of foreign governments—a power that belongs exclusively to the President. To require the President to maintain a treaty with a government he no longer recognizes would interfere with his core constitutional functions.
The Treaty's Own Terms: The court highlighted that the 1954 Treaty already contained a termination clause (Article X). When the Senate originally consented to the treaty, it consented to a document that included a specific mechanism for ending it. The President was simply following the "law" of the treaty itself.
Distinction from Statutes: The court rejected the argument that treaties are exactly like statutes (which require a repeal by Congress). While treaties are "law," they are also international compacts. The President’s role in foreign affairs gives him greater flexibility to withdraw from such compacts than he has to unilaterally repeal domestic law.
Conclusion
The D.C. Circuit reversed the district court’s ruling and held that the President's notice of termination was constitutionally effective. The court concluded that the President did not exceed his authority by acting unilaterally to terminate the treaty in accordance with its terms.
Rule of Law
The President has the constitutional power to unilaterally terminate a treaty, at least when the treaty contains a notice-of-termination provision and the action is taken as part of the President’s authority over foreign foreign relations. While the Senate must concur to enter a treaty, the President may exit a treaty without legislative consent if he is acting pursuant to the treaty's own terms and within his role as the primary conductor of the nation's foreign policy.
US DEC OF INDEPEN.
I. The Philosophical Preamble (The "Why")
Natural Law Foundation: The document asserts that rights do not come from the government or the King, but from the "Laws of Nature and of Nature’s God."
Unalienable Rights: It identifies fundamental rights—Life, Liberty, and the pursuit of Happiness—that are inherent to the individual and cannot be surrendered to the state.
Social Contract Theory: Borrowing heavily from John Locke, it posits that governments are "instituted among Men" solely to secure these rights, deriving their "just powers from the consent of the governed."
The Right of Revolution: When a government becomes "destructive of these ends," the people have a legal and moral right to "alter or to abolish it."
II. The Indictment (The "Breach of Duty")
The bulk of the document is a "bill of particulars"—a list of 27 grievances against King George III. For a law student, these are essentially counts in a complaint:
Interference with Legislation: Refusing to assent to laws necessary for the public good.
Judicial Dependency: Making judges dependent on the King’s will for tenure and salary (a direct precursor to Article III of the Constitution).
Procedural Due Process Violations: Depriving colonists of Trial by Jury and transporting them "beyond Seas" for trial.
Taxation Without Representation: Imposing taxes without consent (a breach of the social contract).
Military Overreach: Keeping standing armies in times of peace and making the military superior to civil power.
III. The Formal Declaration (The "Judgment")
Status Change: The colonies formally declare themselves "Free and Independent States."
Sovereign Powers: As independent states, they claim the full power to levy war, conclude peace, contract alliances, and establish commerce.
The Pledge: The signers conclude with a mutual pledge of "our Lives, our Fortunes and our sacred Honor."
Article I: The Name
Summary: Establishes the name of the confederacy as "The United States of America."
Legal Meaning: It identifies the entity, but notably uses the term "Confederacy" rather than "Union" or "Nation," signaling a loose collection of sovereigns rather than a single unified state.
Article II: State Sovereignty
Summary: Each state retains its sovereignty, freedom, and independence, and every power not expressly delegated to the United States.
Legal Meaning: This is the precursor to the 10th Amendment. However, the use of the word "expressly" created a very weak central government with no "implied powers" (a contrast to the McCulloch v. Maryland standard).
Article III: The "League of Friendship"
Summary: States enter into a "firm league of friendship" for common defense and mutual welfare.
Legal Meaning: This defines the relationship as a treaty among nations rather than a supreme national law. It’s a collective security agreement, not a government with direct authority over citizens.
Article IV: Privileges, Immunities, and Extradition
Summary: Citizens of each state get the "privileges and immunities" of citizens in others; provides for freedom of movement and extradition of fugitives.
Legal Meaning: This survived almost intact as Article IV of the Constitution. It prevents states from treating "out-of-staters" like foreigners, facilitating a sense of national citizenship.
Article V: The Unicameral Legislature
Summary: Establishes a one-house Congress where each state gets one vote, regardless of size. Delegates are appointed by state legislatures.
Legal Meaning: This reflects the "Small State" theory of governance. It made federal action difficult because it prioritized state-identity over population-based representation.
Article VI: Restrictions on States
Summary: States cannot send embassies, make treaties, or engage in war without Congressional consent (unless invaded).
Legal Meaning: An attempt to create a unified foreign policy. It sought to prevent individual states from dragging the whole league into private wars with European powers.
Article VII: Military Appointments
Summary: When land forces are raised, officers of the rank of Colonel and below are appointed by the state legislatures.
Legal Meaning: Further emphasizes state control; the "national" army was essentially a collection of state militias.
Article VIII: Federal Finances (The Fatal Flaw)
Summary: Costs of war and government are paid from a common treasury, supplied by states in proportion to their land value.
Legal Meaning: Congress had no power to tax. It could only "request" funds from states (a "requisition system"). This led to the bankruptcy of the central government.
Article IX: Powers of the Central Government
Summary: Congress has the sole power to declare war, manage Indian affairs, regulate coin/weights/measures, and serve as the "last resort" for boundary disputes between states.
Legal Meaning: While this looks like a lot of power, Congress lacked the enforcement mechanism to back it up. It functioned more as an arbiter than a ruler.
Article X: The Committee of the States
Summary: A committee can act for Congress when it is in recess.
Legal Meaning: An attempt to provide some executive continuity in the absence of a President, though it was largely ineffective.
Article XI: Canada Clause
Summary: Canada is invited to join the union; other colonies need the agreement of nine states.
Legal Meaning: An optimistic (and failed) geopolitical invitation to bring the remaining British North American colonies into the revolution.
Article XII: Public Debt
Summary: The new government assumes all debts contracted by Congress before the Articles.
Legal Meaning: A "Full Faith and Credit" move to ensure creditors that the revolution’s debts would be honored (crucial for international legitimacy).
Article XIII: Perpetual Union and Amendment
Summary: The Articles are "perpetual" and can only be altered if Congress and every single state legislature agrees.
Legal Meaning: The Unanimity Requirement. This made the Articles effectively un-amendable, which is why the Founders eventually chose to scrap them entirely and write the Constitution rather than try to fix them.
THE ARTICLES: The Operating System
Article I: The Legislative Branch (The "Power of the Purse")
Section 1: Vests all legislative power in a bicameral Congress (House and Senate). Legal meaning: The "Vesting Clause" prevents other branches from making laws.
Section 2: Rules for the House (2-year terms, age 25+, population-based).
Section 3: Rules for the Senate (6-year terms, age 30+, two per state).
Section 4-6: Election rules, internal procedures, and "Speech or Debate" immunity for members.
Section 7: The Presentment Clause. How a bill becomes law (passed by both houses + signed by President).
Section 8: The Enumerated Powers. The most important section for 1L. It lists what Congress can do, including the Commerce Clause, Taxing/Spending Power, and the Necessary and Proper Clause.
Section 9: Limits on Congress (No suspension of Habeas Corpus except in emergencies; no ex post facto laws).
Section 10: Limits on States (States can't print money or make treaties).
Article II: The Executive Branch (The "Power of the Sword")
Section 1: Vests executive power in the President. Sets the 4-year term, Electoral College, and qualifications (Natural born citizen, age 35+).
Section 2: Presidential Powers. Commander-in-Chief, the Pardon Power, and the Appointments Clause (hiring judges/officials with Senate "Advice and Consent").
Section 3: Duties. State of the Union, receiving ambassadors, and the Take Care Clause (ensuring laws are "faithfully executed").
Section 4: Impeachment. Removal for "Treason, Bribery, or other high Crimes and Misdemeanors."
Article III: The Judicial Branch
Section 1: Vests power in one Supreme Court and lower courts created by Congress. Provides for life tenure ("Good Behaviour") and salary protection to ensure independence.
Section 2: Jurisdiction. Defines what cases federal courts can hear (Federal Questions and Diversity).
Section 3: Defines Treason. The only crime specifically defined in the Constitution.
Article IV: State Relations (Horizontal Federalism)
Section 1: Full Faith and Credit. States must respect the public acts and records of other states (e.g., your marriage in NY is valid in CA).
Section 2: Privileges and Immunities. States cannot discriminate against citizens of other states. Also includes Extradition.
Section 3-4: Rules for admitting new states and the "Guarantee Clause" (guaranteeing every state a Republican form of government).
Article V: The Amendment Process
Explains how to "patch" the system. Requires 2/3 of Congress to propose and 3/4 of States to ratify. Meaning: It was designed to be difficult to change.
Article VI: The Supremacy Clause
The Constitution and Federal Laws are the "Supreme Law of the Land." If state law and federal law conflict, federal law wins. (See McCulloch v. Maryland).
Article VII: Ratification
Historical procedure: 9 of 13 states needed to approve the document for it to take effect.
THE AMENDMENTS: Plain English Summary
The Bill of Rights (1-10)
1st: You can speak, pray, publish, assemble, and complain to the government.
2nd: Right to own weapons (linked to a "well regulated Militia").
3rd: The government cannot force you to house soldiers.
4th: No "unreasonable" police searches or seizures; requires a warrant based on probable cause.
5th: The "Criminal Trial Rights." Right to a Grand Jury, no "Double Jeopardy," right to remain silent, and Due Process. Also, the government must pay you if they take your land (Eminent Domain).
6th: Right to a speedy trial, a lawyer, and to face your accusers.
7th: Right to a jury trial in civil (non-criminal) cases.
8th: No "cruel and unusual" punishment or crazy high bail.
9th: Just because a right isn't listed here doesn't mean you don't have it.
10th: Any power not given to the feds belongs to the States or the People.
The "Clean Up" & Post-War Amendments (11-15) 11. 11th: You can’t sue a state in federal court without its permission. 12. 12th: Fixes the Electoral College (President and VP run as a team). 13. 13th: Abolishes slavery. 14. 14th:The "Big One" for Law Students. Defines citizenship and guarantees Equal Protection and Due Process against state governments (not just federal). 15. 15th: You can't deny the vote based on race.
The Progressive Era & Modern Updates (16-27) 16. 16th: Congress can collect Income Tax. 17. 17th: People vote directly for Senators (instead of state legislatures picking them). 18. 18th: Prohibition (Alcohol is illegal). 19. 19th:Women get the right to vote. 20. 20th: Moves "Inauguration Day" to January to shorten the "Lame Duck" period. 21. 21st: Repeals the 18th (Alcohol is legal again). 22. 22nd: Presidents are limited to 2 terms. 23. 23rd: D.C. gets to vote for President. 24. 24th: No "Poll Taxes" (you don't have to pay to vote). 25. 25th: Sets the "Line of Succession" if the President dies or becomes disabled. 26. 26th: Lowers the voting age to 18. 27. 27th: If Congress votes themselves a pay raise, it doesn't kick in until after the next election.

Marbury v. Madison
1 Cranch (5 U.S.) 137 (1803)
Supreme Court of the United States
Chief Justice John Marshall
Procedural History - William Marbury filed an original action in the Supreme Court seeking a
writ of mandamus ordering Secretary of State James Madison to deliver his commission as a
Justice of the Peace for the District of Columbia. The claim relied on Section 13 of the
Judiciary Act of 1789 (The Supreme Court has the power to issue writs of mandamus in
cases warranted by the principles and usages of law), which purported to grant the
Supreme Court original jurisdiction to issue writs of mandamus. But The Constitution (Article
III) strictly limits the Supreme Court’s original jurisdiction to only:
●
Cases affecting ambassadors
●
Public ministers
●
Consuls
●
Cases where a state is a party
🚨 Marbury’s case fits none of these categories.
Madison = Secretary of State
Marbury = private citizen
But marshall said he had a right to his commission and right to redress under the law for
deprivation of that right
But the court couldnt issue the write because its at odds w article 3 of the constitution that lays
out the SCOTUS original jurisdiction
The power of judicial review to see if they pass constitutional muster . this did not pass and the
court declinedSupreme courts power of judicial review.
Facts- In the final days of President John Adams’s administration, several Federalist judicial
commissions were signed and sealed but not delivered before Thomas Jefferson took office.
William Marbury was among those appointed. The Jefferson administration refused to deliver
the undelivered commissions. Marbury petitioned the Supreme Court directly for a writ of
mandamus compelling delivery.
Issues
1. 2. 3. 4. Did Marbury have a legal right to his commission?
If Marbury had a right and it was violated, did the law afford him a remedy?
Was a writ of mandamus from the Supreme Court the proper remedy?
Did Congress have the constitutional authority to expand the Supreme Court’s
original jurisdiction through the Judiciary Act of 1789?
Holding
1. 2. 3. 4. Yes. Marbury had a vested legal right to his commission once it was signed and sealed.
Yes. The law generally provides a remedy for the violation of a vested legal right.
No. The Supreme Court lacked jurisdiction to issue the writ.
No. Section 13 of the Judiciary Act of 1789 was unconstitutional insofar as it expanded
the Court’s original jurisdiction beyond Article III.
Judgment - The Supreme Court denied the writ of mandamus for lack of jurisdiction.
Reasoning
1. Right to the Commission
Marshall held that once the President signs a commission and the Secretary of State affixes the
seal, the appointment is complete. Delivery is not required to vest the right. Withholding the
commission violated Marbury’s vested legal right.
2. Availability of a Remedy
Marshall emphasized that the United States is a
“
government of laws, and not of men.
”
Where a legal right is violated, the law must provide a remedy unless the action is purely
political. While some executive actions are discretionary and therefore nonjusticiable, othersinvolve specific legal duties owed to individuals. Delivering the commission was a ministerial
duty, not a discretionary political act.
3. Appropriateness of Mandamus
A writ of mandamus is appropriate where:
●
●
●
The petitioner has a clear legal right,
The respondent has a clear legal duty,
No other adequate legal remedy exists.
These elements were satisfied in theory.
4. Jurisdiction and Judicial Review- However, the Supreme Court’s authority to issue mandamus
in an original action depended on Section 13 of the Judiciary Act of 1789. Article III of the
Constitution exhaustively enumerates the Court’s original jurisdiction, and Congress may not
expand it. Because Section 13 conflicted with Article III, it was unconstitutional.
Crucially, Marshall declared that it is the province and duty of the judicial department to
say what the law is, establishing the principle of judicial review.
Rule of Law
●
●
●
●
A signed and sealed judicial commission creates a vested legal right.
Courts may review executive action when it violates a specific legal duty owed to an
individual.
Congress cannot expand the Supreme Court’s original jurisdiction beyond Article III.
Courts have the authority to declare acts of Congress unconstitutional.
Constitutional Significance
●
●
●
●
Establishes judicial review
Defines limits on congressional power
Clarifies the distinction between political/discretionary acts and ministerial/legal
duties
Strengthens judicial independence while avoiding direct confrontation with the
executiveJudicial review = authority to review laws and legislatoive acts to determine whether they pass
constitutional muster
The act was unconstitutional because it sought to expand SCOTUS original jurtisidction
betyobnd that of the constitution
IT IS EMPATHATICALLY THE PROVINCE AND DUTY OF THE JUDICIAL DEPARTMENT TO SAY
WHAT THE LAW IS.
Section 2: Supreme Court Authority to Review State Court Judgments
Martin v Hunter
Procedural History
A land dispute originating in Virginia state court was decided in favor of Hunter by the Virginia Court of Appeals. The U.S. Supreme Court reversed in Fairfax’s Devisee v. Hunter’s Lessee (1813), directing the Virginia court to enter judgment for Martin. The Virginia Court of Appeals refused to comply, holding that § 25 of the Judiciary Act of 1789, which authorized Supreme Court appellate review of state court decisions on federal questions, was unconstitutional. The case returned to the Supreme Court to resolve the scope of federal appellate power over state courts.
Facts
 The dispute concerned land once owned by Lord Fairfax. Virginia claimed it had lawfully seized Fairfax’s lands during the Revolution as property of a British loyalist and later granted the land to Hunter. Martin claimed title through a devise from Fairfax, arguing that title had not vested in Virginia before 1783 and that his ownership was protected by federal treaties. The Virginia Court of Appeals ruled for Hunter. The Supreme Court reversed, but the Virginia court refused to comply, asserting that federal appellate jurisdiction could not constitutionally extend to state courts.
Issues
1. Does Article III of the Constitution permit the Supreme Court to exercise appellate
jurisdiction over state court decisions involving federal law?
2. Is § 25 of the Judiciary Act of 1789 constitutional insofar as it authorizes Supreme Court
review of state court judgments on federal questions?
Holding- Yes. Article III authorizes Supreme Court appellate review of state court decisions
involving federal law.
Yes. Section 25 of the Judiciary Act of 1789 is constitutional.Judgment- The Supreme Court reversed the Virginia Court of Appeals and reaffirmed its
authority to review and bind state courts on questions of federal law.
Reasoning
1. Appellate Jurisdiction Attaches to the Case, Not the Court
Justice Story reasoned that Article III extends the judicial power of the United States to all cases
arising under the Constitution, laws, and treaties of the United States. The Constitution does
not limit appellate jurisdiction based on the tribunal in which the case arises. If a case falls
within federal judicial power, the Supreme Court must be able to review it, regardless of
whether it originates in a state or federal court.
2. Necessity of Appellate Review Over State Courts
Because state courts were expected to hear federal questions in the ordinary exercise of their
jurisdiction, federal judicial power could not be exercised exclusively through original
jurisdiction in federal courts. Where original jurisdiction properly lies in state courts, federal
judicial power must operate through appellate review, or else not at all.
3. Federal Review Does Not Violate State Sovereignty
The Court rejected the argument that Supreme Court review of state court decisions improperly
infringes state sovereignty. The Constitution explicitly limits state sovereignty in numerous
respects, and state courts are bound by the Constitution and federal law under the Supremacy
Clause. In matters within federal competence, state courts are not independent of the United
States.
4. Uniformity of Federal Law
Justice Story emphasized the necessity of national uniformity in interpreting federal law.
Without Supreme Court appellate review, federal statutes, treaties, and constitutional
provisions could receive divergent interpretations across the states, resulting in inconsistent
and fragmented federal law—an outcome the Constitution was designed to prevent.
5. Distrust of Structural Bias, Not Judicial Integrity
The Court acknowledged the learning and integrity of state judges but noted that structural
concerns—such as state interests, prejudices, and attachments—could influence
decision-making. The Constitution accounts for these risks by vesting final interpretive
authority in the federal judiciary.
Rule of Law
The Supreme Court has constitutional authority to review state court judgments involving
federal law, and Congress may authorize such review through legislation consistent with Article
III.
Constitutional Significance* Confirms federal judicial supremacy over state courts on federal questions
* Upholds the constitutionality of § 25 of the Judiciary Act of 1789
* Ensures uniform interpretation of federal law nationwide
* Complements Marbury v. Madison by extending judicial review vertically (state → federal)
Section 3. Judicial Exclusivity in Constitutional Interpretation
1. Marbury v. Madison (1803)
Facts: In the final days of his presidency, John Adams appointed several "midnight judges." William Marbury was appointed as a Justice of the Peace, but his commission was not delivered before Thomas Jefferson took office. Jefferson’s Secretary of State, James Madison, refused to deliver it. Marbury sued in the Supreme Court for a writ of mandamus.
Issue: (1) Does Marbury have a right to the commission? (2) Does the law afford him a remedy? (3) Does the Supreme Court have the authority to issue that remedy?
Rule: The Constitution is the supreme law of the land. Acts of Congress that conflict with the Constitution are void. The Judiciary has the power of Judicial Review.
Analysis: Marshall held that Marbury had a right to the commission. However, the Judiciary Act of 1789, which gave the Court original jurisdiction to issue writs of mandamus, was unconstitutional because Article III explicitly limits the Court’s original jurisdiction.
Conclusion: The Court cannot grant the writ. Established the principle that "it is emphatically the province and duty of the judicial department to say what the law is."
2. Martin v. Hunter’s Lessee (1816)
Facts: During the Revolutionary War, Virginia enacted laws allowing the state to seize property owned by British loyalists. Lord Fairfax, a loyalist, bequeathed his land to Martin. Meanwhile, Virginia granted a portion of that same land to Hunter. Martin claimed the land was protected by federal treaties with Britain. The Virginia Court of Appeals ruled for Hunter; the U.S. Supreme Court reversed. Virginia then refused to obey, claiming the Supreme Court had no authority over state court decisions.
Issue: Does the U.S. Supreme Court have the authority to review decisions of the highest state courts regarding federal law?
Rule: The Supremacy Clause and Article III grant the Supreme Court appellate jurisdiction over state court cases involving federal questions.
Analysis: Justice Story argued that the Constitution's "judicial Power" extends to all cases arising under federal law, not just those in federal courts. To ensure uniformity of federal law across the nation, one supreme body must have the final word.
Conclusion: Yes. The Supreme Court has the power to review state court rulings to maintain national legal uniformity.
3. Cohens v. Virginia (1821)
The Facts
The Cohen brothers were authorized by an Act of Congress to sell lottery tickets in the District of Columbia. They decided to sell these tickets in Virginia, which had a state law prohibiting the sale of out-of-state lottery tickets. Virginia authorities arrested and convicted the Cohens, fining them $100.
The Cohens appealed their conviction to the U.S. Supreme Court, arguing that because the lottery was authorized by federal law, the Virginia state law was unconstitutional under the Supremacy Clause. Virginia, however, argued that the Supreme Court had no jurisdiction to review a criminal conviction from a state court, claiming that the Eleventh Amendment barred such suits.
Issue
Does the Supreme Court of the United States have the constitutional authority to review a state court’s decision in a criminal proceeding where the defendant claims their federal rights were violated?
Rule
The Supreme Court’s appellate jurisdiction extends to all cases arising under the Constitution or laws of the United States, regardless of the parties involved or whether the case is civil or criminal in nature.
Application
Chief Justice John Marshall applied the following reasoning to bridge the gap between state sovereignty and federal supremacy:
Federal Question Jurisdiction: Marshall argued that Article III gives the federal judiciary power over all cases "arising under" federal law. Since the Cohens' defense relied on a federal statute (the D.C. Lottery Act), the case fell squarely within federal jurisdiction.
The Supremacy Clause: He noted that the Constitution and federal laws are the "supreme Law of the Land." If state courts could have the final word on federal law, the Constitution would be interpreted differently in every state, leading to a "hydra" of conflicting legal systems.
The 11th Amendment: Virginia argued the 11th Amendment prevented the suit. Marshall disagreed, stating that an appeal (a writ of error) is not a "suit" brought against a state by a citizen; rather, it is a continuation of a case the state itself started by prosecuting the defendants.
Final Outcome: While Marshall established that the Supreme Court had the power to hear the case, he ultimately ruled against the Cohens on the merits. He held that Congress didn't intend for the D.C. lottery tickets to be sold in states that prohibited them. Virginia won the battle (the conviction stood), but the Supreme Court won the war (establishing its power over state criminal rulings).
Conclusion
The Court held that it has the power to review state court criminal judgments. This decision solidified the Supreme Court as the final arbiter of federal law, ensuring that states cannot shield their criminal proceedings from federal constitutional scrutiny.
Summary Table for Quick Review
4. Cooper v. Aaron (1958)
The Facts
Following the landmark ruling in Brown v. Board of Education (1954), the school board of Little Rock, Arkansas, formulated a plan to desegregate Central High School. However, the Governor of Arkansas, Orval Faubus, and the state legislature openly resisted. Governor Faubus even called out the Arkansas National Guard to prevent nine African American students (the "Little Rock Nine") from entering the school.
The situation became so volatile that President Eisenhower had to send in the 101st Airborne Division to protect the students. Because of this "chaos, bedlam, and turmoil," the school board (led by William G. Cooper) asked a federal district court to postpone the desegregation plan for two and a half years, arguing that the public hostility made an integrated education impossible.
Issue
Are state government officials (like the Governor and Legislature of Arkansas) bound by the Supreme Court’s interpretation of the Constitution, even if they were not parties to the original case (Brown) and even if they believe the ruling causes civil unrest?
Rule
The Constitution is the supreme Law of the Land, and the federal judiciary is supreme in the exposition (interpretation) of the Law of the Constitution.
Application
The Court’s application of the law centered on three pillars of federal power:
Judicial Supremacy: The Court reaffirmed the principle from Marbury v. Madison that it is "emphatically the province and duty of the judicial department to say what the law is." By extension, the Court’s interpretation of the 14th Amendment in Brown became part of the "supreme Law of the Land."
The Supremacy Clause & Article VI Oaths: The Court pointed out that under Article VI, every state legislator and executive official takes a solemn oath to support the U.S. Constitution. Therefore, no state official can "war against the Constitution" by ignoring a Supreme Court mandate without violating that oath.
Constitutional Rights vs. Public Disorder: The Court held that the constitutional rights of African American children to an integrated education cannot be sacrificed to the violence and disorder created by the actions of state officials. If "law and order" could be maintained by depriving citizens of their rights, the Constitution would be a "solem mockery."
Conclusion
The Court denied the request for a delay. It held that the Little Rock School Board must proceed with integration immediately. The ruling established that a Supreme Court interpretation of the Constitution is just as binding on the states as the text of the Constitution itself.
Key Takeaways for Your Exam
5. Whole Woman’s Health v. Jackson (2021)
The Facts
In 2021, Texas passed S.B. 8, which prohibited abortions once a fetal heartbeat is detected (usually around six weeks). Traditionally, if a state passes an unconstitutional law, a plaintiff sues the state official responsible for enforcing it (like the Attorney General).
However, Texas drafted S.B. 8 to prohibit state officials from enforcing it. Instead, the law gave "any person" the right to bring a private civil lawsuit in state court against anyone who performs an abortion or "aids and abets" one. If successful, the private citizen would receive at least $10,000 in "bounty."
Whole Woman’s Health and other providers sued several defendants in federal court—including a state court judge, a court clerk, the Texas Attorney General, and a private individual—seeking an injunction to stop the law from being enforced. They argued the law was an unconstitutional violation of Roe v. Wade (which was still good law at the time of this specific procedural ruling).
Issue
Can federal courts issue an injunction against state court judges, clerks, or private citizens to prevent them from entertaining or filing lawsuits authorized by a state law that is allegedly unconstitutional?
Rule
Under the Eleventh Amendment and the doctrine of Ex parte Young, federal courts generally cannot enjoin state court judges or clerks from exercising their judicial duties, nor can they enjoin private citizens who have not yet filed a lawsuit.
Application
The Court’s majority (led by Justice Gorsuch) applied a strict procedural lens:
State Court Judges and Clerks: The Court held that judges and clerks are not "adverse" to the plaintiffs. A clerk simply files papers, and a judge simply adjudicates. 
Under Ex parte Young, you can't sue a judge to prevent them from hearing a case just because the law they might apply is unconstitutional.
The Attorney General: Because the Texas law specifically stripped the Attorney General of enforcement power, there was no "threatened enforcement" by him to enjoin.
The Private Citizen: The plaintiffs sued one private individual (Pennycuick) who they feared might sue them. The Court held there was no evidence he intended to sue, thus there was no "case or controversy" regarding him.
The Exception: The Court allowed the suit to proceed only against state licensing officials (like the Texas Medical Board), because they still had some residual power to strip licenses based on S.B. 8 violations.
Conclusion
The Court held that most of the defendants (judges, clerks, and the Attorney General) were immune from the suit. This allowed S.B. 8 to remain in effect despite the then-existing precedent of Roe v. Wade, because there was no proper state official for the federal courts to "block."
Notes on the Dissent
The dissents in this case were particularly sharp, focusing on the threat this "bounty hunter" scheme posed to the Supremacy Clause.
Chief Justice Roberts: He argued that the Court should not allow a state to "nullify" federal rights by simply shifting enforcement to the public. He believed the District Court should have been allowed to hear the case to protect the Supreme Court's role as the final arbiter of the Constitution.
Justice Sotomayor: She famously described the Texas law as a "bounty hunter scheme" and called the majority’s decision "stunning." She argued that by allowing Texas to evade federal review through procedural trickery, the Court was effectively abdicating its duty to protect constitutional rights.
Justice Kagan: She emphasized that S.B. 8 was a "clear-cut" attempt to circumvent Marbury v. Madison and Cooper v. Aaron. She argued that if this model were successful, any state could nullify any federal right (like the right to bear arms or free speech) by simply creating a private cause of action.
1L Study Tip: The "S.B. 8 Loophole"
Export to Sheets
6. Ex parte McCardle (1869)
During the Reconstruction Era, this case was a political powder keg that tested the limits of the Exceptions Clause.
The Facts
William McCardle was a newspaper editor in Vicksburg, Mississippi, who was arrested by federal military authorities under the Military Reconstruction Act. His crime? Publishing "incendiary and libellous" articles urging resistance to Reconstruction.
McCardle sought a writ of habeas corpus, arguing that the Reconstruction Acts were unconstitutional because they allowed military trials for civilians. He appealed his case to the Supreme Court under the Habeas Corpus Act of 1867, which specifically granted the Supreme Court appellate jurisdiction over such cases.
After the Supreme Court heard the oral arguments, but before it could issue a ruling, Congress—fearing the Court would strike down the entire Reconstruction program—passed a new law in 1868. This law explicitly repealed the part of the 1867 Act that gave the Supreme Court jurisdiction over McCardle's appeal.
Issue
Does Congress have the constitutional authority to withdraw the Supreme Court’s appellate jurisdiction over a specific class of cases, even after a case has already been argued and is pending a decision?
Rule
While the Supreme Court's appellate jurisdiction is derived from the Constitution, it is conferred "with such exceptions and under such regulations as Congress shall make."
Source: U.S. Const. art. III, § 2, cl. 2 (The Exceptions Clause); Ex parte McCardle, 74 U.S. 506 (1869).
Application
Chief Justice Salmon P. Chase, writing for a unanimous Court, applied a very literal reading of the Constitution:
The Plain Meaning of the Text: The Court looked at the "Exceptions Clause" and concluded that the power of Congress to make exceptions to the Court's appellate jurisdiction is "unrestricted." If the Constitution says Congress can make exceptions, the Court cannot question the motives of Congress for doing so.
The Repeal’s Effect: Once Congress repealed the Act of 1867, the legal foundation for McCardle’s appeal vanished. Without a statute granting jurisdiction, the Court had no power to proceed. As Chase famously put it: "Without jurisdiction the court cannot proceed at all in any cause."
The Safety Valve: Crucially, the Court noted that Congress had only repealed the appellate jurisdiction granted by the 1867 Act. It had not repealed the Court’s jurisdiction granted under other laws (like the Judiciary Act of 1789). Therefore, while McCardle lost this specific path to the Court, the Court’s overall power to hear habeas corpus cases remained intact.
Conclusion
The Court dismissed the case for want of jurisdiction. This established that Congress has broad authority to limit the Supreme Court’s appellate review, a power often referred to as "jurisdiction stripping."
Summary for Your Outline
Export to Sheets
Sources for Academic Reference:
Chemerinsky, E. (2019). Constitutional Law: Principles and Policies. Wolters Kluwer. (See Section 1.2: "The Scope of Congressional Power to Limit Federal Jurisdiction").
Van Alstyne, W. W. (1973). A Critical Guide to Ex Parte McCardle. 15 Arizona Law Review 229. (The seminal academic analysis of this case).
This case is often paired with United States v. Klein (1872), where the Court pushed back against Congress for trying to use jurisdiction stripping to dictate the outcome of a case.

Section 4. Constitutional and Prudential Limits on Constitutional Adjudication: The “Case or Controversy” Requirements
Standing
Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife
The Facts
The Endangered Species Act of 1973 (ESA) required federal agencies to consult with the Secretary of the Interior to ensure that their funded projects wouldn't jeopardize endangered species. Initially, this rule applied to projects worldwide. However, a new revised regulation limited the ESA’s scope to projects within the United States or on the high seas.
The Defenders of Wildlife (an environmental group) filed suit, seeking a declaratory judgment that the new regulation was illegal. They argued that US-funded projects in Egypt (the Aswan High Dam) and Sri Lanka (the Mahaweli project) would harm the Nile crocodile and the Asian elephant.
To establish standing, two members of the group submitted affidavits:
Ms. Kelly testified she had traveled to Egypt to observe the crocodile’s habitat and intended to do so again.
Ms. Skilbred testified she had traveled to Sri Lanka, saw the elephants' habitat, and intended to return "some day" to see them, though she had no certain plans or plane tickets.
Issue
Do the plaintiffs have "standing" to sue under Article III of the Constitution when they claim a "generalized grievance" regarding the environment without showing a specific, imminent, and concrete injury?
Rule
To satisfy the "Case or Controversy" requirement of Article III, a plaintiff must demonstrate a three-part test for Standing:
Injury in Fact: An invasion of a legally protected interest that is (a) concrete and particularized, and (b) actual or imminent, not "conjectural" or "hypothetical."
Causation: A causal connection between the injury and the conduct complained of.
Redressability: It must be "likely," as opposed to merely "speculative," that the injury will be redressed by a favorable decision.
Application
Justice Scalia, writing for the majority, dismantled the plaintiffs' standing using the three-part test:
Failure of "Imminence": The Court held that the "some day" intentions of the members to visit Egypt or Sri Lanka were insufficient. Without concrete plans or a specific date to return, the injury was not "imminent." Scalia famously noted that a "proffered intent to return to the relevant places" is not enough when there are no plane tickets or firm dates.
Failure of "Particularization": The Court rejected "ecosystem nexus" theories (the idea that any person who uses any part of an ecosystem is injured by a project elsewhere). The injury must affect the plaintiff in a personal and individual way.
Redressability Problems: The Court noted that even if they ordered the Secretary of the Interior to change the regulation, it wasn't clear that the funding agencies (like USAID) would follow it, or that withdrawing US funding would actually stop the foreign projects. Therefore, a court ruling might not actually save the crocodiles or elephants.
The "Citizen-Suit" Provision: Even though the ESA had a statute saying "any person" could sue, the Court held that Congress cannot bypass the Constitution. If a plaintiff doesn't have an Article III injury, a statute cannot magically give them standing to sue the government over a general desire to see the law enforced.
Conclusion
The Court held the plaintiffs lacked standing. The suit was dismissed. This case solidified the requirement that plaintiffs must have a "personal stake" in the outcome, preventing the judiciary from becoming a forum for general policy grievances.
The Lujan "Cheat Sheet" for Your Outline
Export to Sheets
Massachusetts v. Environmental Protection Agency
549 U.S. 497, 127 S. Ct. 1438, 167 L. Ed. 2d 248 (2007).
The Facts
Massachusetts, along with several other states and environmental groups, petitioned the Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) to regulate the emission of greenhouse gases (specifically from new motor vehicles) under the Clean Air Act. They argued that these emissions contribute to global warming, which causes rising sea levels that damage state-owned coastal land.
The EPA denied the petition, arguing two things:
The Clean Air Act did not authorize the EPA to regulate substances that contribute to climate change.
Even if it did, the EPA had the discretion to decline to regulate for policy reasons (like not wanting to interfere with the President's foreign policy negotiations).
The EPA also challenged the standing of Massachusetts. They argued that global warming is a "generalized grievance" felt by everyone on Earth, and that regulating U.S. car emissions would be such a small drop in the bucket that it wouldn't actually "redress" the injury of rising seas.
Issue
Does a state have standing to sue a federal agency over global environmental damage if that damage specifically affects the state's quasi-sovereign interests (like its coastline)?
Does the Clean Air Act give the EPA authority to regulate greenhouse gas emissions?
Rule
A litigant must demonstrate (1) Injury, (2) Causation, and (3) Redressability to have standing. However, when a State is the litigant, it is entitled to "special solicitude" in the standing analysis because of its duty to protect its sovereign territory.
Application
Justice Stevens, writing for the 5-4 majority, applied the Lujan factors but with a significant "State-sized" twist:
Injury in Fact: Unlike the plaintiffs in Lujan who just wanted to visit animals, Massachusetts was losing actual physical territory. The "rising seas" were already swallowing Massachusetts' coastal property. This was concrete and particularized.
Causation: The Court held that while U.S. motor vehicle emissions are only a portion of global emissions, they are a "significant" contributor. You don't have to be the only cause to be a cause for standing purposes.
Redressability: The EPA argued that regulating U.S. cars wouldn't stop global warming. The Court disagreed, stating that while a favorable ruling wouldn't reverse climate change, it could slow or reduce it. A "small step" toward a solution is enough to satisfy redressability.
Special Solicitude: The Court emphasized that Massachusetts is a sovereign state, not an individual. Because it surrendered certain powers to the federal government when it joined the Union (like the power to invade other countries or make treaties), it must be allowed to use the federal courts to protect its interests.
Conclusion
The Court held that Massachusetts had standing. On the merits, it ruled that greenhouse gases are "air pollutants" under the Clean Air Act and that the EPA must regulate them unless it provides a scientific basis for not doing so.
Comparison: Lujan vs. Massachusetts
Export to Sheets
Notes on the Dissents
Chief Justice Roberts: He wrote a scathing dissent arguing that the majority was turning the Supreme Court into a "lawyer for the environment." He argued that the injury (global warming) was too widespread and the remedy (EPA regulation) was too speculative to meet the Lujan test.
Justice Scalia: He dissented on the merits, arguing that "air pollutant" should only refer to things that dirty the air people breathe (like smog), not gases that naturally exist in the atmosphere like CO2.

Clapper v. Amnesty International USA (2013).
If Lujan was about the "where" (geographic proximity) and Massachusetts v. EPA was about the "who" (states vs. individuals), Clapper is about the "when" and the "how certain." This case sets an incredibly high bar for "future" injuries, requiring them to be "certainly impending" rather than just "reasonably likely."
Issue
Do plaintiffs have Article III standing to challenge a surveillance law if their claim of future injury relies on a multi-step chain of speculative events and if their present financial expenditures are based on a fear of that hypothetical future surveillance?
Rule
To establish standing for a future injury, the threatened injury must be "certainly impending"; allegations of a "possible future injury" are insufficient. Furthermore, plaintiffs cannot "manufacture standing" by incurring costs to avoid a speculative harm.
Application
Justice Alito’s majority opinion utilized a "Chain of Speculation" analysis to deny standing:
The Attenuated Chain: The Court identified five distinct links that would all have to happen for the plaintiffs to be harmed (Government must target their contacts, must use Section 702 specifically, the FISA court must approve it, the interception must succeed, and the plaintiffs must be on the other end of the call). The Court found this "highly speculative."
Strict "Imminence": The Court moved away from a "reasonable probability" standard, insisting instead on "certainly impending." Since the plaintiffs couldn't prove the government was actually listening or certainly would listen, they failed this prong.
Self-Inflicted Injury: The plaintiffs argued they were currently harmed because they had to spend money on secure travel and encrypted comms to avoid being overheard. The Court rejected this, holding that you can't spend your way into standing based on a fear of a harm the Court hasn't deemed "imminent.”
Conclusion
The Court held the plaintiffs lacked standing. The case was dismissed without the Court ever reaching the question of whether the surveillance itself was constitutional.
Addressing Your Specific Questions
1. Do the Snowden revelations vindicate Justice Breyer?
In a purely factual sense, yes. Justice Breyer’s dissent argued that surveillance was a "common-sense" probability. Shortly after Clapper, Edward Snowden revealed that the NSA was indeed engaging in bulk data collection (though often under different authorities like Section 215 of the PATRIOT Act).
Breyer’s View: The world is governed by probabilities. If the government has the motive, the money, and the legal green light, they will use it.
The Vindicated Reality: The revelations proved that the "speculative chain" Alito dismissed was, in many cases, a functioning reality. It turned "possible" harm into "demonstrated" harm.
2. Who would have standing now?
Post-Snowden, the landscape shifted. To get standing now, a plaintiff generally needs:
Direct Evidence of Targeting: In ACLU v. Clapper (2nd Cir. 2015), the court found standing because the government admitted it was collecting the metadata of the specific plaintiffs (bulk collection of all phone records).
A "Concrete" Link: You no longer need to speculate; if you can point to the leaked documents showing your specific organization or communication channel was swept up, the "certainly impending" bar is replaced by "actual past injury."
3. Why restrict standing to "certainly impending"?
Professor (now Judge) Frank Easterbrook’s quote touches on the Separation of Powers rationale. The Court restricts standing for two main reasons:
Preventing Advisory Opinions: If the Court ruled on every "probable" harm, it would become a council of revision, constantly editing laws before they even hurt anyone.
Judicial Economy: By requiring "certainly impending" injury, the Court ensures it only intervenes when a dispute is "ripe"—meaning the harm is real enough that the parties have a true incentive to litigate vigorously.
Summary Table: The Clapper Standard
FEC v. Akins (1998)
The Facts
A group of voters (Akins and others) wanted the Federal Election Commission (FEC) to classify the American Israel Public Affairs Committee (AIPAC) as a "political committee." If classified as such, AIPAC would be legally required to disclose its membership, contributions, and expenditures. The voters argued that without this information, they couldn't effectively evaluate candidates for office. The FEC refused. The voters sued, but the FEC argued they had no standing because their "injury" (not knowing who funds a group) was a "generalized grievance" shared by every voter in America.
IRAC Analysis
Issue: Does a "widely shared" harm (like the lack of information for voters) constitute a "concrete injury" sufficient for Article III standing if Congress has created a statutory right to that information?
Rule: A "generalized grievance" does not bar standing if the harm is concrete, even if it is widely shared. An informational injury (the failure to obtain information that a statute requires to be public) is sufficiently concrete.
Application: Justice Breyer distinguished between "abstract" harms and "widely shared" harms.
An abstract harm (like a general desire for the government to follow the law) is not enough.
However, the inability to get information that would help a voter cast an informed ballot is a concrete disadvantage. Because the Federal Election Campaign Act (FECA) specifically gave citizens a right to this information, the deprivation of that right is a real injury.
Conclusion: The voters had standing. The "widely shared" nature of the harm didn't make it less "concrete."
Case 2: Vermont Agency of Nat. Res. v. Stevens (2000)
The Facts
This case involves a qui tam suit under the False Claims Act (FCA). A "relator" (a private whistleblower) sued the State of Vermont, alleging it had submitted false claims for federal grant money. In a qui tam action, the private citizen sues on behalf of the government and gets to keep a percentage of the recovered funds (a "bounty"). The question was: how can a private citizen have standing when it was the government that was cheated out of money, not the citizen?
IRAC Analysis
Issue: Does a private "relator" have standing to sue for an injury committed against the United States government?
Rule: A private plaintiff has standing if the government has legally assigned its claim to that plaintiff. The assignee "stands in the shoes" of the injured party (the government).
Application: Justice Scalia (writing for the Court) held that a mere "interest in the bounty" (like a bet on a horse race) is not enough for standing. An interest that is a "byproduct" of the suit cannot create an injury. However, the False Claims Act acts as a partial assignment of the government’s claim. Because the government was actually injured (loss of money), and because the law "assigns" part of that claim to the relator, the relator inherits the government’s Article III standing.
Conclusion: The relator had standing. Congress can "transfer" the government's injury to a private individual to sue on the government's behalf.

1. Threshold Justiciability Challenges
Before the court looks at the merits of the Voter Equality Act, it must determine if a "Case or Controversy" exists under Article III.
Standing (Injury-in-Fact): The Plaintiff must show a "concrete and particularized" injury. Under Rucho v. Common Cause, the Supreme Court noted that partisan gerrymandering claims are often generalized grievances. If the Plaintiff cannot prove their specific district was diluted or affected, they lack standing.
Ripeness: The prompt states the claim was filed "as soon as the President signs the Act." If the State has not yet drawn a new map or applied the Act’s criteria to an existing map, the injury is speculative. The case is likely "unripe" for judicial review.
Political Question Doctrine: You should argue that determining what constitutes "partisan fairness" is a non-justiciable political question. As the Court held in Rucho, there are no "judicially manageable standards" for deciding when partisan advantage goes too far.
2. Lack of Subject Matter Jurisdiction
Sovereign Immunity (11th Amendment): The Act authorizes a voter to sue the State. However, the 11th Amendment generally bars private citizens from suing states in federal court. Unless Congress validly abrogated state immunity (which requires a showing of a pattern of constitutional violations under Section 5 of the 14th Amendment), the suit is barred.
3. Constitutional Validity of the Act
This is where you show off your understanding of Federalism and the limits of Congressional power.
A. The Elections Clause (Federal vs. State Seats)
Congress has broad power under the Elections Clause (Art. I, §4) to regulate the "Times, Places and Manner" of Federal elections. However, the Act also prohibits gerrymandering for State legislative seats.
The Argument: Congress lacks the enumerated power to dictate how a state draws its own internal legislative districts. This interferes with state sovereignty and violates the 10th Amendment.
B. The "Intent" vs. "Effect" Standard
The Act creates a "5% discrepancy" rule (Section B).
The Argument: This essentially mandates Proportional Representation. The Supreme Court has never held that the Constitution requires proportional outcomes. By forcing states to hit a specific mathematical percentage, Congress may be overstepping its authority to enforce the 14th Amendment, as this rule may not be "congruent and proportional" to any recognized constitutional violation.

WEEK 3 
Political Questions
Baker v. Carr, 369 U.S. 186 (1962)
The Facts
For over 60 years, the state of Tennessee had failed to redraw its state legislative districts. Since the last apportionment in 1901, the state's population had shifted drastically from rural areas to cities.
Charles Baker, a resident of an urban county (Shelby County), sued Joe Carr, the Secretary of State of Tennessee. Baker argued that because his district had grown so much larger in population than rural districts, his vote was "diluted." Specifically, a vote in a small rural county carried much more weight than his vote in the city. He claimed this violated the Equal Protection Clause of the 14th Amendment.
The lower courts dismissed the case, relying on older Supreme Court precedents (like Colegrove v. Green) which held that "districting" was a "political thicket" that courts should not enter.
The Dispute
Baker (Plaintiff): Argued that this wasn't a "political" issue, but a constitutional one. His individual right to an equal vote was being denied by the state's inaction.
Carr (Defendant/State): Argued that the composition of legislative districts is a "political question" for the legislature to decide, not the judiciary. They argued the courts lacked the authority to tell a state how to draw its map.
Issue
Do federal courts have the jurisdiction (legal authority) to hear a constitutional challenge to a state’s legislative apportionment, or is this a "non-justiciable" political question?
Rule
The Court established the six-factor test to determine if a case is a "Political Question" (and therefore outside the court's power):
A textually demonstrable constitutional commitment of the issue to a coordinate political department (e.g., the Constitution says only Congress decides this).
A lack of judicially discoverable and manageable standards for resolving it.
The impossibility of deciding without an initial policy determination of a kind clearly for nonjudicial discretion.
The impossibility of a court's undertaking independent resolution without expressing lack of the respect due coordinate branches of government.
An unusual need for unquestioning adherence to a political decision already made.
The potentiality of embarrassment from multifarious pronouncements by various departments on one question.
Application
Justice Brennan, writing for the Court, famously "opened the courthouse doors":
Not a Political Question: The Court found that "apportionment" didn't meet the criteria for a political question. There was no "textual commitment" in the Constitution giving only the legislature the right to ignore population shifts.
Judicial Standards: Under the Equal Protection Clause, the Court could develop standards to see if a citizen's rights were being harmed.
Distinction from the Guarantee Clause: The Court noted that if Baker had sued under the "Guarantee Clause" (Art. IV, § 4, guaranteeing a republican form of government), it would have been a political question. However, because he sued under the 14th Amendment (Equal Protection), it was a legal claim the courts could settle.
Conclusion
Yes. The Supreme Court held that the case was justiciable (capable of being decided by a court). They sent the case back to the lower court to be tried on the merits. This decision essentially said: "Courts have the power to fix broken voting maps."
1. Congressional Qualifications: Powell v. McCormack (1969)
The Core Concept: This case clarifies the difference between interpreting the Constitution and exercising a power granted by it.
The Textual Commitment: The House argued that Art. I, § 5 gave them the "sole" power to judge qualifications. The Court disagreed, holding that this power is limited to the specific qualifications listed in the Constitution (age, citizenship, residence).
The Legal vs. Political Split: * Legal Question (Justiciable): What are the qualifications? (The Court can answer this).
Political Question (Non-justiciable): Does a specific person meet those qualifications? (The House decides this).
Key Takeaway: The Court will not allow a branch of government to "expand" its own power by re-interpreting the Constitution. If the House tries to add a new qualification (like "honesty" or "clean financial records"), the Court can step in because interpreting the text is a judicial function.
2. Treaty Abrogation: Goldwater v. Carter (1979)
The Core Concept: When the Constitution is silent on a process, the Court is often hesitant to step into a fight between the President and Congress, especially in foreign affairs.
The Dispute: President Carter ended a treaty with Taiwan without Senate approval. The Constitution says how to make a treaty (Senate must approve), but doesn't say how to end one.
The Plurality View (Rehnquist): This is a political question. Why? Because it’s a "power struggle" between two co-equal branches regarding foreign policy, and both branches have their own political "tools" to fight it out (e.g., Congress can withhold funding).
The Dissenting/Concurring View (Powell/Brennan): They argued it is justiciable. Just because a question is difficult or involves foreign policy doesn't mean the Court should hide. They believed the Court’s job is to say what the law is when the text is silent.
Key Takeaway: Goldwater shows that "Foreign Policy" + "Constitutional Silence" usually equals a Political Question.
3. Impeachment Proceedings: Nixon v. United States (1993)
(Note: Your prompt asks the question about the Senate committee; the answer is found in the 1993 case involving Judge Walter Nixon.)
The Core Concept: The word "sole" in the Constitution is a very strong signal of a "textually demonstrable commitment."
The Facts: Judge Walter Nixon was impeached. The Senate used a small committee to hear the evidence instead of the full Senate. Nixon argued this violated the requirement that the Senate "try" (like a trial) the impeachment.
The Rule: The Court held this was a non-justiciable political question.
The Reasoning: 
1.  Textual Commitment: Art. I, § 3, cl. 6 says the Senate has the "sole" power to try impeachments. "Sole" means "exclusive"—the courts have no seat at that table. 
2.  Lack of Standards: The word "try" is too vague. There are no "judicially manageable standards" for a court to determine what a "trial" looks like in a legislative body.
Key Takeaway: Impeachment is the ultimate political check. If the Supreme Court could review impeachment trials, it would destroy the "check" that Congress has over the Judiciary.
Bush v. Gore, 531 U.S. 98 (2000)
The Facts
The 2000 Presidential Election between George W. Bush and Al Gore came down to the state of Florida. The initial count showed Bush leading by a very small margin. Gore sought a manual recount in several counties. After weeks of legal battles, the Florida Supreme Court ordered a statewide manual recount of all "undervotes" (ballots where machines failed to detect a vote).
The Florida court commanded that the standard for the recount be the "intent of the voter." however, the court did not provide specific, uniform sub-standards for how to determine that intent (e.g., what to do with "hanging chads" vs. "dimpled chads"). Bush appealed to the U.S. Supreme Court, arguing that the recount was being handled inconsistently across different counties, violating the 14th Amendment.
The Dispute
Bush (Petitioner): Argued that the lack of uniform standards for the recount meant that a ballot counted in one county might be rejected in another, violating the Equal Protection Clause.
Gore (Respondent): Argued that the "intent of the voter" was a sufficient standard and that the state's highest court had the final word on interpreting Florida's election laws. He argued that stopping the recount disenfranchised voters whose ballots hadn't been counted.
Issue
Does the use of varying standardless manual recounts violate the Equal Protection and Due Process Clauses of the Fourteenth Amendment, and if so, is there a remedy available before the "safe harbor" deadline?
Rule
Equal Protection Clause (14th Amendment): While a citizen has no initial federal constitutional right to vote for President (the State Legislature chooses the method), once a state grants the right to vote, it may not value one person’s vote over another through arbitrary and disparate treatment.
3 U.S.C. § 5 ("Safe Harbor"): Federal law provides that if a state settles election disputes by a specific deadline (December 12 in 2000), that state's electors are "conclusive" and must be counted by Congress.
Application
The Court (per curiam) found the recount unconstitutional:
Non-Uniform Standards: The Court observed that "intent of the voter" was applied differently by various recount teams. Some teams counted "dimpled chads," others did not. This "standardless" process meant that similar ballots were being treated differently, which is the definition of arbitrary treatment.
The Remedy & The Clock: The Court determined that a constitutional recount would require uniform standards. However, the Court interpreted Florida law as being committed to the December 12 "safe harbor" deadline. Since that deadline was the very day the opinion was released, the Court concluded there was no time left to conduct a proper, constitutional recount.
Conclusion
Yes. The Supreme Court reversed the Florida Supreme Court’s recount order. By a 7-2 vote, the Court found an Equal Protection violation; by a 5-4 vote, the Court determined that no remedy could be fashioned in time, effectively ending the recount and resulting in George W. Bush winning the presidency.
Key Dissents (The "1L Gold Mine")
Justice Stevens: Argued that the "intent of the voter" was a traditional and sufficient legal standard. He famously warned that the decision would damage the nation's confidence in the Court's impartiality.
Justice Souter: Agreed there was an Equal Protection issue but wanted to remand (send it back) to Florida to let them try to fix the standards and finish the count by the Dec. 18 Electoral College meeting.
Justice Breyer: Invoked the Political Question Doctrine. He argued the Court should never have taken the case; the Constitution (and the Electoral Count Act) leaves it to Congress, not the Court, to resolve disputed presidential elections.
Note: Pay close attention to the Court's "disclaimer"—they stated their holding was "limited to the present circumstances." In your exams, this is a signal that the Court was wary of creating a broad precedent for federal courts to take over every local election dispute.
1. The Bush v. Gore Debate: Judicial Necessity vs. Political Restraint
The Core Concept: Was the Court "saving the country" from a constitutional crisis, or were they "invading" a process the Constitution reserved for Congress?
The Textual Argument: Article II and the 12th Amendment, combined with the Electoral Count Act (3 U.S.C. § 5), suggest that Congress—not the Court—is the final arbiter of disputed electoral votes.
Baker v. Carr vs. Bush v. Gore:
Baker: The Court intervened to clear the channels of democracy (allowing people to vote fairly in the future).
Bush: The Court intervened to determine the outcome of an election already held.
The "Political Thicket" Critique (Issacharoff): The federal government had already learned the lesson of the 1876 election and created a "political" solution in the Electoral Count Act. By stepping in, the Court ignored a "considered judgment of Congress" that these disputes have an "inevitable political cast."
Minority vs. Majority (Ely Theory): John Hart Ely argued courts should protect "discrete and insular minorities." In Bush, who was the minority? One could argue the "voters whose ballots weren't counted" were the minority, but the Court's intervention ultimately protected the "majority preference" as certified by the state.
2. Recognition of Foreign Sovereigns: Zivotofsky v. Clinton (2012)
The Core Concept: Does a statute about what goes on a passport infringe on the President’s exclusive power to "recognize" foreign nations?
The Holding: The Court found the case justiciable. They distinguished between making foreign policy (Political Question) and interpreting a statute (Legal Question).
Justiciability vs. Merits: The Court said: "We can decide if this law is constitutional (Justiciable), but we aren't deciding right now if the President is right or Congress is right (Merits)."
Breyer’s Dissent (The Prudential View): Justice Breyer argued the Court should use "self-restraint" because:
It’s foreign affairs.
The Court isn't equipped to handle Middle East political blowback.
The interest (a word on a passport) is "ideological" rather than a "property or bodily integrity" interest.
Key Takeaway: If a case requires the Court to simply interpret a statute or the Constitution, they will usually find it justiciable, even if it touches on sensitive foreign policy.
3. Partisan Gerrymandering: Rucho v. Common Cause (2019)
The Core Concept: When is a district map "too political"? The Court’s answer: "We don't know, so we can't touch it."
The Holding: Partisan gerrymandering is a non-justiciable political question.
The "Manageable Standards" Problem: Under the Baker v. Carr factors, a court must have a clear "legal yardstick" to measure a violation. Chief Justice Roberts argued there is no standard for "fairness" in districting.
Is fairness "proportional representation"? (Not required by the Constitution).
Is fairness "compact districts"? (A political choice).
The Conclusion: Because any line the Court drew would be "indeterminate and arbitrary," the issue is left to state legislatures and Congress. Federal courts are closed to these claims.
Comparison Table for Your Outline
Rucho v. Common Cause, 588 U.S. 684 (2019)
The Facts
The case involved two separate challenges to redistricting maps:
North Carolina: A Republican-led legislature drew a map to ensure a 10-3 Republican advantage in a state that was otherwise closely divided. One legislator famously stated he drew the map to give Republicans 10 seats because he "did not believe it was possible to draw a map with 11 Republicans."
Maryland: A Democratic-led legislature redrew a district to flip it from reliably Republican to reliably Democratic.
In both instances, the "out" party sued, arguing that the maps were so skewed that they violated the Equal Protection Clause, the First Amendment (retaliation based on political views), and the Elections Clause. The lower courts had found these "extreme" partisan gerrymanders unconstitutional.
The Dispute
The Plaintiffs (Common Cause, et al.): Argued that while some partisanship is expected, "extreme" gerrymandering effectively disenfranchises voters and prevents the "channels of political change" from working. They proposed various mathematical tests (like the "Efficiency Gap") to measure when a map becomes unconstitutionally unfair.
The Defendants (Rucho/State Officials): Argued that the Constitution gives the power to draw districts to state legislatures and Congress. They contended there is no "standard" in the Constitution for what a "fair" map looks like, making it a political question.
Issue
Do federal courts have the jurisdiction to decide whether a legislative district map is an unconstitutional "partisan gerrymander," or is this a non-justiciable political question?
Rule
Based on Baker v. Carr, an issue is a non-justiciable Political Question if there is a "lack of judicially discoverable and manageable standards for resolving it."
Application
Chief Justice Roberts, writing for the 5-4 majority, applied the Baker factors:
Historical Acceptance: The Court noted that the Framers were aware of gerrymandering and chose to vest the power of districting in the political branches (Art. I, § 4).
No Standard for "Fairness": This was the "death blow" for the claim. The Court argued that "fairness" is a political concept, not a legal one.
Does fairness mean proportional representation? (The Constitution doesn't require this).
Does it mean following county lines?
The Court found that no matter which "math test" was used, the judges would essentially be making policy choices about how much partisanship is "too much."
Institutional Competence: The Court feared that if they entered this arena, they would become the "ultimate arbiters" of every election map in the country, which would undermine the Court’s appearance of independence.
Conclusion
No. The Supreme Court held that partisan gerrymandering claims present non-justiciable political questions. While the Court admitted that such gerrymanders might be "incompatible with democratic principles," the remedy must come from state legislatures, state courts (using state constitutions), or Congress—not federal courts.
