CHAPTER 2
Federalism: History and Principles
I. Failures of the Articles of Confederation
Economic Barriers: State trade barriers were mutually hostile, preventing the formation of a single national market.
Military Weakness: National and state militias were too weak to defend against external attacks or internal insurrections.
Fiscal Dependency: The national government depended on state funds; states held the sole power to tax and often failed to pay national war debts.
Treaty Enforcement: States failed to enforce national treaty obligations.
Limited Central Authority: Article II stated that each State retained every power not "expressly" delegated to the United States.
II. Drafting the New Federal Structure
Shift to Enumerated Powers: The Framers moved from a general grant of legislative power (the Virginia Plan) to a specific list of enumerated powers in Article I, § 8.
Key New Powers: The Constitution granted the national government the crucial powers to levy taxes and regulate interstate/foreign commerce.
Necessary and Proper Clause: Article I, § 8, cl. 18 authorized Congress to make all laws "necessary and proper" for executing its enumerated powers.
The Tenth Amendment: This was added to clarify that powers not delegated to the U.S. are reserved to the States or the people, notably omitting the word "expressly" found in the Articles.
State Restrictions: Article I, § 10 barred states from specific acts like entering treaties or coining money.
III. The Conflict Over the First Bank of the U.S. (1790)
Hamilton’s Proposal: Secretary Alexander Hamilton urged the creation of a national bank to increase productive capital and facilitate tax payments.
Madison’s Opposition: James Madison argued the bank was unconstitutional because the power to incorporate was not an enumerated power and was too important to be "implied".
Jefferson’s Strict Construction: Thomas Jefferson argued that "necessary" meant "absolutely necessary"—those means without which a grant of power would be useless.
Hamilton’s Broad Construction: Hamilton argued "necessary" meant "needful, requisite, or conducive to," asserting that the Clause gave explicit sanction to implied powers.
Constitutional Criterion: Hamilton proposed that if the "end" is within the national authority and the "means" has an obvious relation to it, the act is constitutional.
IV. The Second Bank and McCulloch v. Maryland
Creation of the Second Bank: Despite original opposition, President Madison supported the 1816 charter of the Second Bank due to fiscal difficulties following the War of 1812.
"Liquidation" of Meaning: Madison believed that repeated recognitions of a power by all branches of government "liquidated" or settled its constitutional validity.
Economic Boom and Bust: A post-war boom led to loose management of the Bank, followed by a financial panic in 1818 where the Bank called in excessive loans.
State Resistance: Several states, including Maryland, reacted to the "monied power" by imposing heavy taxes on "foreign" (national) bank operations.
Corruption at the Baltimore Branch: The cashier, James McCulloch, was suspected of looting the bank, setting the stage for the landmark Supreme Court case.
McCulloch v. Maryland
4 Wheat. (17 U.S.) 316, 4 L. Ed. 579 (1819).
Facts
In 1816, Congress established the Second Bank of the United States to address national fiscal difficulties following the War of 1812. The Bank was highly controversial, particularly after a financial panic in 1818 caused it to call in excessive loans, sparking anger from state banks and individuals. Several states, including Maryland, Tennessee, and Georgia, imposed heavy taxes on "foreign" bank operations (banks not chartered by the state) to retaliate against the national bank.
In 1818, the Maryland legislature passed an act imposing a tax on all banks or branches in the state not chartered by the Maryland legislature. James McCulloch, the cashier of the Baltimore branch of the Second Bank of the United States, refused to pay the tax. Maryland sued McCulloch to recover the tax penalties, and the state courts upheld the tax.
Issue 1 Does Congress have the power to incorporate a national bank, even though that power is not explicitly listed in the Constitution?
Issue 2 Does a state have the power to tax an institution (like the Bank) that was created by the federal government under its constitutional authority?
Rule
Implied Powers: Under the Necessary and Proper Clause (Article I, § 8, cl. 18), Congress has the power to use all means "needful, requisite, incidental, useful or conducive to" the execution of its enumerated powers.
Supremacy Clause: The Constitution and the laws made in pursuance thereof are the supreme law of the land (Article VI).
Power to Tax: The power to tax involves the power to destroy.
Application
On Congressional Power: Chief Justice Marshall rejected the "strict constructionist" view that "necessary" means "absolutely indispensable". He argued that a Constitution is meant to endure for ages and must be interpreted broadly to meet various crises. If the end is legitimate and within the scope of the Constitution, all means which are appropriate and plainly adapted to that end are constitutional. Since a bank helps Congress manage taxes, borrow money, and regulate commerce, it is a valid exercise of implied power.
On State Power to Tax: Marshall argued that the federal government's powers are granted by the people, not the states; therefore, the federal government is supreme. If a state could tax a federal institution, it could effectively tax it out of existence, allowing a state to "destroy" what the national government has the right to "create". This would make the states supreme over the national government, which violates the Supremacy Clause.
Conclusion The Supreme Court ruled that (1) Congress did have the power to incorporate the Bank, and (2) Maryland could not constitutionally tax the Bank.
I. Aftermath of the McCulloch Decision
Post-Decision Recovery: In the decade following the ruling, the Bank of the United States recovered from the depression and was managed more competently.
The Jackson Veto: The Bank faced a fatal attack during the Jackson Administration; President Jackson vetoed its recharter in 1832, and the Bank ceased to exist in 1836.
James McCulloch's Fate: The Baltimore branch cashier was removed from office for misconduct one month after the Court's decision; subsequent criminal proceedings by Maryland failed because the state lacked an embezzlement statute.
The Newspaper Debates: A fierce ideological debate occurred in newspapers between Judge Spencer Roane (writing as "Hamden") and John Marshall (writing as "A Friend of the Constitution").
Sovereignty Clarified: In his essays, Marshall argued the Constitution was the act of the people of the states in their sovereign capacity, not an "alliance or league" between independent state governments.
II. Analysis of the Two McCulloch Holdings
1. The First Holding: Implied Powers
Constitutional Interpretation: Marshall emphasized that "it is a constitution we are expounding," intended to endure and be adapted to various human crises.
Deference to Congress: The Court held that Congress's judgment deserves deference if the means adopted are "appropriate" and "plainly adapted" to legitimate ends.
Judicial Limits: Marshall denied Congress had "unlimited power" and stated the Court would invalidate laws enacted under a "pretext" of exercising granted powers (e.g., altering inheritance laws under the guise of tax collection).
Historical Expansion: Federal power expanded significantly after the Civil War and the New Deal; from 1937 to 1995, many viewed federal legislative authority as almost unlimited.
2. The Second Holding: Immunity from State Taxation
Structural Reasoning: Marshall's source for this holding was an "inference from the structures and relationships" created by the Constitution, rather than specific text.
National Supremacy: The holding relies on the idea that the power to tax involves the power to destroy; states cannot destroy what the national government has a right to create.
Representation-Reinforcement: Marshall argued that judicial intervention is justified because when a state taxes a national institution, most of the nation’s voters lack representation in that state's legislature to stop the "oppressive" tax.
Security Against Abuse: In the national legislature, everyone is represented, providing a "sufficient security" against federal oppression of the states that does not exist in reverse.
III. Modern Limits of the Necessary and Proper Clause
Recent Interpretations: The Supreme Court continues to hold that "necessary" does not mean "absolutely necessary" (Ayestas v. Davis, 2018).
United States v. Comstock (2010): The Court ruled (7–2) that the Clause allows the federal government to order the civil commitment of sexually dangerous prisoners beyond their release dates.
NFIB v. Sebelius (2012): A 5–4 majority found the Necessary and Proper Clause was an insufficient basis to sustain the individual mandate of the Affordable Care Act (ACA) if it exceeded Commerce Clause bounds.
Federalism Limits: While Marshall's broad interpretation remains, modern cases suggest the Clause cannot be used to sustain regulations that the Court deems a violation of federalism-based limits on other powers.
Depagent no use fund to assit funds for immigration law. 
United States v. Comstock
560 U.S. 126, 130 S. Ct. 1949, 176 L. Ed. 2d 878 (2010).
Facts
The case centered on 18 U.S.C. § 4248, a federal statute that authorized the Department of Justice to move for the civil commitment of "sexually dangerous" federal prisoners. Under this law, the federal government could keep these individuals in custody even after they had completed their criminal sentences if a district court found they were mentally ill and likely to engage in "sexually violent conduct" if released.
The respondents (Comstock and others) were federal prisoners nearing the end of their sentences for various sex crimes. They challenged the constitutionality of the statute, arguing that the power to civilly commit individuals—traditionally a state "police power"—was not among the enumerated powers granted to Congress in the Constitution.
Issue Does the Necessary and Proper Clause grant Congress the authority to enact a statute allowing for the civil commitment of federal prisoners beyond the dates they would otherwise be released from criminal custody?
Rule The Necessary and Proper Clause (Article I, § 8, cl. 18) grants Congress broad authority to enact laws that are "convenient, or useful" or "conducive" to the exercise of an enumerated power. This authority is not limited to a single step but can involve a "long chain" of reasoning as long as each link is tethered to a constitutional grant of power.
Application Writing for the 7–2 majority, Justice Breyer identified five factors that, taken together, justified the statute:
The Breadth of the Clause: The Clause has historically been interpreted broadly (citing McCulloch).
Long-Standing History: The federal government has a long history of involvement in federal prison systems and the mental health of prisoners.
Reasonable Extension: It is "reasonable" for Congress to protect the public from the dangers posed by federal prisoners who are already in federal custody.
Accommodation of State Interests: The statute provided that a prisoner must be turned over to the state if the state chooses to assert its own civil commitment authority.
Narrow Scope: The link between the statute and the enumerated powers (such as the power to create a federal criminal code) was not too "attenuated" or "remote".
Conclusion The Supreme Court ruled that the statute was constitutional. The Necessary and Proper Clause provided sufficient authority for Congress to manage the safety of the federal prison system and its impact on the public.
Concurring Opinions
Justice Kennedy
The "Rational Basis" Warning: Kennedy cautioned against a standard that is too deferential to Congress.
Means-Ends Relationship: He argued the Court must ensure the "means" are not just related to the "end," but that the connection is tangible and specific to avoid an unlimited federal police power.
Focus on Prisoners: He emphasized that since the individuals were already in lawful federal custody, the government had a unique responsibility and authority to manage their release safely.
Justice Alito
Strict Tethering: Alito argued that the power to civilly commit must be directly tethered to the same enumerated power that allowed Congress to imprison the person in the first place (e.g., the power to regulate interstate commerce or protect federal property).
Limited Scope: He supported the result but wanted to ensure the decision didn't give Congress a general power to commit any dangerous person, only those already in the federal system.
Dissenting Opinion (Justice Thomas, joined by Justice Scalia)
The "Enumerated Powers" Barrier: Thomas argued that the power to civilly commit is a "Police Power" reserved strictly to the States by the Tenth Amendment.
The Two-Step Problem: He criticized the majority for using a "double-step" logic:
The power to criminalize an act (Step 1).
The power to imprison for that act (Step 2).
The power to continue imprisonment for mental health (The "unconstitutional" Step 3).
Missing Link: Thomas argued there is no specific enumerated power in Article I, § 8 that authorizes "civil commitment," and the Necessary and Proper Clause cannot be used to create a power that doesn't exist.
Anti-Expansionism: He feared the majority's "multi-factor" test was too vague and would allow Congress to justify almost any law by finding a "remote" connection to an enumerated power.
Summary Table: The Ideological Split
Section 2. The Limits of the Necessary and Proper Clause
1. Determining the Proper Standard of Review
Rational Basis vs. Five-Factor Test: In Comstock, the Court stated a statute must be "rationally related" to an enumerated power, yet applied a specific five-factor test to find the law "necessary and proper".
Kennedy’s Stricter Review: Justice Kennedy’s concurrence argued for a more "exacting" rational-basis review, similar to Commerce Clause scrutiny, grounded in federalism principles to ensure the federal government does not become a general police force.
Thomas’s Prerequisite: In his dissent, Justice Thomas argued that the Court must first identify a specific enumerated power; if none exists, the Necessary and Proper Clause cannot be invoked.
2. “Proper Federal Action” (NFIB v. Sebelius)
The "Proper" Requirement: Chief Justice Roberts analyzed "proper" as a distinct prong, holding that laws must be consistent with the "letter and spirit" of the Constitution to be valid.
Necessary Predicate vs. Derivative Power: Roberts ruled the individual mandate was not proper because it gave Congress the "extraordinary ability to create the necessary predicate" for exercising power (compelling activity) rather than just being a derivative of an existing power.
Ginsburg’s Dissent: Justice Ginsburg argued the mandate was an "essential part of a larger regulation of economic activity" (insurance reform) and that without it, the regulatory scheme would fail (the "death-spiral" argument).
Interpretive Shift: Roberts's opinion is viewed as a departure from the broad constructionism of McCulloch, moving toward a structuralist or narrow constructionist view that limits the "proper" use of federal power.
3. United States v. Kebodeaux (2013)
The Military Regulation Power: The Court (7-2) upheld a federal requirement for a convicted Air Force member to register as a sex offender under SORNA.
Breyer’s Broad View: Justice Breyer argued the Necessary and Proper Clause allows Congress to ensure public safety through systems like registration as a consequence of military crimes.
Roberts’s Concurrence: He warned that "public safety" is not a valid basis for federal power; there is no federal police power. The registration is only valid if it gives force to the Uniform Code of Military Justice.
Thomas’s Dissent: Argued SORNA aims at protecting society rather than "carrying into execution" an enumerated power, failing the McCulloch test.
4. Institutional Role (John Manning)
Judicial Deference: Manning argues the Court should not substitute its judgment for Congress’s on what is "necessary and proper," as the text delegates this discretion to the legislature.
Structural Concerns: This view rejects the Roberts Court’s use of "proper" to enforce the Court's own conceptions of federalism.
Section 3. The Location of Sovereignty in the Federal System
1. McCulloch and the Location of Sovereignty
The Three Frameworks:
National Populace: "We the People" as a single mass (Nationalist view).
Compact of States: "We the States" as independent sovereigns (Compact theory).
Peoples of the States: "We the Peoples" of several states acting through conventions (Intermediate view).
Marshall’s Middle Ground: Marshall rejected the "one common mass" idea but insisted the Constitution is not a compact or alliance between independent sovereigns.
Madison’s Federalist No. 39: He described the act of establishing the Constitution as "federal," not "national," because it derived from the supreme authority of the people in each distinct State.
2. The “Compact Theory” of Sovereignty
Definition: The theory that national power emanates from a compact among the states, rather than directly from the people.
Virginia and Kentucky Resolutions (1798): Madison and Jefferson argued states have the right to interpose or arrest the progress of federal "evils" if they exceed the compact’s grants.
Nullification Crisis (1832): South Carolina attempted to nullify federal tariffs based on compact theory; President Jackson rejected this as a violation of the Supremacy Clause.
Civil War and Aftermath: While the war resolved the legality of secession, states used compact theory to resist desegregation (Cooper v. Aaron) and the Affordable Care Act (NFIB v. Sebelius).
Recent Challenges to the Location of Sovereignty
U.S. Term Limits, Inc. v. Thornton (1995): The Court addressed whether Arkansas could add qualifications for congressional candidates.
National vs. State Disagreement: This 5-4 decision highlighted deep divisions on whether the power to set qualifications resides with the national populace or remains with the sovereign states.
U.S. Term Limits, Inc. v. Thornton
514 U.S. 779, 115 S. Ct. 1842, 131 L. Ed. 2d 881 (1995).
Facts
In 1992, voters in Arkansas adopted Amendment 73 to their State Constitution. This "Term Limitation Amendment" provided that any person who had served three or more terms in the U.S. House of Representatives or two or more terms in the U.S. Senate would be ineligible to appear on the ballot for re-election.
The amendment was challenged by Bobbie Hill, on behalf of the League of Women Voters, who argued that the amendment violated the federal Constitution. The Arkansas Supreme Court held that the amendment violated the Qualifications Clauses of Article I, which set forth the age, citizenship, and residency requirements for members of Congress. The case rose to the U.S. Supreme Court to determine if the state-imposed restriction was constitutional.
Issue May states alter or add to the qualifications for federal office established in the U.S. Constitution, or are the qualifications set forth in Article I the exclusive requirements for congressional eligibility?
Rule
The Qualifications Clauses (Art. I, §§ 2, 3): Establish the only requirements for serving in Congress (age, citizenship, and residency).
The Tenth Amendment: Reserves to the states powers not delegated to the federal government; however, it does not reserve powers that the states never originally possessed.
The Principle of Uniformity: The Framers intended for the national legislature to represent the people of the entire nation, not just separate states.
Application
Justice Stevens (Majority): Writing for the 5-4 majority, Stevens argued that the Constitution's qualifications are exclusive. He asserted that the power to add qualifications was not a "reserved power" under the Tenth Amendment because the states never had the power to appoint federal representatives before the Union was formed. He relied on the nationalist "We the People" view, stating that sovereignty rests in a single national populace and that a "patchwork" of state qualifications would undermine the "uniformity and national character" of the government.
Justice Thomas (Dissent): Representing the "compact theory" view, Thomas argued that the Constitution is silent on whether states can add qualifications. Therefore, under the Tenth Amendment, that power is reserved to the states. He famously asserted that the "ultimate source of the Constitution's authority is the consent of the people of each individual State, not the consent of the undifferentiated people of the Nation as a whole".
Conclusion The Supreme Court ruled that Arkansas's Amendment 73 was unconstitutional. States may not add qualifications for federal office, as doing so would violate the "fundamental principle of our representative democracy" that the people should choose whom they please to govern them.
Section 1. Term Limits and McCulloch
Opposing Structural "Default Rules":
Majority View: States possess no reserved powers over the federal government’s composition because the federal government did not exist prior to the Founding; thus, power cannot be "reserved" over a non-existent entity.
"Big Bang" Theory: The Founding created a new entity, and states only have the specific powers over it that the Constitution explicitly delegates to them.
Dissenting View: States retain all powers they previously held unless the Constitution specifically withholds them via express prohibition or necessary implication.
Withholding Power: The dissent argued the Constitution did not expressly withhold the power to set term limits, nor was state control over these limits a threat to federal structure like the tax in McCulloch.
Representation Reinforcement:
McCulloch Logic: A state cannot tax a national entity because it allows one state to impose costs (externalities) on citizens of other states who have no representation in that state’s legislature.
Negative Externalities of Term Limits: Argued that state-imposed limits "dumb down" Congress by reducing seniority and legislative experience for the entire nation.
Inverse McCulloch Argument: Conversely, it is argued that Arkansas only hurt itself by reducing the relative power and seniority of its own delegation in the national legislature.
Congressional Remedy: If Congress believed state-imposed limits weakened the body, it theoretically has the power to enact legislation prohibiting such measures.
Reliance on the Federalist Papers:
Dueling Interpretations: Both Justice Kennedy (concurrence) and Justice Thomas (dissent) cited Federalist No. 39 to reach opposite conclusions regarding whether the government is "national" or a collection of "independent States".
Reliability Debate: Historians disagree on the weight of the Federalist Papers; some argue they were mere advocacy that played little role in the actual ratification, while others view them as a "consensus understanding".
Section 4. Values Served by Federalism
Federalism in the Framers’ View:
"Double Security": Madison (Federalist No. 51) argued that dividing power vertically (state/federal) and horizontally (branches) provides a dual layer of protection for individual rights.
Functional Allocation: Roles like national defense and interstate commerce were given to the federal government because state-level regulation in these areas was unlikely to succeed.
Default Norm: The Framers expected that the majority of public policy regulation would remain with the states.
Structural Changes in the Federal System:
Shift in Power: The Civil War, the New Deal, and the rise of national regulatory agencies significantly shifted power from states to the federal government.
Fourteenth Amendment: Eventually used to apply nearly the entire Bill of Rights to the states, drastically limiting state autonomy.
Seventeenth Amendment: Providing for the direct election of Senators removed the primary structural check states had over the national legislature.
Values Promoted by State Autonomy:
Geographic Tailoring: States can adjust policies to fit local diversity in mores and circumstances.
Competition: Jurisdictions compete for citizens and investment, allowing people to "vote with their feet".
Laboratories of Democracy: States can try "novel social and economic experiments" without risking the entire nation (New State Ice Co.).
Accessibility: Smaller scale makes government more responsive and accessible to individual citizens.
Tyranny Prevention: Keeping functions like policing local avoids the danger of a national police force being captured by autocratic rulers.
Values Promoted by National Policymaking:
Externalities: National regulation is better for issues that cross state lines, such as air pollution.
Public Goods: The federal government provides goods that individual states underproduce, like common defense.
Social Insurance: The federal level can better redistribute resources to handle unpredictable catastrophes like hurricanes or floods.
Preventing "Races to the Bottom": National standards for child labor and minimum wage prevent states from cutting standards to attract business.
Protecting Minorities: Madison (Federalist No. 10) argued that a larger national "sphere" is less likely to be captured by local special interests or factions that might oppress minorities.
Section 5. Who is the Best Guardian of Federalism?
Political Safeguards:
Wechsler's View: The state's role in selecting the President and Congress (via the Electoral College and state boundaries) serves as an intrinsic check on federal overreach.
Erosion of Safeguards: Critics argue that the 17th Amendment and national media have destroyed these formal political checks.
Informal Safeguards: Lobbying groups (e.g., National Governors Association) and the use of states as training grounds for federal officials keep states influential.
Party System: Political parties link the fortunes of state and federal officeholders, inducing federal lawmakers to consider state interests.
Judicial vs. Political Determination:
John Manning: Argues the Necessary and Proper Clause delegates discretion to Congress, and the Court should not substitute its own "proper" criteria for that of the legislature.
Judicial Competence: It is debated whether courts are even capable of determining the "constitutional minimum" role for states, as the balance of power is not fixed to 1789 levels.
Election Law and Sovereignty:
"Hyperfederalized" Elections: Most key election decisions are made at the local level under Article I, § 4.
Independent State Legislature Theory: Recent controversies (Moore v. Harper) question whether state legislatures have sole authority over elections or if state courts can intervene.

Chapter 3
I. Executive Summary: The Evolution of Commerce Power
The Motivation: The Commerce Clause (Art. I, § 8, cl. 3) was written to replace the weak Articles of Confederation and prevent "trade wars" between states.

The Three Eras:
Early Era (1824–1890s): Broad definition of commerce (intercourse) but rarely used for national regulation.
Lochner/Pre-New Deal Era (1890s–1937): The Court used formalistic tests (Direct vs. Indirect) to strike down federal laws, trying to protect state "police powers."
New Deal/Post-1937 Era (1937–1995): Total judicial deference. The Court adopted the Aggregation Principle and the Substantial Effects Test, allowing Congress to regulate almost anything.
Modern Era (1995–Present): A "partial return" to limits. The Court (beginning with Lopez) emphasized that the activity must be economic/commercial in nature to be aggregated.
II. Detailed Case Briefs: Pre-New Deal Era
Gibbons v. Ogden (1824)
Procedural Posture: Ogden (monopoly holder) won an injunction in NY state courts. Gibbons (federal licensee) appealed to the Supreme Court.
Facts: The NY legislature granted a steamboat monopoly to Livingston and Fulton, who licensed Ogden. Gibbons, a former partner, began a competing service. Gibbons held a federal license under the 1793 Enrollment and Licensing Act.
Issue: Does the Commerce Clause grant Congress authority over navigation, and does federal law trump a state-granted monopoly?
Detailed Reasoning & Application:
The "Intercourse" Rule: Chief Justice Marshall rejected a narrow definition of commerce as "traffic" (buying and selling). He defined it as "intercourse," which necessarily includes navigation.
The "Among" Rule: "Among" means "intermingled with." Commerce does not stop at the state line; it can enter the interior of a state if the activity affects more than one state.
The "Plenary" Rule: Once an activity is deemed "interstate commerce," Congress’s power is plenary (absolute). The only checks are those specifically in the Constitution and the political process (voters).
Supremacy: Because the federal licensing statute conflicted with the NY monopoly, the state law was void under the Supremacy Clause.
United States v. E.C. Knight Co. (1895)
United States v. E.C. Knight Co. (1895)
Facts: The American Sugar Refining Co. acquired four Philadelphia refineries, giving it 98% control of the nation’s sugar refining. The government sued under the Sherman Antitrust Act.
Issue: Does the Commerce Clause allow Congress to regulate a monopoly in manufacturing?
Application & Reasoning:
Manufacture vs. Commerce: The Court drew a sharp line: Manufacture is NOT commerce. Production happens before commerce begins.
Direct vs. Indirect: The Court admitted that a 98% monopoly affects commerce, but called it an "indirect" effect.
State Police Power: The Court feared that if "indirect" effects were enough, Congress could regulate everything (agriculture, mining, etc.), leaving states with no power.
Shreveport Rate Case (1914)
Facts: Railroads charged lower rates for hauls within Texas than for hauls between Texas and Louisiana (Shreveport), discriminating against interstate trade. The ICC ordered the rates equalized.
Issue: Can Congress regulate intrastate rail rates?
Holding: Yes.
Application: The Court used the Substantial Economic Effects test. Because the intrastate rates had a "close and substantial relation" to interstate traffic, Congress could regulate them to protect interstate commerce.
Hammer v. Dagenhart (1918)
Facts: The Keating-Owen Act banned the interstate shipment of goods made by child labor. A father (Dagenhart) sued to allow his two underage sons to work in a cotton mill.
Issue: Can Congress use the transportation of goods to regulate the conditions of production?
Application & Reasoning (The "Pretext" Rule):
Harmless Goods: Unlike "lottery tickets" or "diseased meat," the cotton produced by children was harmless in itself.
Local Concern: The "evil" was child labor, which occurred entirely within the factory (the state).
10th Amendment: The Court held this was a pretext to invade the reserved powers of the states.
Note: This was famously overruled by US v. Darby.
III. Detailed Case Briefs: The New Deal "Switch"
Carter v. Carter Coal Co. (1936)
Facts: The Bituminous Coal Conservation Act set maximum hours and minimum wages for coal miners.
Issue: Is mining "commerce"?
Holding: No.
Application: The Court followed E.C. Knight. Mining is "production," which is local. The Court held that the magnitude of the effect doesn't matter; what matters is the nature of the relation. Since labor happens before the coal moves, it is an "indirect" effect.
NLRB v. Jones & Laughlin Steel Corp. (1937)
Facts: The 4th largest steel producer in the U.S. fired employees for unionizing. The NLRB ordered them reinstated under the Wagner Act.
Issue: Can Congress regulate labor relations in a local manufacturing plant?
Application & Reasoning:
Practical Conception: The Court abandoned the "direct/indirect" formalism. It viewed the steel company as a "highly integrated body" across many states.
Substantial Relation: If a labor strike at this plant happened, it would "paralyze" interstate commerce. Thus, the activity had a "close and substantial relation" to commerce, making it reachable.
Shift in Deference: This case signaled the Court would no longer strike down laws simply because they regulated "production."
United States v. Darby (1941)
Facts: A Georgia lumber manufacturer (Darby) was indicted for violating the Fair Labor Standards Act (minimum wage and maximum hours).
Issue: Can Congress regulate wages and hours of employees making goods for interstate commerce?
Application & Reasoning:
Regulation of Channels: Congress can prohibit the shipment of "proscribed goods" (made under bad conditions) as a way to regulate the channels of commerce.
Anti-Competition Rationale: Substandard labor in one state gives it an "unfair" advantage over states with better laws. Congress has the power to stop this "race to the bottom."
The "Truism": Justice Stone famously called the 10th Amendment a "truism"—it states that what isn't given is retained, but it doesn't limit the powers that are given (like the Commerce Clause).
Wickard v. Filburn (1942)
Facts: Filburn, a small dairy farmer, grew 239 bushels of wheat over his federal quota. He used the extra wheat solely to feed his own livestock and family—it never left his farm.
Issue: Can Congress regulate purely intrastate, non-commercial activity?
Application & Reasoning (The Aggregation Principle):
Market Effect: Even though Filburn’s wheat stayed on the farm, it affected the market. If he didn't grow his own, he would have bought it on the market, increasing demand.
Aggregation: Filburn’s own contribution was trivial, but "taken together with that of many others similarly situated," it had a substantial effect on the national price of wheat.
IV. Detailed Case Briefs: Civil Rights & Modern Limits
Heart of Atlanta Motel v. United States (1964)
Facts: A motel refused to rent rooms to Black travelers.
Issue: Can the Civil Rights Act use the Commerce Clause to stop private discrimination?
Holding: Yes.
Application: Discrimination "impedes" interstate travel. Since the motel served interstate travelers, its actions "affected commerce." The "moral" motive of Congress doesn't matter if there is an economic basis.
United States v. Lopez (1995)
Facts: A high school student (Lopez) carried a concealed handgun to school. He was charged under the federal "Gun-Free School Zones Act of 1990."
Issue: Does the Commerce Clause give Congress the power to regulate gun possession in a school zone?
Application & Reasoning (The Three Categories):
Chief Justice Rehnquist identified three things Congress can regulate:
Channels of commerce (roads, rivers).
Instrumentalities of commerce (planes, trains, persons/things in commerce).
Activities that Substantially Affect commerce.
The "Economic" Requirement: Carrying a gun is a non-economic, criminal activity. Unlike the wheat in Wickard, it isn't part of a larger commercial regulatory scheme.
Rejecting "Piling Inferences": The government argued that guns lead to crime, which affects insurance rates and travel. The Court rejected this, saying it would turn the Commerce Clause into a "general police power" and destroy federalism.
V. Summary Table of Judicial Tests

Section 4.The Contemporary Commerce Power
WEDNESDAY, FEBRUARY 4, 2026
Week 5
I. The Taxing Power as a Regulatory Device
Case 1: Child Labor Tax Case (Bailey v. Drexel Furniture Co.), 1922
Issue: Does the Child Labor Tax Law of 1919—which imposed a 10% tax on net profits of companies employing children—constitute a valid exercise of the Taxing Power, or is it an unconstitutional penalty intended to regulate local labor?
Rule:
Tax vs. Penalty: A "tax" is an exaction for the primary purpose of revenue. A "penalty" is an exaction intended to punish or regulate conduct.
Pretext Doctrine: Per McCulloch v. Maryland, if Congress uses a granted power (taxing) as a "pretext" to reach a subject not delegated to the federal government (local labor/police power), the Court must strike it down.
Analysis (Application):
The "Face" of the Act: The Court noted the law contained a detailed "scienter" requirement (the employer must know the child is underaged) and specific regulatory standards (hours/days worked).
The Regulatory Nature: The exaction was not proportional to the number of children employed; rather, it was a flat 10% of total profits for even a single violation.
Enforcement: It was enforced by the Department of Labor (regulatory) rather than just the Treasury.
Conclusion: The Court found the "tax" was actually a penalty used to achieve what Hammer v. Dagenhartpreviously prohibited under the Commerce Clause.
Conclusion: The law is Unconstitutional.
Case 2: United States v. Kahriger (1953)
Issue: Is a federal occupational tax on gamblers ($50/year plus 10% of wagers) a valid tax or an unconstitutional attempt to regulate intrastate gambling?
Rule: A federal excise tax is valid even if it discourages the activity taxed, and even if the revenue produced is negligible, provided it does not contain "penalty" provisions extraneous to tax collection.
Analysis (Application):
Revenue: Unlike the Child Labor Tax, this produced significant revenue.
Regulatory Effect: The Court held that "every tax is in some measure regulatory." The fact that it discourages gambling doesn't make it a penalty.
Distinction from Drexel: There was no "detailed specification" of conduct on the face of the act; it simply taxed a business.
Concurrence (Jackson): Expressed "doubts" because the law targeted illegal activity to "tax out of existence" what couldn't be prosecuted out of existence, but joined to avoid impairing the general taxing power.
Dissent (Frankfurter): Argued this was "verbal cellophane"—Congress wrapping a regulation in the language of a tax to control conduct reserved to the states.
Case 3: NFIB v. Sebelius (2012) – The Taxing Power Portion
Issue: Can the "Individual Mandate" (requiring people to buy health insurance or pay a "shared responsibility payment") be sustained under the Taxing Power?
Rule:
Functional Approach: The label (tax vs. penalty) does not control.
Three-Factor Test (Drexel factors): 1.  Burden: Is the cost prohibitive? 2.  Scienter: Does it require a "guilty mind"? 3.  Enforcement: Who collects it?
Analysis (Application):
Burden: The payment is much less than the cost of insurance (not a "heavy exaction").
Scienter: There is no "knowing" requirement; the payment is due regardless of intent.
Enforcement: It is collected by the IRS through normal tax returns and specifically lacks criminal sanctions.
Distinction: Unlike Drexel, it does not make the underlying conduct (not buying insurance) unlawful; it just attaches a price to that choice.
Dissent (Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Alito): * Argued the mandate is a "penalty" because the statute itself calls it one.
Contended that the Court shouldn't "rewrite" the statute to save it. If Congress chose to punish a violation of law, it's a penalty, not a tax.
Conclusion: The mandate is Constitutional as a tax.
II. The Spending Power as a Regulatory Device
Case 4: United States v. Butler (1936)
Issue: Is the Agricultural Adjustment Act (AAA)—which taxed processors to pay farmers to reduce acreage—a valid exercise of the Spending Power?
Rule:
Hamiltonian View (Adopted): Congress’s power to spend for the "General Welfare" is a standalone power, not limited by other enumerated powers (like Commerce).
Tenth Amendment Limit: Even a broad spending power cannot be used to "purchase" compliance in a field of purely local concern (like agriculture in 1936).
Analysis (Application):
The Court found the plan was "coercion by economic pressure." Farmers who didn't comply faced "financial ruin."
Even if voluntary, it was a "pretext" to regulate production, which the Court then considered a strictly state-level "police power" issue.
Dissent (Stone): * Argued "hope of gain" is not "threat of loss."
Conditions are valid if they ensure the money is spent for the national purpose intended.
Conclusion: The AAA is Unconstitutional.
Case 5: South Dakota v. Dole (1987)
Issue: Can Congress withhold 5% of highway funds from states that refuse to raise the drinking age to 21?
Rule (The Four-Part Test):
General Welfare: Must be for the public good (deference to Congress).
Unambiguous: Conditions must be clearly stated.
Germaneness: The condition must relate to the federal interest in the specific program.
Independent Bar: The condition cannot induce states to engage in unconstitutional conduct.
Coercion (Added): The financial inducement must not be so great as to be "compulsion."
Analysis (Application):
Germaneness: Drinking age is related to "safe interstate travel" (preventing "blood borders").
Coercion: Withholding only 5% of highway funds is "relatively mild encouragement." South Dakota could still say no without going bankrupt.
Dissent (O'Connor): * Argued the condition was not germane. Drinking ages are too "attenuated" from road construction.
Warned the Spending Clause was becoming a "back door" for Congress to regulate everything.
Conclusion: The law is Constitutional.
Case 6: NFIB v. Sebelius (2012) – The Spending Power Portion
Issue: Is the Medicaid expansion—which threatened to withhold all Medicaid funding if a state didn't expand eligibility—unconstitutionally coercive?
Rule: While Congress may offer grants and attach conditions, it may not use "financial weapons" to "dragoon" the states into a federal program.
Analysis (Application):
Pressure vs. Choice: Medicaid makes up ~20% of a state's budget. Losing 100% of that is "a gun to the head," not "mild encouragement."
New Program vs. Amendment: This wasn't just a "tweak" to Medicaid; it transformed it from a program for the "neediest" to a "comprehensive national plan."
Surprise: States could not have anticipated this "shift in kind" when they first joined Medicaid decades ago.
Dissent (Ginsburg/Sotomayor):
Argued Medicaid is a single program and Congress has amended it 50+ times.
States have no "right" to federal money; if they don't like the new deal, they can leave.
The "coercion" test is too vague for judges to manage (how much money is too much?).
Conclusion: The expansion's penalty (total fund withdrawal) is Unconstitutional. (The Court severed the penalty, allowing the expansion to exist as an option for states).
WEEK 6
DORMAT COMMERCE CLAUSE AND THE IMMUNITIS CLAUSE

The DCC isn't a written clause in the Constitution; it’s an inference drawn from the Commerce Clause (). It essentially says: "If Congress has the power to regulate interstate commerce, the States don't have the power to mess it up."
When a law isn't openly "discriminatory" (treating out-of-staters worse than locals), we use the Pike Balancing Test. Think of it as a literal scale. On one side, we have the state’s benefit; on the other, the burden on the national economy.
⚖️ The Pike Balancing Test (The Canonical Rule)
Source: Pike v. Bruce Church, Inc., 397 U.S. 137 (1970).
If a state law is facially neutral (applies to everyone) and regulates "even-handedly" to achieve a legitimate local public interest, it will be upheld UNLESS:
The burden on interstate commerce is clearly excessive in relation to the putative (supposed) local benefits.
The Pro-Tip: The Court also looks at whether that local interest could be promoted just as well with a lesser impact on interstate activities.
🚛 The "Battle of the Trucks": Safety vs. Commerce
A huge chunk of DCC litigation involves transportation. Why? Because trucks and trains move the commerce!
1. The Deferential View: Barnwell Bros.
Source: South Carolina State Highway Dept. v. Barnwell Bros., 303 U.S. 177 (1938).
The Rule: If a law is nondiscriminatory and involves local infrastructure (like highways), the Court should stay out of it. Highways are built and paid for by the State; they have a "primary and immediate concern" in their safety.
2. The Strict Balancing View: Southern Pacific & Kassel
Source: Southern Pacific Co. v. Arizona, 325 U.S. 761 (1945).
The Rule: If the "national interest in a free-flow of commerce" outweighs the "dubious" safety benefit of a state law, the law fails. Arizona tried to limit train lengths; the Court said, "No, we need national uniformity for trains."
Source: Kassel v. Consolidated Freightways Corp., 450 U.S. 662 (1981).
The Conflict: Iowa banned double-trailer trucks, claiming "safety."
The Outcome: The Court struck it down. Why? Because the "safety benefit" was illusory (fake) and the law had too many exemptions for Iowa companies, meaning Iowa was just "deflecting" traffic to other states.
🏢 Business Entry & Corporate Takeovers
The DCC also prevents states from acting like "parochial" (narrow-minded) gatekeepers of their markets.
🏛️ The Modern Shift: National Pork Producers v. Ross
Source: National Pork Producers Council v. Ross, 598 U.S. 356 (2023).
This is the "Pork Case" you mentioned, and it is the most important recent update for your 1L exam. California passed Proposition 12, which banned the sale of pork in California unless the pig was raised in a space large enough for it to turn around. Since most pork comes from outside California, pig farmers sued.
Key Takeaways from the Five Opinions:
Rejection of the "Extraterritoriality" Doctrine: The Court clarified that just because a state law has "upstream" effects (forcing out-of-state farmers to change their ways to sell in CA), it doesn't make it automatically unconstitutional.
The Pike "Bite" is Weakening: Several Justices (led by Gorsuch) argued that judges are ill-equipped to balance "incommensurate" values. How do you weigh "moral concerns about animal cruelty" against "the price of a pork chop"?
Scalia's Legacy: The plurality echoed Justice Scalia’s famous "line vs. rock" analogy. You can't compare the length of a line to the weight of a rock.
The Shift to History/Text: The Court is signaling a move away from "policy balancing" and toward asking: "Does this law look like the kind of economic protectionism the Founders specifically hated?"
Professor's Note: If you get a DCC question on an exam, check for discrimination first. If it's neutral, apply Pike, but mention that National Pork Producers suggests the Court is now very hesitant to strike down laws based on "balancing" unless the burden is truly extreme.

National Pork Producers Council v. Ross 
598 U.S. 356, 143 S. Ct. 1142, 215 L. Ed. 2d 336 (2023).
The Facts
In 2018, California voters approved Proposition 12. This law prohibited the sale of pork meat in California unless the pig was raised in conditions that allowed the animal to lie down, stand up, fully extend its limbs, and turn around freely.
The Problem: California consumes about 13% of the nation's pork but produces almost none of it. This meant that farmers in states like Iowa and North Carolina would have to completely overhaul their farming operations (costing hundreds of millions of dollars) if they wanted to sell into the massive California market. The National Pork Producers Council sued, arguing that California was essentially "regulating" farmers in other states, violating the DCC.
Issue
Does a state law violate the Dormant Commerce Clause when it imposes substantial compliance costs on out-of-state businesses, but does not purposefully discriminate against out-of-state economic interests?
Specifically, does the Pike Balancing Test require a court to weigh California’s moral interest in animal welfare against the nationwide economic burden on the pork industry?
Rule
The Dormant Commerce Clause generally prohibits:
Facial Discrimination: Laws that treat out-of-state goods differently than in-state goods.
Extraterritoriality (The "Old" Rule): Laws that have the "practical effect" of controlling commerce occurring wholly outside the state.
The Pike Balancing Test: Nondiscriminatory laws are unconstitutional if the burden on interstate commerce is "clearly excessive in relation to the putative local benefits." (Pike v. Bruce Church, Inc.).
Application (The "A" in IRAC)
The Court’s application here is split into three distinct "battles":
1. The Death of "Extraterritoriality": The Pork Producers argued that Prop 12 was unconstitutional simply because it forced out-of-state farmers to change their practices. The Court rejected this. Justice Gorsuch wrote that the DCC's core purpose is to stop protectionism (favoring local biz). Since Prop 12 applies to California pig farmers and Iowa pig farmers equally, it isn't protectionist.
2. The Pike Balancing "Muddle": The Producers argued that under Pike, the "benefit" (California's moral preference) was small compared to the "burden" (billions of dollars in industry costs).
The Application: The Court refused to balance. Gorsuch (joined by Thomas and Barrett) argued that judges are not "economists or philosophers." They cannot mathematically compare "animal dignity" vs. "pork prices." Because the interests are incommensurable (cannot be measured by the same standard), the court shouldn't even try to balance them unless there is evidence of a protectionist motive.
3. The Institutional Competence Argument: The Court applied the rule that the Constitution does not require a "uniform national market" for all goods. If California wants expensive, "ethical" pork and Iowans want cheap, "standard" pork, that is a matter for the market and the legislature, not the federal courts.
Conclusion
The Supreme Court affirmed the dismissal of the case. Proposition 12 stands. The Court held that while the DCC prohibits state laws that discriminate against out-of-state commerce, it does not allow courts to strike down neutral laws just because they are expensive or have effects across state lines.
💡 Why this matters for your 1L Exam
Source: National Pork Producers Council v. Ross, 598 U.S. 356 (2023).
The "New" Pike: After this case, the Pike balancing test is much harder to win. If a state has a moral or health justification, the Court is now very unlikely to second-guess the "cost" to the industry.
Discriminatory Intent is King: To win a DCC case now, you almost must show that the state was trying to help its own local businesses at the expense of outsiders.
