Section 2.The Interstate Privileges and Immunities Clause of Article IV
lass is in session! You’ve just hit on one of the most common "trap" areas for 1L Constitutional Law exams. Students often confuse the Dormant Commerce Clause (DCC) with the Article IV Privileges and Immunities (P&I) Clause.
While they both hate "protectionism," they operate with very different mechanics. Think of the DCC as a tool to protect the National Economy, while Art. IV P&I is a tool to protect Individual Citizens.
🔑 The Four "Key Distinctions" (The Cheat Sheet)
🏛️ The "Camden Case": United Building & Construction Trades Council v. Camden
Source: United Building & Construction Trades Council v. Mayor and Council of Camden, 465 U.S. 208 (1984).
The Facts
The City of Camden, NJ, passed an ordinance requiring that 40% of workers on city-funded construction projects be Camden residents. This was a direct attempt to fight local unemployment.
The Conflict
Under the DCC, this might have been okay because of the Market Participant Exception (Camden was the one paying for the construction). However, the challengers sued under Article IV P&I, arguing it discriminated against out-of-state workers (like those from nearby Philadelphia).
The Court’s Reasoning
Municipalities Count: Even though Art. IV mentions "States," it applies to cities/municipalities because they are subdivisions of the State.
No Market Participant Exception: Justice Rehnquist famously held that while the DCC keeps the government out of the "market," Art. IV P&I is a civil right. A state cannot buy its way out of the obligation to treat citizens of other states fairly.
The "Fundamental Right" Test: Pursuing a common calling (a job) is a fundamental right. Discriminating against out-of-staters for private-sector jobs (even on public contracts) triggers the Clause.
⚖️ The Two-Step Test for Art. IV P&I
When you see a state law discriminating against out-of-staters on your exam, run this check:
Step 1: Does the ordinance burden a "Privilege or Immunity"?
The Court looks for rights that are "fundamental to the promotion of interstate harmony."
Fundamental: The right to work, the right to travel, access to courts, medical care.
Non-Fundamental: Recreational hunting/fishing licenses (e.g., Baldwin v. Montana Fish and Game, 436 U.S. 371).
Step 2: Is there a "Substantial Reason" for the discrimination?
If a fundamental right is burdened, the State must show:
Non-residents are a peculiar source of the evil the law is trying to fix.
The discrimination is closely tailored (substantially related) to solving that problem.
Note on Camden: The Court remanded the case because Camden might have had a substantial reason (extreme urban decay/unemployment), but they hadn't proven it yet.
💡 Professor’s Pro-Tip: The "Citizen" Trap
On a 1L exam, always check the Plaintiff.
If "XYZ Corp" is suing, Art. IV P&I is DEAD on arrival (). You must use the Dormant Commerce Clause.
If "John Doe" (a person) is suing, you should analyze BOTH the DCC and Art. IV P&I.
United Building & Construction Trades Council v. Mayor and Council of City of Camden
465 U.S. 208, 104 S. Ct. 1020, 79 L. Ed. 2d 249 (1984).
📋 The Facts
The city of Camden, New Jersey, passed a municipal ordinance requiring that at least 40% of the employees of contractors and subcontractors working on city-funded construction projects be residents of Camden.
The United Building and Construction Trades Council (a labor organization) challenged the law. They argued it unconstitutionally discriminated against out-of-state workers (specifically those from nearby Pennsylvania) who were being denied jobs simply because they lived across the state line.
The New Jersey Supreme Court had initially rejected the challenge, arguing that the Market Participant Exception(which allows a city to favor its own citizens when it is "buying" services) applied.
Issue
Does the Privileges and Immunities Clause of Article IV apply to a municipal ordinance (city law) as opposed to a state law?
Does the Market Participant Exception (used in Dormant Commerce Clause analysis) exempt a law from scrutiny under the Privileges and Immunities Clause?
Does the ordinance violate the Privileges and Immunities Clause by discriminating against out-of-state residents regarding the "fundamental right" of employment?
Rule
Source: United Building & Construction Trades Council v. Mayor and Council of Camden, 465 U.S. 208 (1984).
The Scope Rule: A municipality is a political subdivision of a State; therefore, a city ordinance is subject to the Privileges and Immunities Clause just like a state law.
The "Fundamental Right" Rule: The right to pursue a "common calling" (private sector employment) is a fundamental privilege protected by Article IV.
The Two-Prong Test: Discrimination against out-of-state citizens is only valid if:
There is a substantial reason for the difference in treatment (i.e., out-of-staters are a "peculiar source" of the evil the law addresses).
The discrimination bears a substantial relationship to the state's objective (it is "closely tailored").
Application
1. No Market Participant Out: The Court explicitly rejected the city's defense that it was acting as a "market participant." While the Dormant Commerce Clause is a check on the market, the P&I Clause is a check on discrimination against individuals. A state cannot "buy" the right to violate a citizen's constitutional right to equal treatment.
2. Is Employment Fundamental? The Court held that the opportunity to seek employment with private contractors is "sufficiently fundamental to the promotion of interstate harmony" to fall within the Clause. Even though the projects were city-funded, the workers were private employees, not government officials.
3. The "Substantial Reason" (The Remand): Camden argued its "substantial reason" was "grave economic and social ills," such as high unemployment and a declining tax base. The Supreme Court noted these could be valid reasons, but because the lower court hadn't actually weighed the evidence of whether out-of-staters were the cause of these problems, the Court remanded the case for further fact-finding.
Conclusion
The Court reversed the lower court's judgment. It held that the ordinance was subject to the Privileges and Immunities Clause and that the "Market Participant Exception" did not apply. The city had to prove on remand that the discrimination against out-of-staters was necessary to solve its specific economic crisis.
💡 1L Study Note: The "Camden" vs. "White" Distinction
In your exams, you might see White v. Massachusetts Council of Construction Employers (1983). In White, a similar Boston residency requirement was upheld under the Dormant Commerce Clause because Boston was a market participant.
The Takeaway: The exact same law can be legal under the Commerce Clause but illegal (or strictly scrutinized) under the Privileges and Immunities Clause.
The Scope and Limits of Interstate Privileges and Immunities
We are moving from the "Economic Protectionism" of the Commerce Clause into the "Civil Rights of Federalism" found in Article IV, § 2.
These notes are designed to help you crush a 1L ConLaw issue-spotter by distinguishing between "fundamental" and "non-fundamental" rights and applying the specific intermediate scrutiny test used in P&I cases.
⚖️ 1. The Threshold: Is it a "Privilege"?
The Privileges and Immunities (P&I) Clause does not protect every activity. It only protects rights that are "fundamental" to national unity.
The "Common Calling" (Private Employment)
The Rule: The right to pursue a "common calling" (a job) is a fundamental privilege.
Public vs. Private: * Private Employment: Covered ().
State-Licensed Professions: Covered ().
Public Employment: The Court has left this open, but it likely doesn't count as a "privilege" because states have more leeway to hire their own citizens for government roles.
Case Study: Supreme Court of New Hampshire v. Piper (1985)
Facts: Piper lived in Vermont (400 yards from the border), passed the NH Bar, but was denied admission because she wasn't an NH resident.
Holding: Violates Art. IV P&I.
Why? The practice of law is "important to the national economy" and essential for representing people with "unpopular federal claims." Lawyers aren't just state officers; they are professionals in a national market.
🧪 2. The P&I Test (Intermediate Scrutiny)
If a law discriminates against out-of-state citizens regarding a fundamental right, the state must satisfy a two-part test (from Camden and Piper):
Substantial Reason: There must be a "substantial reason" for the difference in treatment. The state must show that non-residents are a "peculiar source of the evil" the statute aims to fix.
Substantial Relationship: The discrimination must bear a "substantial relationship" to the state’s objective. This includes looking at less restrictive means.
The Piper Application of the Test:
NH offered four reasons to bar non-residents, all of which the Court rejected:
Familiarity with local rules: Non-residents can study just as hard.
Ethical behavior: Residency doesn't guarantee morality.
Availability for court: We have phones and cars (and now, Zoom).
Pro bono work: You can require a certain amount of pro bono work without requiring residency.
🛡️ 3. Rehnquist’s Dissent: The "State's Lawyer" Exception
Justice Rehnquist (the frequent dissenter in these cases) argued that the law is different from other jobs.
The "Legislative Pool" Argument: He argued states have a right to ensure a pool of resident lawyers from which to draw future lawmakers.
Attack on "Less Restrictive Means": He hated importing this "First Amendment" logic into Article IV, fearing it would allow judges to strike down almost any state law by dreaming up a "better" way to write it.
🥊 4. P&I vs. Dormant Commerce Clause (DCC)
This is where the points are made on your final.
Levels of Scrutiny
DCC (Discrimination): Uses Strict Scrutiny. Virtually per se invalid. The state must show a compelling interest and that the law is the least restrictive means ().
Art. IV P&I: Uses Intermediate Scrutiny. The state needs a substantial reason and a substantial relationship.
The "Camden" Remand Hypothetical
If the Camden case hadn't settled, how would the city prove non-residents were the "peculiar source of evil"?
The Argument: Camden would need to show that out-of-state commuters were "draining" the city's wealth, causing urban decay, and that hiring locals was the only way to stop the "death spiral" of the city.
The Problem: It’s hard to prove that Pennsylvanians are the specific cause of Camden's poverty.
Summary Comparison Table
Export to Sheets
💡 Professor’s Final Strategy Note
When you see a residency requirement on an exam:
Check the Plaintiff: If it’s a person, argue both DCC and P&I. If it’s a company, DCC only.
Check for "Market Participant": If the state is spending its own money (like in Camden), the DCC claim might fail, but the P&I claim will survive and likely win.
Check the Right: If it's something "non-essential" like an elk-hunting license, the P&I claim dies because it's not a "fundamental" privilege ().
Section 3.Congressional Ordering of Federal-State Relationships by Preemption and Consent
We are moving from the Dormant Commerce Clause (where Congress is silent) to Preemption (where Congress has spoken).
This is a Supremacy Clause () issue. Once Congress passes a law, the "dormant" period ends, and the "active" period begins. The question is no longer "Does this burden commerce?" but rather "Did Congress mean to kick the State out of this room?"
🚀 The Three Flavors of Preemption
When Congress regulates, it can displace state law in three ways. Use this hierarchy for any exam answer:
1. Express Preemption
The Rule: The federal statute contains explicit language stating that it displaces state law (e.g., "No State shall establish any requirement regarding...").
The Task: Simple statutory interpretation. Does the state law fall within the "zone" Congress explicitly forbade?
2. Field Preemption (Implied)
The Rule: Federal regulation is so "pervasive" or the federal interest is so "dominant" that we assume Congress left no room for the states to supplement it.
The Classic Example: Alien registration or nuclear safety.
Key Phrase: Congress has "occupied the field."
3. Conflict Preemption (Implied)
This is the most common "grey area" on exams. It splits into two sub-types:
Impossibility Conflict: It is physically impossible to comply with both state and federal law (e.g., Federal law says "Label this 'Red'" and State law says "Label this 'Blue'").
Obstacle Conflict: The state law stands as an "obstacle to the accomplishment and execution of the full purposes and objectives of Congress."
You said 
Pacific Gas & Electric Co. v. State Energy Resources Conservation & Development Comm’n 
461 U.S. 190, 103 S. Ct. 1713, 75 L. Ed. 2d 752 (1983).
📋 The Facts
In 1976, California amended its Public Resources Code to address nuclear power. Specifically, Section 25524.2 imposed a moratorium on the certification of new nuclear power plants until the State Energy Commission found that there was a demonstrated technology or a "means of disposal" for high-level nuclear waste.
The Federal Law: The Atomic Energy Act (AEA) of 1954 created a comprehensive federal scheme for regulating nuclear power, managed by the Nuclear Regulatory Commission (NRC).
The Conflict: Pacific Gas & Electric (PG&E) sued, arguing that because the federal government had "occupied the field" of nuclear regulation and had already deemed nuclear power "safe" enough to license, California couldn't step in and block construction based on waste disposal concerns.
Issue
Does the federal Atomic Energy Act (AEA) preempt a state law that prohibits the construction of nuclear power plants until a state agency determines there is a viable method for nuclear waste disposal?
Rule
Source: Pacific Gas & Electric Co. v. State Energy Resources Conservation & Development Comm'n, 461 U.S. 190 (1983).
Under the Supremacy Clause (), federal law preempts state law in three instances:
Express Preemption: Congress explicitly states its intent to displace state law.
Field Preemption: Federal regulation is so "pervasive" that it's reasonable to infer Congress left no room for states to supplement it.
Conflict Preemption:
Impossibility: Compliance with both is a physical impossibility.
Obstacle: State law stands as an "obstacle to the accomplishment" of federal objectives.
Application
The Court, led by Justice White, broke the analysis into "Safety" vs. "Economics":
1. Field Preemption (The "Safety" Field): The Court admitted that the federal government has occupied the entire field of nuclear safety. If California had passed this law because it thought nuclear plants were "dangerous," the law would be preempted immediately.
2. The "Economic" Loophole: California argued its motive was economical, not safety-oriented. They claimed that without a waste disposal plan, nuclear plants would become "clogged" and incredibly expensive to maintain, hurting California ratepayers. The Court held that states have a traditional "police power" to regulate the economics of utilities (rates, need for power, etc.). Because the AEA didn't explicitly take away the state's power to decide if a plant was a "good investment," the state law survived.
3. Conflict Preemption (The "Obstacle" Test): PG&E argued California was an "obstacle" to the federal goal of promoting nuclear power. The Court disagreed, stating the AEA’s goal was to develop nuclear power safely, not to force states to build nuclear plants they didn't want or couldn't afford.
Conclusion
The Supreme Court held the California law was NOT preempted. As long as the state could point to a "rational" non-safety basis (economics), the law did not intrude on the federal government’s exclusive control over nuclear safety.
💡 1L Study Secret: The "Motive" Trap
In PG&E, the Court refused to look behind California's stated "economic" reason to see if they were secretly lying about "safety."
The Lesson: For your exam, if a state law looks like it's preempted, check if the state has labeled its law under a different "purpose" (e.g., calling a safety law an "economic" law). If that purpose is a traditional state power (like utility regulation), the Court is very likely to let it slide.
Modes of Preemption Analysis
t covers how the Court decides when a state law must die because a federal law exists.
Here are your "detailed as fuck" notes, organized by the specific cases and doctrines mentioned.
🏛️ 1. The Three Pillars of Preemption
Source: Rice v. Santa Fe Elevator Corp. (1947); Hines v. Davidowitz (1941).
A. The Presumption Against Preemption
In areas of "historic police powers" (health, safety, banking, farming), the Court starts with the assumption that the state law is VALID unless there is a "clear and manifest purpose of Congress" to kill it.
🥑 2. Conflict Preemption: The "Two Strands"
Strand 1: Physical Impossibility
Source: Florida Lime & Avocado Growers, Inc. v. Paul (1963).
The Conflict: Federal law said avocados are "mature" at X% oil. California said "We won't sell them unless they have Y% oil."
The Holding: No Preemption.
The Logic: You can comply with both! Just meet the higher California standard. Federal law set a floor (minimum), not a ceiling (uniformity).
Strand 2: Obstacle Preemption
Source: Hines v. Davidowitz (1941); Wyeth v. Levine (2009).
The Logic: Does the state law stand as an "obstacle to the accomplishment and execution of the full purposes and objectives of Congress?"
Wyeth v. Levine Case: A drug company argued they couldn't be sued under state law for a bad label because the FDA approved the label.
Result: No Preemption. The Court held that the manufacturer is ultimately responsible for its own labels, and state lawsuits actually help federal safety goals rather than hindering them.
Justice Thomas (Concurrence): He hates "Obstacle Preemption." He thinks judges shouldn't guess at "purposes" or "objectives"—they should only look at the text of the law.
🌍 3. Foreign Affairs Preemption
Source: Crosby v. National Foreign Trade Council (2000).
Facts: Massachusetts tried to boycott Burma. Congress also had sanctions on Burma.
Holding: Preempted.
The Reasoning:
Calibration of Force: Congress gave the President "flexible discretion" to turn sanctions on/off. Massachusetts' law was "inflexible," like a blunt instrument where Congress wanted a scalpel.
One Voice: The President needs to speak for the whole nation in foreign policy. State laws "compromise the very capacity of the President to speak for the Nation with one voice."
🛂 4. The Immigration Showdown: Arizona v. United States
Source: Arizona v. United States (2012). Arizona passed SB 1070 to "deter" illegal immigration. The Court struck down 3 out of 4 provisions:
Provision 1 (Alien Registration): Preempted. Congress already has a "complete scheme" for registering aliens. This is Field Preemption. Even "complementary" state laws are banned.
Provision 2 (Working while undocumented): Preempted. Congress chose civil penalties for workers. Arizona chose criminal penalties. This is Obstacle Preemption because it upsets the balance Congress struck.
Provision 3 (Warrantless Arrests): Preempted. It gave state officers more power than federal officers, leading to "unnecessary harassment."
Provision 4 (Status Check): NOT Preempted (Yet). The "show me your papers" provision. The Court said states can talk to the feds. Communication is encouraged, so asking for a status check isn't an obstacle—unless it leads to prolonged, illegal detention.
🎯 1L Summary Checklist for Preemption Exams
Is the Federal Law Silent? (If yes, go back to Dormant Commerce Clause/P&I).
Is there Express Language? (Check the prompt for "No state shall...").
Is it a "Field" we traditionally leave to Feds? (Foreign policy, Immigration, Nuclear safety).
Can a person follow both laws? (If you can follow the "stricter" one, there is no Impossibility).
Does the State law "frustrate" a Federal goal? (This is the most "lawyerly" argument where you argue about the purpose of the federal act).
Section 4.Other Aspects of Federal-State Relationships
State Taxation of Interstate Business and Income
You’ve just reached the "pocketbook" of Constitutional Law. This section covers how states get their money (taxation), how the federal government and states stay out of each other's way (immunities), and how states play nice with one another (interstate obligations).
Here are your detailed notes, simplified for the 1L experience.
💰 1. State Taxation of Interstate Commerce
The Court tries to prevent "Multiple Taxation" (where every state a truck drives through tries to tax 100% of its value).
A. The Complete Auto Four-Part Test
Source: Complete Auto Transit, Inc. v. Brady, 430 U.S. 274 (1977). A state tax is constitutional under the Dormant Commerce Clause only if it meets all four prongs:
Substantial Nexus: The activity taxed must have a significant connection to the state.
Fair Apportionment: The state can only tax the portion of the business activity that actually happens in that state.
Nondiscriminatory: The tax cannot treat out-of-state businesses worse than in-state ones.
Fair Relationship: The tax must be reasonably related to the services provided by the state (police, roads, fire protection).
B. The "Physical Presence" Revolution: Wayfair
Source: South Dakota v. Wayfair, 584 U.S. 162 (2018).
Old Rule (Quill): A state could only force a business to collect sales tax if that business had a physical building or employees in the state.
New Rule (Wayfair): Physical presence is dead. "Substantial nexus" can be established through "virtual presence." If you sell enough into a state (e.g., $100,000 or 200 transactions), that state can make you collect sales tax.
The Logic: Justice Kennedy argued that the old rule created a "tax shelter" for online giants like Amazon (at the time) and hurt local "mom-and-pop" shops.
🛡️ 2. Intergovernmental Tax Immunities
This is the "You can't tax me, I'm the Government" rule, originating in McCulloch v. Maryland.
The Rule: A State may not lay a tax directly upon the United States ().
The Narrow Approach: Immunity only applies when the tax falls on the U.S. itself or an "instrumentality" so closely connected to the U.S. that they are effectively the same thing (United States v. New Mexico).
Third Parties: If you work for the Feds, the State can tax your personal income (Graves v. O'Keefe). The immunity doesn't "rub off" on employees or contractors anymore.
🚦 3. Intergovernmental Regulatory Immunities
Can a state tell a federal employee how to do their job? Usually, no.
The Rule: Federal instruments are immune from state control that interferes with their duties (Johnson v. Maryland).
Example: A Post Office driver doesn't need a state driver's license to drive a mail truck because the Feds have already decided they are competent.
The Limit: Federal employees must still follow "general rules" that affect them incidentally, like traffic lights or turn signals.
🤝 4. Mutual Obligations Among the States
How the 50 states act like a single nation.
A. The Extradition Clause ()
If a criminal flees from State A to State B, State B must give them back if the Governor of State A asks.
Enforcement: This is a mandatory duty. Federal courts can use mandamus (a court order) to force a governor to hand over a fugitive (Puerto Rico v. Branstad).
B. The Compact Clause ()
States can enter into "Agreements or Compacts" with each other (e.g., sharing a river or a bridge).
Consent Requirement: You only need Congress's permission if the compact increases the political power of the states in a way that interferes with federal supremacy.
Example: The Multistate Tax Compact did not need consent because it didn't take power away from the feds (U.S. Steel Corp.).
🎯 1L Study Checklist: State Tax Problems
When you see a state tax on an exam, run the Complete Auto check:
Nexus: Is the business selling into the state? (Post-Wayfair, virtual is enough).
Apportionment: Is the state trying to tax all the income, or just the income made there?
Discrimination: Is there a "tax break" that only locals get? (If yes, it's a "tariff" and it's dead—Wynne).
Relationship: Does the state provide police/roads to the business?
WEEK 8
WEDNESDAY MARCH 4, 2026
SECTION 5: EXECUTIVE Privileges, Immunities and Congress’s Power of Impeachment
United States v. Nixon 418 U.S. 683, 94 S. Ct. 3090, 41 L. Ed. 2d 1039 (1974).
The Facts: The Watergate Tangle
In 1972, five men were caught breaking into the Democratic National Committee headquarters at the Watergate complex. The ensuing investigation by a Special Prosecutor revealed that President Richard Nixon had a voice-activated taping system in the Oval Office.
The Special Prosecutor issued a subpoena duces tecum (a court order to produce documents/evidence) for specific tapes to be used in the criminal trial of Nixon’s associates. Nixon moved to quash (void) the subpoena, claiming "executive privilege"—an asserted right of the President to keep communications confidential to ensure candid advice from aides.
Issue
Whether the President of the United States has an absolute, unqualified executive privilege of immunity from judicial process under all circumstances, specifically regarding a subpoena for evidence in a criminal trial?
Rule
While the Constitution does not explicitly mention executive privilege, a presumptive privilege exists based on the separation of powers and the need for confidential high-level communications. However, this privilege is not absolute. It must be weighed against the primary constitutional duty of the Judicial Branch to do justice in criminal proceedings.
Analysis
The Court’s reasoning followed three major steps:
Marbury Redux: Chief Justice Burger reaffirmed Marbury v. Madison, stating it is the province of the Court to "say what the law is." This means the President doesn't get to be the final judge of his own privilege.
The Balancing Test: The Court weighed the generalized interest in confidentiality (President's side) against the specific need for evidence in a criminal trial (Judiciary's side).
Note: The Court hinted that if the tapes involved "military, diplomatic, or sensitive national security secrets," the privilege would be much stronger.
The Fair Administration of Justice: The Court held that "the manifest need for relevant evidence in criminal trials" is a requirement of the Fifth Amendment Due Process Clause and the Sixth Amendment right to confront witnesses. Allowing a President to withhold evidence based on a general claim of confidentiality would "gravely impair the basic function of the courts."
Conclusion
No. The Supreme Court affirmed the District Court's order. The President must produce the tapes. The Court held that a generalized assertion of privilege cannot prevail over the fundamental demands of due process of law in the fair administration of criminal justice.
Key Takeaway for your Outline
Source: United States v. Nixon, 418 U.S. 683 (1974).
The "Nixon Rule": Executive privilege is qualified, not absolute. It is a presumptive privilege that can be overcome by a showing of "demonstrably relevant" evidence needed for a criminal trial.
NOTES FROM THE CASE
 Amenability to Process: Can the President be Sued?
The fundamental question in Nixon was whether a President is "above the law" or "amenable" to the orders of a judge.
The Nixon Shift: Before 1974, the rule from Mississippi v. Johnson (1867) suggested the President was largely immune from injunctions because of the difficulty of enforcement. Nixon settled this: The President is amenable to judicial process while in office.
The Impeachment Argument: Nixon argued that the only way to hold a President accountable was via impeachment. The Court rejected this, decoupling criminal evidence from the political process of removal.
Academic Critique: Professor Gunther (22 UCLA L. Rev. 30) argues the Court may have overreached. He suggests the Court could have said "Yes, the President must answer the subpoena," while still letting the President decide which specific conversations remain secret (autonomy).
2. The Strength of the Privilege: Criminal vs. Legislative
The "weight" of executive privilege changes depending on who is asking for the information.
Criminal Subpoenas (Strongest Counter-Interest): In Nixon, the need for "the fair administration of criminal justice" was a constitutional heavyweight that crushed the President's generalized claim of confidentiality.
Congressional Oversight (The Gray Area): Administrations have claimed privilege against Congress since George Washington.
The Tension: Congress has a "weighty" oversight power, but is it as "quasi-constitutional" as a criminal trial?
The Political Check: Often, these disputes are settled by politics (public opinion/withholding funding) rather than courts. If a President looks like they are hiding something, they lose "political capital."
3. The "Who" and "What": Scope of the Privilege
How far down the organizational chart does this "bubble of protection" go?
The Protective Function Privilege: In In re Sealed Case (1998), the D.C. Circuit rejected a "Secret Service Privilege." The government argued that if the President doesn't trust his guards, he might distance himself and risk "national calamity." The courts, however, are hesitant to create new privileges that "hide the truth."
The "Voluntary" Paradox: If confidentiality is so vital to the office of the Presidency, shouldn't the President be forbidden from sharing secrets even if he wants to? This highlights that the privilege is often used as a shield for the person, not just a seal for the office.
4. The Modern Standard: Trump v. Mazars (2020)
When Congress seeks a President's personal records (like tax returns or bank records), we use a specific balancing test because "separation of powers" is at its peak tension.
The Mazars Four-Factor Test:
Does the legislative purpose warrant involving the President? (Can they get the info elsewhere?)
Is the subpoena no broader than necessary?
Does the subpoena provide detailed evidence of a valid legislative objective?
What is the burden on the President?
Summary Table for Your Outline
Trump v. Mazars USA, LLP
591 U.S. 848, 140 S. Ct. 2019, 207 L. Ed. 2d 951 (2020).
The Facts
In 2019, three committees of the House of Representatives issued subpoenas to third-party financial institutions (Mazars LLP, Deutsche Bank, and Capital One) seeking the personal financial records of President Donald Trump, his children, and his affiliated businesses.
The House argued it needed these documents to evaluate the adequacy of existing ethics-in-government laws and money-laundering statutes. President Trump, acting in his personal capacity, sued to block the subpoenas, arguing they lacked a "legitimate legislative purpose" and violated the separation of powers. Unlike Nixon, Trump did not technically assert "executive privilege" (since these were personal, pre-presidency records), but he argued that the presidency itself required special protection from overbroad congressional inquiries.
Issue
Does the separation of powers set limits on the authority of Congress to subpoena the personal financial records of a sitting President and his family?
Rule
Congress has a broad, implied power to investigate, but that power is justified only if it serves a "legitimate legislative purpose." When a subpoena targets the President’s personal records, a "balanced" approach is required that respects the separation of powers, rather than the low-bar "rational basis" test used for ordinary citizens.
Application
The Court rejected both extreme arguments: the House's claim that the President should be treated like any other citizen, and the President's claim that Congress must show a "critical" need. Chief Justice Roberts applied a four-factor balancing test to determine if the subpoena is valid:
Availability of Other Sources: Congress should not target the President if the information can be reasonably obtained from other sources.
Scope of the Subpoena: The subpoena must be "no broader than necessary" to support the legislative objective. (Essentially, don't ask for a decade of records if one year will do).
Evidence of Purpose: Congress must provide detailed evidence that the subpoena actually serves a valid legislative goal (the more detailed the plan for future legislation, the better).
Burden on the President: Courts must assess the burden (time and distraction) the subpoena imposes on the Executive branch.
Conclusion
Vacated and Remanded. The Supreme Court held that the lower courts had failed to properly account for the "significant separation of powers concerns" raised by the subpoenas. The Court sent the case back to the lower courts to apply the new four-factor test.
Key Takeaway for your Outline
The "Mazars Test": When Congress subpoenas a President's personal records, it isn't "business as usual." The Court won't just rubber-stamp the request; Congress must prove the information is uniquely necessary for lawmaking and is narrowly tailored to avoid interfering with the President’s duties.
Clinton v. Jones
520 U.S. 681, 117 S. Ct. 1636, 137 L. Ed. 2d 945 (1997).
Facts
In 1994, Paula Jones, a former Arkansas state employee, filed a civil lawsuit against President Bill Clinton. She alleged that in 1991, while Clinton was the Governor of Arkansas, he made "abhorrent" sexual advances toward her in a hotel suite and that her rejection of those advances led to retaliation by her state supervisors.
President Clinton moved to dismiss the complaint on the grounds of Presidential immunity and requested that all proceedings be deferred until he left office. He argued that the distraction of private litigation would violate the separation of powers by interfering with his ability to discharge his Article II duties.
Issue
Is a sitting President entitled to immunity (even temporary) from civil litigation arising out of unofficial events that occurred before he took office?
Rule
The doctrine of separation of powers does not require federal courts to stay (pause) all private civil litigation against a sitting President until his term ends. While a President has absolute immunity for official acts (Nixon v. Fitzgerald), that immunity does not extend to unofficial conduct, especially conduct that predates his presidency.
Application
The Court’s analysis rested on three main points:
The Nature of the Acts: The Court emphasized that immunity is "functional." It exists to protect the functions of the office (official acts), not the person (unofficial acts). Because the alleged harassment occurred before Clinton was President, it was unrelated to his Article II powers.
Separation of Powers: The Court rejected the idea that the Judiciary’s exercise of jurisdiction over the President violates the separation of powers. They noted that the Judiciary often reviews executive actions (e.g., U.S. v. Nixon), and performing this duty is not "interference."
The "Distraction" Argument: The Court dismissed Clinton’s claim that the trial would be too time-consuming. Justice Stevens wrote that it was "highly unlikely to occupy any substantial amount of [the President's] time."
Note for your notes: This turned out to be historically incorrect, as the discovery in this case led directly to the Lewinsky scandal and Clinton's impeachment.
Conclusion
No. The Supreme Court unanimously (9–0) held that the litigation could proceed. The President is not entitled to temporary immunity for unofficial acts, though trial judges should show "high respect" for the President’s schedule when managing the case.
Professor's Tip for your 1L Exam
When you see a tort or contract claim against a President on an exam, check the timeline:
Pre-Presidency/Private Act? Apply Clinton v. Jones (No immunity).
During Presidency/Official Act? Apply Nixon v. Fitzgerald (Absolute immunity).
Trump v. Vance
591 U.S. 786, 140 S. Ct. 2412, 207 L. Ed. 2d 907 (2020).
Trump v. Vance is the modern definitive word on how Federalism (the relationship between the States and the Federal Government) interacts with the Presidency.
Facts
In 2019, Cyrus Vance Jr., the District Attorney for New York County, was conducting a state grand jury investigation into business transactions involving the Trump Organization. As part of this investigation, he issued a subpoena duces tecum to Mazars USA, LLP—President Trump’s personal accounting firm. The subpoena sought nearly ten years of the President’s personal and business financial records, including tax returns.
President Trump sued in federal court to block the subpoena. He argued that as a sitting President, he enjoyed absolute immunity from state criminal process under Article II and the Supremacy Clause. He claimed that state investigations would "distract" him from his duties, "stigmatize" the office, and open the door to "harassment" by politically motivated local prosecutors.
Issue
Does the Constitution grant a sitting President absolute immunity from state criminal subpoenas for private papers, or at least require a "heightened standard of need" before such a subpoena can be enforced?
Rule
Article II and the Supremacy Clause do not categorically preclude the issuance of a state criminal subpoena to a sitting President, nor do they require a "heightened need" standard for subpoenas seeking a President’s private, non-privileged records.
Application
The Court’s analysis, led by Chief Justice Roberts, systematically dismantled the President’s three "burden" arguments:
Diversion/Distraction: The Court relied on the precedent of Clinton v. Jones, noting that if the President isn't immune from federal civil trials, he isn't immune from the relatively low burden of a third-party subpoena for documents.
Stigma: The Court held that there is nothing inherently "stigmatizing" about a President performing the "citizen's normal duty" of providing evidence. Furthermore, grand jury secrecy rules protect the President from public embarrassment.
Harassment: While the Court acknowledged the risk of "vexatious" state prosecutions, it held that the Supremacy Clause and the Due Process Clause already provide a shield. A President can challenge a subpoena in court if it is "issued in bad faith" or is "unduly broad," just like any other citizen.
No Heightened Standard: The Court refused to apply the "demonstrated, specific need" standard from U.S. v. Nixon because that case involved Executive Privilege (official communications). Here, the records were private financial documents, and the President "stands in nearly the same situation with any other individual" regarding his private papers.
Conclusion
Affirmed. The Supreme Court held that the President is not immune from the subpoena. The case was remanded to the lower courts, allowing the President to raise standard, non-immunity-based objections (like overbreadth).
Professor’s Note for the Exam
The "Burr Connection": Chief Justice Roberts relied heavily on the 1807 treason trial of Aaron Burr, where Chief Justice Marshall famously ruled that President Thomas Jefferson could be subpoenaed. The takeaway is: No person, not even the President, is so high that they are above the common duty to produce evidence for a criminal proceeding.
Trump v. United States
603 U.S. 593, 144 S. Ct. 2312, 219 L. Ed. 2d 991 (2024).
Facts
In 2023, President Donald Trump was indicted on four federal counts related to his efforts to overturn the 2020 presidential election. The indictment alleged he conspired to defraud the U.S., obstruct an official proceeding, and interfere with voting rights.
Trump moved to dismiss the indictment, claiming absolute immunity from criminal prosecution for all "official acts" performed while in office. He argued that without such immunity, the Presidency would be crippled by the threat of future prosecutions by political rivals. The District Court and the D.C. Circuit both rejected this claim, holding that a former President has no more immunity than any other citizen.
Issue
Whether, and to what extent, does a former President enjoy immunity from criminal prosecution for conduct alleged to involve "official acts" during his tenure in office?
Rule
The Court established a tripartite framework for Presidential criminal immunity:
Core Constitutional Acts: The President has absolute immunity for actions within his "conclusive and preclusive" constitutional authority (e.g., the pardon power, veto power, or removing executive officers).
Official Acts: The President has presumptive immunity for all other official acts within the "outer perimeter" of his responsibility. This presumption can only be overcome if the government shows that the prosecution poses "no danger of intrusion on the authority and functions of the Executive Branch."
Unofficial Acts: The President has no immunity for unofficial (private) acts.
Application
The Court applied this framework to the specific allegations against Trump:
The DOJ Allegations: The Court held that Trump’s discussions with Department of Justice officials regarding election investigations were core official acts subject to absolute immunity.
Vice President Pressure: The Court found Trump's communications with Mike Pence regarding the certification of the election were official acts, entitling him to presumptive immunity.
The "Evidence Rule": Critically, the Court held that in a trial for unofficial acts, the prosecutor cannot introduce evidence of immune official acts. To do so, the Court reasoned, would effectively nullify the immunity by allowing the jury to punish the President for his official conduct.
Conclusion
Vacated and Remanded. The Court did not dismiss the entire case but sent it back to the District Court to determine which specific acts in the indictment were "official" versus "unofficial" under this new test.
The Concurrences
Justice Thomas: Wrote separately to question the legality of the Special Prosecutor (Jack Smith) himself. He argued that if the office of the Special Prosecutor was not established by an Act of Congress, the entire prosecution might be unconstitutional under the Appointments Clause.
Justice Barrett: Concurred in part but disagreed with the "Evidence Rule." She argued that if a President is prosecuted for a private crime (e.g., a bribe), the government should still be allowed to show the "official act" (the thing the President did in exchange for the bribe) as evidence of the crime.
The Dissents
Justice Sotomayor (joined by Kagan and Jackson): Issued a scathing dissent, famously writing: "In every use of official power, the President is now a king above the law." She argued that the majority created a "legal no-man’s zone" where a President could order a political rival assassinated or organize a military coup and be immune so long as the order was given as an "official act."
Justice Jackson: Wrote a separate dissent focusing on the "Accountability Gap." She argued that the Court’s new rule creates a "bespoke" system of justice for the President that ignores the fundamental principle that nobody is above the law.
Professor's Tip: When you outline this, remember that Nixon v. Fitzgerald (absolute civil immunity) was the springboard for this decision. The Court essentially took the civil immunity from Fitzgerald and "upgraded" it to the criminal context, which had never been done before.
1. The Youngstown Framework in Trump v. United States
Chief Justice Roberts ingeniously (or controversially, depending on who you ask) mapped the new criminal immunity doctrine onto Justice Jackson’s famous tripartite framework from Youngstown Sheet & Tube Co. v. Sawyer.
2. Impeachment: The Political Check (Art. II, § 4)
Impeachment is the Constitution’s "ultimate weapon," but its history shows it is more political than strictly legal.
The "High Crimes and Misdemeanors" Debate
A central 1L question: What does this phrase actually mean?
The Restricted View: Limited to indictable, statutory criminal offenses (Nixon’s defense).
The Broad View: Includes "abuses of official power" or "breaches of public trust," even if not technically criminal (House Judiciary Staff view).
The "Maladministration" Rejection: The Framers rejected "maladministration" (low bar) to ensure the President wasn't just a servant of the Senate's "pleasure."
Historical Precedents
Andrew Johnson (1868): Impeached for firing a cabinet member (Tenure of Office Act). Highlighted the tension over the President's removal power (later settled in Myers v. U.S.).
Richard Nixon (1974): Resigned before the full House vote. The Articles of Impeachment focused on "subversive" conduct and "willful disobedience" of subpoenas.
Bill Clinton (1998): Impeached for perjury/obstruction regarding private conduct (Clinton v. Jones). The debate was whether "private" misdeeds could constitute "High Crimes."
Donald Trump (2019 & 2021): * First: Abuse of power (Ukraine).
Second: Incitement of Insurrection (Jan 6). This was the first time a President was impeached for conduct related to a transition of power.
3. Disqualification: The 14th Amendment, Section 3
Section 3 (the "Insurrectionist Clause") was designed to keep former Confederates out of office. Its modern application was settled in Trump v. Anderson (2024).
Trump v. Anderson (2024)
The Issue: Can states (like Colorado) unilaterally remove a federal candidate from the ballot based on Section 3?
The Holding (Per Curiam):
States vs. Federal Power: States can disqualify people from state office. However, states have no power to enforce Section 3 against federal officeholders, especially the President.
The "Patchwork" Concern: If every state could decide who is an "insurrectionist," we would have a chaotic "patchwork" where a candidate is eligible in one state but not another for the same national office.
Section 5 Enforcement: The Court (specifically the majority) held that Section 3 is not "self-executing" for federal offices. Only Congress can enforce it by passing legislation under Section 5 of the 14th Amendment.
The Internal Court Conflict
The Concurrence (Sotomayor, Kagan, Jackson): They agreed Trump should be on the ballot but hated the majority's "Section 5" requirement. They argued the majority "shut the door" on other ways to enforce the Constitution (like federal lawsuits) that didn't require an Act of Congress.
The Originalist Critique: Professors Baude and Paulsen argue the Court ignored "originalism" by relying on Griffin’s Case (a non-precedential lower court opinion by Salmon Chase) to claim Section 3 isn't self-executing. They argue the text of the Constitution should be enough to disqualify someone without a new law from Congress.
4. Synthesis for Your Exam
If an exam question asks about removing or stopping a President:
For Criminal Acts in Office: Look to Trump v. United States (The Tripartite Immunity test).
For Private Acts/Prior Conduct: Look to Clinton v. Jones (No immunity).
For Removal from Office: Look to Impeachment (A political process for "High Crimes").
For Ballot Eligibility: Look to Trump v. Anderson (States cannot disqualify; only Congress can under Section 5).
WEEK 10
Barron v. Mayor and City Council of Baltimore 
32 U.S. (7 Pet.) 243, 8 L. Ed. 672 (1833). 
The Facts: 
John Barron owned a profitable wharf in Baltimore. The city started paving its streets, which redirected the flow of several streams. These streams carried large amounts of sand and earth into the water around Barron's wharf, making it too shallow for vessels to dock.
Barron sued, claiming the city violated the Fifth Amendment, specifically the Takings Clause, which says private property shall not be taken for public use without "just compensation."
Issue
Does the Fifth Amendment of the Constitution (and by extension, the Bill of Rights) apply to local and state governments, or is it limited solely to the federal government?
Rule
The provisions of the Bill of Rights are intended to protect the people against the powers of the General (Federal) Government, not against the actions of local or state authorities.
Analysis
Chief Justice John Marshall (the "G.O.A.T." of early Con Law) looked at the intent of the Framers. He argued that:
Constitutional Intent: The Constitution was established by the people of the United States for themselves—for their government (the Federal one)—not for the governments of the individual states.
Textual Evidence: Article I, Sections 9 and 10 of the Constitution already distinguish between what the Federal government can't do and what the States can't do. Since the Fifth Amendment doesn't explicitly mention "States," it must only apply to the United States government.
State Constitutions: If a state government infringes on its citizens' rights, the citizens must look to their own state's constitution for protection, not the federal one.
Conclusion
The Supreme Court ruled that it had no jurisdiction over the case because the Fifth Amendment did not apply to the City of Baltimore. Barron lost his wharf money, and the Bill of Rights remained a "Federal-only" club for several more decade
I. The "Invisible" Institution: Slavery in the Original Text
While the Framers avoided the word "slavery," the institution was woven into the constitutional fabric through three key clauses:
The Three-Fifths Clause (Art. I, § 2): Enslaved persons were counted as  of a person for representation and taxation. This gave Southern states disproportionate political power in the House.
The Slave Trade Clause (Art. I, § 9): Prohibited Congress from banning the international slave trade until 1808.
The Fugitive Slave Clause (Art. IV, § 2): Guaranteed that enslaved people could be recaptured and returned even if they escaped to "free" states.
II. The "Firebell in the Night": Legislative Failures
Before the courts stepped in, Congress tried (and failed) to solve the slavery crisis through three major political compromises:
1. The Missouri Compromise of 1820
The Deal: Missouri entered as a slave state; Maine entered as a free state.
The Line: Slavery was prohibited in territories north of the  parallel (Missouri's southern border).
Significance: This held the Union together for 30 years but created a rigid geographic divide.
2. The Compromise of 1850
The Shift: Introduced "Popular Sovereignty," allowing residents of a territory (like Utah) to vote on whether to allow slavery.
The Trade-off: California became a free state, but the North had to accept a much harsher Fugitive Slave Law, which forced Northern citizens to assist in slave catching.
3. The Kansas-Nebraska Act of 1854
The Result: It repealed the Missouri Compromise line and applied "popular sovereignty" to Kansas.
"Bleeding Kansas": This led to literal warfare between pro-slavery and anti-slavery settlers, proving that the political process was breaking down.
III. The Judicial "Solution"
The text highlights a crucial turning point: when the political branches (Congress and the President) failed to find a solution, they looked to the Supreme Court to end the debate.
President Buchanan’s Hope: He believed the Court could "finally settle" the issue in the Dred Scott case.
The Irony: Instead of settling the issue, the Court’s decision in Dred Scott (which you now know declared Black people could never be citizens and that Congress couldn't ban slavery in territories) acted as a catalyst for the Civil War.
Summary Table: Constitutional Evolution

Dred Scott v. Sandford 
.S. (19 How.) 393, 15 L. Ed. 691 (1857). 
The Facts
Dred Scott was an enslaved man born in Virginia. His owner, John Emerson, took Scott from Missouri (a slave state) to Illinois (a free state) and then to the Wisconsin Territory (where slavery was prohibited by the Missouri Compromise).
After Emerson died, Scott sued Emerson’s widow (and later her brother, John Sanford—misspelled "Sandford" in court records) for his freedom in Missouri state court. Scott argued that his residence in free territory made him a free man. After years of litigation in state and lower federal courts, the case reached the Supreme Court in 1856.
Issue
Jurisdiction/Standing: Can a person of African descent, whether enslaved or free, be a "citizen" of a state for purposes of Suing in federal court under Diversity Jurisdiction?
The Missouri Compromise: Did Congress have the authority to prohibit slavery in the territories?
Rule
People of African descent are not "citizens" under the Constitution and therefore have no standing to sue in federal court.
The Fifth Amendment protects the property rights of slaveholders; therefore, Congress cannot pass laws (like the Missouri Compromise) that deprive citizens of their "property" (enslaved people) simply for entering a federal territory.


  b Chief Justice Roger Taney’s majority opinion relied on a "frozen-in-time" originalist approach:
Original Intent: Taney argued that at the time the Constitution was drafted, Black people were considered "subordinate and inferior beings" and were not intended to be part of the "sovereign people" who created the Union.
Due Process (The "Taking" of Property): The Court held that an Act of Congress which deprives a citizen of his liberty or property merely because he came himself or brought his property into a particular Territory "could hardly be dignified with the name of due process of law."
Voiding the Compromise: Because the Constitution protected property, Congress had no power to ban slavery in the territories, rendering the Missouri Compromise unconstitutional.
Conclusion
The Court ruled that Scott remained a slave. Furthermore, it declared that no Black person could ever be a U.S. citizen. This decision effectively pushed the United States toward the Civil War.
I. The Post-Mortem of Dred Scott
Your text asks a profound question: What was the "principled" error in Dred Scott?
Judicial Overreach vs. Jurisdiction: In Marbury v. Madison, Marshall used a lack of jurisdiction to establish the power of Judicial Review. In Dred Scott, Taney used a lack of jurisdiction (finding Scott wasn't a citizen) but then kept going to rule on the merits (the Missouri Compromise). This is the definition of Dicta (statements not necessary to the legal conclusion) being used to set national policy.
The Failure of "Finality": Taney’s goal was to "settle" the slavery question to save the Union. This is a classic example of Judicial Hubris. Instead of settling the issue, the decision delegitimized the Court in the eyes of the North and accelerated the Civil War.
A "Slave Power" Conspiracy: Abraham Lincoln’s "House Divided" speech reframed the case not as a legal ruling, but as a political plot to make slavery national rather than local.
II. The Reconstruction Amendments (The "Second Founding")
After the war, the "Civil War Amendments" were passed to specifically overturn the world Dred Scott created.
Export to Sheets
III. The Slaughter-House Cases (1873): The Great Constriction
This is arguably the most important case you will read this semester that you will also hate. It effectively "gutted" the 14th Amendment's Privileges or Immunities Clause.
The Issue: Did a Louisiana-granted slaughterhouse monopoly violate the 14th Amendment rights of independent butchers?
The Holding (Justice Miller): No. The Court drew a sharp line between State Citizenship and National Citizenship.
State Privileges: These include almost all fundamental civil rights (property, trade, etc.). The 14th Amendment does not protect these from your own state government.
National Privileges: A tiny list of rights (access to seaports, subtreasuries, protection on the high seas).
The Result: The Privileges or Immunities Clause was rendered "mere surplusage" (useless). This is why, later in the semester, you will study "Substantive Due Process"—lawyers had to find a different clause to protect rights because this one was dead.
The Dissent (Justice Field): He argued that if the Clause only protected what was already protected, it was a "vain and idle enactment." He believed the 14th Amendment protected the "natural and inalienable rights" of all citizens.
IV. The "Wandering" Right to Travel
Because Slaughter-House closed the door on the Privileges or Immunities Clause, the "Right to Travel" had to wander through the Constitution looking for a home.
Commerce Clause: In Edwards v. California (the "Anti-Okie" law case), the Court used the Commerce Clause to say states can't stop poor people from moving in.
Equal Protection: In Shapiro v. Thompson, the Court struck down 1-year residency requirements for welfare, saying it "penalized" the fundamental right to travel.
The Resurrection (Saenz v. Roe): Finally, in 1999, the Court used the Privileges or Immunities Clause for the first time in a century to protect the right of a new citizen to be treated the same as an old citizen.
V. Summary of Residency Requirements
Your text provides a helpful "cheat sheet" for what states can and cannot do regarding new residents:
CANNOT Delay: Welfare benefits (Shapiro, Saenz), Voting (Dunn), Non-emergency medical care (Maricopa County).
CAN Delay: In-state tuition (Starns), Divorce court access (Sosna).
The Logic: If the delay deprives you of a "basic necessity of life" or a "fundamental right," it usually fails. If it's a "portable" benefit (like a divorce or a cheap degree), the state can make you wait to ensure you are a "bona fide" resident
Saenz v. Roe
S. 489, 119 S. Ct. 1518, 143 L. Ed. 2d 689 (1999).
The Facts: A Tiered Welfare System
In 1992, California was facing a budget crisis. To save money and discourage "welfare tourism," the state passed a law (Section 11450.03) that limited new residents' welfare benefits.
For their first 12 months in California, new residents would not receive the full California benefit (which was $641 for a family of three). Instead, they would receive the amount they were eligible for in their previous state. For example, a family moving from Mississippi would receive only $190. Brenda Roe, a mother who moved to California to escape an abusive relationship, challenged the law.
Issue
Does a state statute that limits the welfare benefits of new residents based on their prior state of residency violate the Fourteenth Amendment’s right to travel?
Rule
The right to travel is a fundamental right that includes three components:
The right to enter and leave another State;
The right to be treated as a welcome visitor while temporarily present;
For those who become permanent residents, the right to be treated like other citizens of that State.
Analysis
Justice Stevens, writing for the 7-2 majority, grounded the decision in the Privileges or Immunities Clause.
The "Third Component": The Court held that when a person moves to a state with the intent to reside there, they become a citizen of that state immediately.
Strict Scrutiny: Since this involves a fundamental right, the state must show a "compelling interest." California’s interest in saving $10 million (budgetary concerns) was not "compelling" enough to justify treating citizens like "second-class" residents based on how long they had lived there.
Congress Can't Help: Even though Congress had passed a federal law (PRWORA) allowing states to do this, the Court held that Congress cannot authorize a state to violate the 14th Amendment.
Conclusion
The Court struck down the California law. It affirmed that once you become a "bona fide" resident of a state, you are entitled to the same privileges and immunities as every other citizen of that state, regardless of your "seniority."
Chief Justice Rehnquist’s Dissent
Rehnquist argued that the "right to travel" does not inherently include the right to "immediate equal welfare benefits." He believed a one-year residency requirement was a reasonable way to ensure someone was a true resident and not just passing through to collect a check.
Justice Thomas’s Dissent
Justice Thomas wrote a fascinating dissent. He actually liked the idea of using the Privileges or Immunities Clause (he’s a fan of its history), but he argued that the majority was giving it a "new" meaning. He suggested the Court should re-examine the original 1873 cases (The Slaughter-House Cases) that originally killed the clause before using it to create new welfare rights.
I. The "New" Right to Travel: Saenz v. Roe vs. Shapiro
The text highlights a major shift in how the Court protects the right to move between states.
Methodological Shift: * Old Way (Shapiro): Used Equal Protection. It asked if a benefit (like welfare) was a "necessity of life." If it was, delaying it "penalized" the right to travel.
New Way (Saenz): Uses the Citizenship Clause and Privileges or Immunities Clause. It doesn't care if the benefit is "important." It simply says: A state cannot have two tiers of citizens based on length of residence.
Structure vs. Liberty: Saenz is about Federalism. The Court views the Union as a collection of equal states where a citizen of the U.S. becomes a citizen of a State the moment they move there.
The "Portable Benefit" Exception: States can still have residency requirements for:
In-state tuition (Starns)
Divorce jurisdiction (Sosna)
Reasoning: These are "portable." You could move to a state, get a cheap degree or a quick divorce, and then leave. Welfare, conversely, is consumed where you live.
II. The Doctrine of Incorporation: Three Competing Schools
How does the Bill of Rights (Amendments 1–8) apply to the States? Your text outlines three historical battles:
1. Fundamental Fairness (The "Flexible" Approach)
Proponents: Justices Cardozo (Palko) and Frankfurter (Adamson).
The Rule: The 14th Amendment’s "Due Process" clause doesn't automatically include the Bill of Rights. Instead, it protects rights that are "of the very essence of a scheme of ordered liberty" or "rooted in the traditions and conscience of our people."
The Result: A right like "Free Speech" is included because you can't have a fair society without it. But a "Jury Trial" or "Grand Jury" could be abolished by a state, and the system could still be "fair."
2. Total Incorporation (The "Literal" Approach)
Proponent: Justice Black (Adamson dissent).
The Rule: The 14th Amendment was intended to apply all of the first eight Amendments to the states—nothing more, nothing less.
Black’s Logic: He feared "Fundamental Fairness" was too subjective. He didn't want judges deciding what was "civilized" or "decent." He wanted the specific text of the Bill of Rights to be the yardstick.
3. Selective Incorporation (The Winner)
The Outcome: This is the current law. The Court never accepted "Total Incorporation," but over time, it "selectively" found almost every right in the Bill of Rights to be "fundamental."
Today: Nearly everything is incorporated (Speech, Religion, Guns, Jury Trials) except:
The 5th Amendment right to a Grand Jury indictment.
The 7th Amendment right to a Jury Trial in civil cases.
III. The Great Debate: Fairness vs. Specificity
Your text poses a classic 1L "Policy Question": Which approach is better?
Export to Sheets
IV. Summary of Key Precedents
Murray’s Lessee (1856): Defined Due Process as "the law of the land" (Magna Carta).
Twining v. New Jersey (1908): First suggested that some Bill of Rights might be in the 14th Amendment—but because they are "fundamental," not because they are in the Bill of Rights.
Palko v. Connecticut (1937): Established that Double Jeopardy was not fundamental enough to apply to states (this was later overruled).
Adamson v. California (1947): Held that the privilege against self-incrimination was not applicable to the states (this was also later overruled).
Duncan v. Louisiana
391 U.S. 145, 88 S. Ct. 1444, 20 L. Ed. 2d 491 (1968).
The Facts: A Slap and a Sentence
Gary Duncan, a 19-year-old Black man in Louisiana, was driving when he saw his two cousins (who were transferring to a previously all-white school) being confronted by a group of white boys. Duncan pulled over to intervene and get his cousins into his car.
Witnesses claimed Duncan "slapped" one of the white boys on the elbow. Duncan was arrested and charged with simple battery, a misdemeanor in Louisiana punishable by a maximum of two years in prison and a fine. Duncan requested a jury trial, but the Louisiana Constitution only granted jury trials in cases where capital punishment or imprisonment at hard labor could be imposed.
The judge denied his request, found him guilty in a bench trial, and sentenced him to 60 days in prison and a fine of $150. Duncan appealed, arguing his Sixth Amendment right to a jury trial was violated.
Issue
Does the Sixth Amendment right to a jury trial in criminal cases apply to the states through the Due Process Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment?
Rule
A right protected by the Bill of Rights is "incorporated" (applied) against the states if it is "fundamental to the American scheme of justice" or "essential to a fair trial."
Analysis
Justice White, writing for the 7-2 majority, shifted the Court's analytical framework:
The History of Liberty: The Court looked at the history of the jury trial, tracing it back to the Magna Carta. They determined that a jury is not just a procedural relic; it is a vital safeguard against "the compliant, biased, or eccentric judge."
Selective Incorporation: The Court held that for a right to be incorporated, it doesn't have to be a "universal" human right, but rather one that is "fundamental" to our specific American system.
Serious vs. Petty Offenses: The Court clarified that while "petty offenses" (usually those with a maximum sentence of six months or less) do not require a jury, a crime punishable by two years is a serious offense, regardless of the actual sentence the judge handed down.
Conclusion
The Supreme Court reversed the lower court's decision. The Sixth Amendment right to a jury trial is incorporated against the states. Louisiana was required to grant Duncan a jury trial.
Justice Black’s Concurrence
Justice Black argued for "Total Incorporation." He believed the 14th Amendment was intended to make every singleright in the Bill of Rights applicable to the states. He felt "Selective Incorporation" gave judges too much power to pick and choose which rights they liked.
Justice Harlan’s Dissent
Justice Harlan was a "Federalism" hawk. He argued that the 14th Amendment only required "fundamental fairness." He believed states should be "laboratories of democracy" and be allowed to experiment with different trial procedures (like bench trials for minor crimes) as long as the process wasn't inherently unfair.
I. The "Jot-for-Jot" Debate: Scope of Incorporation
Once the Court decides a right (like the 6th Amendment jury trial) applies to the states, a new question arises: Does it apply exactly the same way it does to the Feds?
The Frankfurter/Harlan View (Rejected): They argued for "partial" incorporation. They believed the "core" of a right should apply to states, but states should have flexibility on the details (e.g., states must provide a jury, but maybe not a 12-person one).
The Modern "Jot-for-Jot" Rule: The Court eventually rejected flexibility. Under cases like Mapp v. Ohio, once a right is incorporated, it applies to the states with the same "contours" and "details" as it does to the Federal government.
The "Dilution" Warning: Justice Harlan warned that if we force states to follow every federal detail, the Court might "dilute" the federal right to make it easier for states to comply.
Example: In Williams v. Florida, the Court allowed 6-person juries for states. To keep things "jot-for-jot," they had to rule that 12-person juries aren't required for the Feds either.
II. The "Incorporation Status" Checklist
As of your current reading, here is the breakdown of what is and is not incorporated:
What IS Incorporated (Applies to States):
1st Amendment: Speech, Press, Assembly, Petition, Free Exercise, and Establishment Clause.
2nd Amendment: Right to bear arms (McDonald).
4th Amendment: Search and Seizure, Warrant requirements, and the Exclusionary Rule.
5th Amendment: Double Jeopardy, Self-Incrimination, and Just Compensation (Takings).
6th Amendment: Speedy/Public trial, Jury trial, Confrontation of witnesses, and Right to Counsel.
8th Amendment: Cruel and Unusual Punishment, and Excessive Fines (Timbs v. Indiana, 2019).
What is NOT Incorporated (Does NOT apply to States):
3rd Amendment: Quartering of soldiers (rarely litigated).
5th Amendment: The requirement for a Grand Jury Indictment (Hurtado). States can charge you via a "Preliminary Hearing" instead.
7th Amendment: The right to a jury trial in civil cases involving more than $20.
III. The Second Amendment Revolution: Heller
Before Heller, the Second Amendment was largely seen as a "collective right" related to militias (U.S. v. Miller). Justice Scalia changed that by splitting the Amendment into two parts:
The Prefatory Clause: "A well regulated Militia..." Scalia argued this just states a purpose; it doesn't limit the right.
The Operative Clause: "...the right of the people to keep and bear Arms, shall not be infringed." Scalia argued "the people" always refers to an individual right in the Constitution.
The Scalia Holding (Majority):
The 2nd Amendment protects an individual right to keep and bear arms for self-defense, specifically handguns in the home.
Limits: The right is not absolute. The government can still ban guns for felons, the mentally ill, or in "sensitive places" (schools/govt buildings).
The Dissents:
Justice Stevens: Argued the history shows the Amendment was only about protecting state militias from federal interference, not private gun ownership for hunting or self-defense.
Justice Breyer: Argued even if there is an individual right, the Court should use an interest-balancing test. He believed D.C.'s interest in reducing urban crime outweighed the individual's right to a handgun.
IV. The Vehicle for Incorporation: A New Consensus?
While the Court traditionally uses the Due Process Clause of the 14th Amendment to incorporate rights, a new "originalist" consensus is forming:
Justice Thomas: Has long argued that the Privileges or Immunities Clause is the correct historical vehicle.
Justice Gorsuch: In Timbs (2019), he joined Thomas in suggesting that Privileges or Immunities might be the better legal home for these rights.
McDonald v. City of Chicago
561 U.S. 742, 130 S. Ct. 3020, 177 L. Ed. 2d 894 (2010).
The Facts: A Neighborhood in Transition
Otis McDonald was a 76-year-old retired maintenance worker living in a high-crime neighborhood in Chicago. He had been a victim of multiple burglaries and threats. He wanted to own a handgun for self-defense within his home.
However, Chicago had a city ordinance that effectively banned the registration of handguns, making it nearly impossible for a private citizen to legally possess one. McDonald sued the city, arguing that the Second Amendment should apply to the states via the Fourteenth Amendment, thereby invalidating Chicago’s ban.
Issue
Does the Second Amendment right to keep and bear arms for self-defense apply to state and local governments through the Fourteenth Amendment?
Rule
A Bill of Rights guarantee applies to the states if it is "fundamental to our scheme of ordered liberty" or "deeply rooted in this Nation’s history and tradition."
Analysis
Justice Alito, writing for a 5-4 majority, applied the "Selective Incorporation" test established in Duncan:
Historical Significance: The Court looked at the post-Civil War era and the drafting of the 14th Amendment. They found that the Framers of that Amendment specifically intended to protect the right of newly freed slaves to own firearms for self-defense against groups like the KKK.
Self-Defense as Core: Building on Heller, the Court held that individual self-defense is the "central component" of the Second Amendment right.
Due Process vs. Privileges or Immunities: While four justices (Alito, Roberts, Scalia, Kennedy) used the Due Process Clause to incorporate the right, Justice Thomas (concurring) argued it should be done through the Privileges or Immunities Clause (remember his dissent in Saenz?).
Conclusion
The Supreme Court reversed the lower court. The Second Amendment is incorporated against the states. Chicago’s handgun ban was effectively struck down as unconstitutional.
Important Dissenting & Concurring Notes
Justice Thomas’s Concurrence
Thomas agreed with the result but refused to use "Substantive Due Process," which he considers a "legal fiction." He wrote a long historical treatise arguing that the Privileges or Immunities Clause was the original, intended vehicle for applying the Bill of Rights to the states.
Justice Stevens’s Dissent
Stevens argued that the right to own a handgun is not a "fundamental" liberty interest. He believed that unlike freedom of speech or trial by jury, firearm ownership is uniquely dangerous to others and should be left to state and local regulation.
Justice Breyer’s Dissent
Breyer focused on judicial restraint. He argued that there is nothing in the Constitution's text or history that provides a clear answer, and therefore, the Court should defer to the "democratically elected" local officials who are trying to solve the problem of urban gun violence.
I. Competing Historical Narratives of the Second Amendment
The Court in Heller and McDonald didn't just look at law; they looked at "The American Story."
1. The "Liberty" Narrative (Scalia/Thomas)
English Roots: Traces the right back to the 1689 Declaration of Right, where Protestant subjects sought arms to prevent disarmament by a tyrannical monarch.
The Frontier Ethos: Viewed guns as a "practical necessity" for survival, hunting, and protection during westward expansion.
The Equalizer: Samuel Colt’s famous adage—"God made men, but Colt made them equal"—suggests that firearms are essential to individual dignity and the defense of liberty.
2. The "Regulation" Narrative (Breyer Dissent)
Colonial Restrictions: Cities like Boston historically prohibited discharging firearms within city limits for safety reasons.
The Police Power: States have a long-standing "Police Power" to protect the health and safety of their citizens, which included banning concealed pistols or restricted weapons long before the modern era.
Consensus on Limits: Even the majority admits that the government can prohibit possession by felons and the mentally ill, proving the right has never been absolute.
II. The Second Amendment and Race
This is a critical area for 1L exams. The textbook introduces a complex "double-edged sword" regarding the Second Amendment and African Americans.
1. Incorporation as a Shield
Justice Thomas and scholar Carol Anderson highlight that the 14th Amendment’s Framers specifically wanted to protect the rights of newly freed slaves.
Post-Civil War: White mobs (like in the Hamburg Massacre of 1876) used disarmament as a tool of terror.
Self-Defense: Groups like the Deacons for Defense and Justice used their Second Amendment rights in the 1960s to protect civil rights workers from white supremacist violence.
The Cruikshank Failure: The 1875 Cruikshank decision (which held the 2nd Amendment didn't apply to states) essentially told white supremacists they could disarm and slay Black citizens without federal interference.
2. Incorporation as a Threat
Conversely, Carol Anderson argues that modern Second Amendment jurisprudence can harm the Black community:
Disproportionate Impact: Laws stripping felons of gun rights hit Black men harder due to systemic racial inequalities in the criminal justice system.
Gun Violence: The Black community often bears the brunt of the gun violence that expanded gun rights may enable.
III. Statistics: Firearms and Demographics (2020-2022 Context)
To understand the "Safety vs. Right" debate mentioned in your text, it helps to look at the concrete numbers that the Justices often weigh implicitly.
Export to Sheets
IV. The Move to "History and Tradition"
The most significant "legal" takeaway from your text is the rejection of Means-End Scrutiny.
What was rejected: Most 1Ls learn "tiers of scrutiny" (Rational Basis, Intermediate, Strict). Lower courts used to use these to balance "public safety" vs. "gun rights."
The New Rule (Bruen): The Court moved away from balancing. They now use a "History and Tradition" test.
If the text covers the conduct, the government must prove the regulation is consistent with the historical tradition of firearm regulation from 1791 or 1868.
New York State Rifle & Pistol Association, Inc. v. Bruen
597 U.S. 1, 142 S. Ct. 2111, 213 L. Ed. 2d 387 (2022).
The Facts: "May-Issue" vs. "Shall-Issue"
New York State law required any person seeking a license to carry a concealed handgun in public to demonstrate "proper cause"—a special need for self-protection distinguishable from that of the general community.
Two individuals, Robert Nash and Brandon Koch, applied for unrestricted licenses. Both were denied because they failed to prove a "special need" beyond a general desire for self-defense. They sued Kevin Bruen, the Superintendent of the New York State Police, claiming the "proper cause" requirement violated their Second and Fourteenth Amendment rights.
Issue
Does the New York "proper cause" requirement for obtaining a concealed-carry license violate the Second Amendment’s right to keep and bear arms for self-defense?
Rule
When the Second Amendment’s plain text covers an individual’s conduct, the Constitution presumptively protects that conduct. To justify a regulation, the government must demonstrate that the regulation is consistent with this Nation’s historical tradition of firearm regulation.
Analysis
Justice Thomas, writing for the 6-3 majority, introduced a new "Text, History, and Tradition" test:
The Death of Intermediate Scrutiny: The Court rejected the "two-step" framework used by lower courts (which balanced the government's interest in public safety against the individual's right). Thomas argued that the Second Amendment is not a "second-class right" subject to a mere interest-balancing inquiry.
Public Carry is Protected: The Court held that the "right to bear arms" naturally includes the right to carry them outside the home for self-defense.
Historical Analogues: New York failed to provide historical evidence from 1791 (when the 2nd Amendment was ratified) or 1868 (when the 14th was ratified) of a similar broad prohibition on law-abiding citizens carrying arms in public. Since no such historical tradition existed, the law was unconstitutional.
Conclusion
The Supreme Court reversed the lower court's decision. New York's "proper cause" requirement was struck down. States can still require licenses ("shall-issue" regimes), but they cannot grant officials the discretion to deny a license to a law-abiding citizen based on a subjective "need."
Important Dissenting & Concurring Notes
Justice Alito’s Concurrence
Alito took aim at the dissent's focus on modern gun violence. He argued that the Second Amendment is precisely for the purpose of self-defense against such violence, and that the Court's job is to interpret the Constitution, not to act as a legislature solving social problems.
