Wiz Tip for the Final
If you have a case involving a "New Right" (e.g., a right to bio-hack your body or a right to a specific medical procedure):
Cite Glucksberg: Perform a "History and Tradition" search. Was this right protected in 1868? (Usually No).
The "Dignity" Counter-Move: Cite the Lawrence/Obergefell "Synergy." Argue that the right is part of a broader "personal relationship" or "autonomy" that the Court has recognized more recently.
The Dobbs Reality Check: Mention that Dobbs has re-installed Glucksberg as the "king" of the methodology, making it much harder to win these cases today.
ce vs. Casey
Justice Scalia accused the majority of being hypocrites regarding Stare Decisis (the rule of following precedent).
The Comparison: In Casey, the Court refused to overrule Roe v. Wade because of "reliance interests." In Lawrence, they overruled Bowers after only 17 years.
The Distinction: Kennedy argued Bowers had not induced "detrimental reliance" (no one's life was built around the legality of sodomy bans) and that Bowers itself had become an outlier that was "not correct when it was decided."
5. Comparative Constitutional Law
Lawrence is the first time a Supreme Court majority cited foreign law (The European Court of Human Rights case Dudgeon v. UK) to interpret the U.S. Constitution.
The Justification: Kennedy argued that "Western civilization" generally shared the view that these laws were wrong, suggesting the U.S. shouldn't be a global outlier.
The Backlash: Justice Scalia was furious, arguing that "foreign moods, fads, or fashions" should have no place in American constitutional interpretation.
6. The Road to Marriage: Scalia’s Prediction
Justice Scalia’s dissent was a roadmap for the next decade of civil rights litigation.
The "End of Morals Legislation": Scalia argued that if "moral disapproval" isn't a legitimate state interest, then laws against bigamy, incest, and same-sex marriage were all in danger.
The Impact: Just five months later, Massachusetts cited Lawrence to legalize same-sex marriage in Goodridge. This eventually led to the invalidation of DOMA (the Defense of Marriage Act) and the final victory in Obergefell.
Wiz Tip for the Final
When you see a Substantive Due Process question, use the "Lawrence Framework":
Define the right broadly: Don't talk about the specific act; talk about "dignity," "autonomy," and "personal relationships."
Attack the state's interest: If the only reason for the law is "tradition" or "morality," argue that under Lawrence, that is not a legitimate interest sufficient to justify an intrusion into private life.
The "Stigma" Argument: Mention that laws criminalizing private conduct create a "stigma" that the Equal Protection and Due Process Clauses are designed to prevent.
United States v. Windsor
570 U.S. 744, 133 S. Ct. 2675, 186 L. Ed. 2d 808 (2013).
The Facts
Edith Windsor and Thea Spyer were a same-sex couple who lived in New York and were legally married in Ontario, Canada, in 2007. New York recognized their marriage as valid. When Spyer died in 2009, she left her entire estate to Windsor.
Under federal law, a surviving spouse typically qualifies for an unlimited estate tax marital deduction, meaning they pay no federal estate tax on the inheritance. However, Section 3 of the Defense of Marriage Act (DOMA) defined "marriage" and "spouse" for all federal purposes as excluding same-sex partners.
Because of DOMA, the IRS refused to recognize Windsor as a "surviving spouse." She was forced to pay $363,053 in estate taxes. She sued, arguing that Section 3 of DOMA violated the equal protection principles of the 5th Amendment.
Issue
Does Section 3 of the Defense of Marriage Act (DOMA), which defines marriage as a union between one man and one woman for federal purposes, violate the Equal Protection component of the 5th Amendment Due Process Clause?
Rule
The federal government cannot tip the scales of Federalism to create a "second-class" status for marriages that are validly recognized by a State. When the "primary purpose" of a federal law is to impose a disadvantage and a "stigma" on a class of persons, it violates the Equal Protection and Due Process guarantees of the 5th Amendment.
Application
Justice Kennedy, writing for the 5-4 majority, used a unique blend of Federalism and Liberty:
State Sovereignty: Historically, the power to define "marriage" belongs to the States, not the Federal Government. By creating a federal definition that contradicted New York's definition, DOMA interfered with a traditional state power.
The "Animus" Finding: Citing Romer v. Evans, Kennedy found that the "clear purpose" of DOMA was to "disparage and injure" those whom the State, through its marriage laws, sought to protect.
The "Second-Class" Marriage: The Court held that DOMA wrote "inequality into the entire United States Code." It treated same-sex marriages as "less worthy" than others, which harmed the "dignity" of the individuals and the financial stability of their families.
Conclusion
Yes. Section 3 of DOMA is unconstitutional. The Court held that the federal government must recognize same-sex marriages that are legally performed and recognized under state law.
Wiz Tip: The "5th Amendment" Nuance
On your exam, remember that the 14th Amendment’s Equal Protection Clause applies only to States. Because this was a challenge against a Federal law (DOMA), the Court had to use the 5th Amendment.
The Logic: Even though the 5th Amendment doesn’t have an "Equal Protection Clause" in its text, the Supreme Court has "read" one into the Due Process Clause (this is called Reverse Incorporation via Bolling v. Sharpe).
Justice Scalia’s Dissent: Scalia was again the "prophet" of the dissent. He argued that the majority's logic (state sovereignty + dignity) would inevitably lead the Court to force every state to allow same-sex marriage. He was right—two years later, the Court decided Obergefell v. Hodges.
The Meaning and Implications of Windsor
1. The Rationale: Why Not "Equal Protection" Alone?
Justice Kennedy’s opinion is famously "murky" regarding the specific tier of scrutiny.
The Animus Shortcut: The Court could have simply used the Moreno/Romer "animus" rule (a bare desire to harm is not a legitimate interest). However, by including Federalism, Kennedy added a layer of "State Sovereignty" that made DOMA look even more radical—it wasn't just mean; it was a federal intrusion into a traditional state power.
The Dignity Hybrid: The notes suggest that "Dignity" might be a new constitutional principle that merges Due Process and Equal Protection. If a law deprives you of dignity, it is infringing on your liberty (to be a whole person) and your equality (to be treated like others). This hybrid approach bypasses the "tiers of scrutiny" entirely.
2. Differing Conceptions of Dignity
The transition from Lawrence to Windsor shows an evolution in how the Court views the source of your rights:
Lawrence (Inherent Dignity): Dignity is intrinsic. You are born with the right to be free from state interference in your bedroom. The state invades this pre-existing dignity.
Windsor (Conferred Dignity): Dignity is a status. New York gave Edith Windsor the status of "Spouse." DOMA then took it away. Here, the dignity interest is created by the state's recognition of the relationship.
The Question for Obergefell: If dignity is conferred by the state (Windsor), can a state choose not to confer it? Or is it inherent (Lawrence), meaning the state must recognize it?
3. The Role of Federalism
Windsor is a "Federalism" case disguised as a "Civil Rights" case.
Traditional State Functions: Domestic relations (marriage, divorce, custody) have been the exclusive province of the states for over 200 years. Federal courts usually even refuse to hear these cases (the "domestic relations exception" to diversity jurisdiction).
The "Unusual" Nature of DOMA: Because the federal government reached into a state-regulated area specifically to "injure" a class the state wanted to protect, the Court viewed it with heightened suspicion.
The Line-Drawing Problem: If the "traditional" nature of the law matters, what about federal tort reform or insurance regulations? Does Windsor mean the federal government can't have any uniform definitions if they conflict with state law?
4. The "Circuit Split" and the Road to Obergefell
After Windsor, the "dominoes" began to fall, but not in a straight line:
The Pro-Marriage Wave: The 4th, 7th, 9th, and 10th Circuits all used Windsor and Lawrence to strike down state bans on same-sex marriage.
The 6th Circuit Dissent: The 6th Circuit (covering MI, KY, OH, and TN) broke the trend, ruling that Windsor was only about federal overreach and that states still had the right to define marriage as they saw fit.
The Result: This created a "Circuit Split," which is the most common reason the Supreme Court agrees to hear a case. This set the stage for Obergefell v. Hodges (2015).
Wiz Tip for the Final
If you get a Windsor-style problem (where a higher level of government overrides a lower level's civil rights protection):
Check for Animus: Is the primary purpose to "disparage" or "stigma"? (Romer/Windsor).
Check for Tradition: Is the higher government intruding on a "traditional" area of local law (like marriage or property)?
The "Dignity" Argument: Use the phrase "Double Helix." Argue that the law doesn't just treat people differently; it treats their legal status as "second-class," which violates both Liberty (5th/14th DP) and Equality (5th/14th EP).
Obergefell v. Hodges
576 U.S. 644, 135 S. Ct. 2584, 192 L. Ed. 2d 609 (2015).
The Facts
Groups of same-sex couples from Ohio, Michigan, Kentucky, and Tennessee sued their relevant state agencies, challenging the constitutionality of those states' bans on same-sex marriage or their refusal to recognize legal same-sex marriages performed in other states.
The lead plaintiff, James Obergefell, had married his terminally ill partner, John Arthur, in Maryland (where it was legal). When they returned to their home in Ohio, the state refused to list Obergefell as the "surviving spouse" on Arthur’s death certificate.
While several federal appeals courts had struck down such bans following Windsor, the Sixth Circuit Court of Appeals upheld them, creating a "circuit split" that forced the Supreme Court to intervene.
Issue
Does the 14th Amendment require a State to license a marriage between two people of the same sex?
Does the 14th Amendment require a State to recognize a marriage between two people of the same sex when their marriage was lawfully licensed and performed out-of-state?
Rule
The right to marry is a fundamental right protected by the Due Process Clause and the Equal Protection Clause of the 14th Amendment. Under Substantive Due Process, the state cannot exclude same-sex couples from civil marriage on the same terms and conditions as opposite-sex couples because marriage is essential to individual liberty and dignity.
Application
Justice Kennedy, writing for the 5-4 majority, identified four distinct "principles and traditions" that make marriage a fundamental right:
Individual Autonomy: The right to personal choice regarding marriage is inherent in the concept of individual autonomy.
Unique Union: Marriage supports a two-person union unlike any other in its importance to the committed individuals.
Safeguarding Children: Marriage provides a permanent home and legal recognition for children, protecting them from the "stigma" of being raised by "lesser" families.
Social Order: Marriage is a "keystone of our social order," and there is no difference between same-sex and opposite-sex couples with respect to this principle.
The Synergistic Approach: Kennedy explicitly linked the two clauses. He argued that while the Due Process Clause protects the liberty of the individual to marry, the Equal Protection Clause ensures that the state cannot grant that liberty to some while denying it to others based on a trait like sexual orientation.
Conclusion
Yes on both counts. The Court held that same-sex couples may exercise the fundamental right to marry in all 50 states and that there is no lawful basis for a State to refuse to recognize a lawful same-sex marriage performed in another State.
Wiz Tip: The "Scrutiny" Silence
Just like in Lawrence and Windsor, Justice Kennedy never explicitly states whether he is using "Strict Scrutiny."
1L Exam Strategy: In your outline, categorize Obergefell as a Fundamental Rights case. Generally, when a law burdens a fundamental right (like marriage), Strict Scrutiny is the default. However, Kennedy’s "Dignity" framework is often seen as a "New Substantive Due Process" that focuses on the nature of the right rather than a rigid "tiered" test.
The Dissents: Chief Justice Roberts wrote the primary dissent, arguing that the Constitution does not define marriage and that the Court was overstepping its "judicial" role to act as a "legislature." He famously told the victors: "Celebrate your triumph... but do not celebrate the Constitution. It had nothing to do with it."
Doesn’t say strict scrutiny but it is- for a state to stop they need a compelling state interest without bring able to vindicate the interest. Lots of people who don’t agree. 
The Meaning and Implications of Obergefell
1. The "Double Helix": Synergy of Due Process & Equal Protection
The Court in Obergefell explicitly tied the two clauses together, creating a "synergistic" effect.
The "Gap-Filling" Function: * Equal Protection (EP) usually asks: Is the group being treated differently? * Due Process (DP) asks: Is the right being taken away? * The Synergy: When a law treats a group differently in a way that demeans their dignity, both clauses are violated simultaneously. The DP element ensures the state cannot simply "level down" by taking the right away from everyone (e.g., a state cannot respond to Obergefell by abolishing marriage entirely for both straight and gay couples).
The Yoshino Thesis: Professor Kenji Yoshino argues that using DP compels a state to expand a right rather than contract it. If the Court had used only EP, a state like Alabama could have theoretically stopped issuing all marriage licenses to avoid "discriminating." By calling it a "Fundamental Right" under DP, the state must provide it to everyone.
2. Dignity as a Source of Rights
Obergefell moved away from the "Glucksberg Test" (which said Substantive Due Process only protects rights "deeply rooted in history and tradition").
The "Emerging Awareness" Model: The Court held that "history and tradition guide and discipline this inquiry but do not set its outer boundaries." Rights can be recognized based on an emerging consensus or a better understanding of "injustice that the previous generation saw as necessary."
The "Lochner" Fear: The dissenters (Scalia and Roberts) argued this is "judicial activism" or a return to the Lochner Era, where judges used their own moral philosophy to strike down laws. They asked: If "dignity" is the test, what stops a judge from legalizing polygamy or any other arrangement they find "dignified"?
The Limiting Principle: Justice Kennedy suggested that same-sex marriage is different from something like polygamy because same-sex marriage is a two-person union that fits the existing "social order" and "keystone" of society, whereas other arrangements might involve different social costs or "subordination."
3. The Court as a "Moral Educator"
This is a controversial view of the Supreme Court's role.
The "Teaching" Effect: Justice Kennedy argued that by excluding gay people from marriage, the law was teaching the public that they are "unequal." Therefore, the Court has a duty to change the law to stop that "lesson."
Adjudication vs. Education: Critics argue that judges are only supposed to settle "cases and controversies," not try to change public opinion.
The "Backlash" Thesis (Klarman): Some scholars argue that when the Court moves faster than the public (like in Roe v. Wade), it creates a political backlash that hurts the cause. Others argue that Lawrence and Windsor actually helped shift public opinion faster by normalizing the conversation.
The judges cannot stop marrying gay people because of their religious beliefs.
4. Standard of Review: The "Missing" Scrutiny
In your 1L exams, you will notice that Kennedy almost never uses the "Tiers of Scrutiny."
Dignity is the Scrutiny: Instead of checking if a law is "Narrowly Tailored" (Strict Scrutiny) or "Substantially Related" (Intermediate Scrutiny), Kennedy asks if the law demeans or stigmatizes.
Exam Strategy: If a fact pattern involves a "Dignity" interest:
Argue that Strict Scrutiny should apply because it's a "Fundamental Right" (Loving/Skinner).
Argue that even if it's not Strict Scrutiny, the law fails "Rational Basis with Bite" because "Moral Disapproval" or "Animus" is not a legitimate state interest (Romer/Lawrence).
Wiz Summary Table: Comparing the Clauses
Substantive Due Process and Rights over the Timing and Circumstances of One’s Death
The Core Holding: Refusal vs. Suicide
The Court finally addressed whether "Liberty" includes the right to die. The result was a nuanced "Yes, but..."
The Protected Interest: The Court assumed (but didn't explicitly rule) that a competent person has a constitutionally protected liberty interest in refusing unwanted medical treatment, including lifesaving nutrition and hydration.
The Procedural Limit: The Court held that a State (Missouri) may require "clear and convincing evidence" of an incompetent patient’s prior wishes before allowing a surrogate to pull the plug.
More than a preponderance but less than a reasonable doubt- if I had 60% of the proof Im good 
Preponderance is 51%
The Rationale: Because death is an "irrevocable" decision, the State has a legitimate interest in the preservation of life and may protect against potential abuses by family members or mistakes by doctors by setting a high evidentiary bar.
2. Competing Judicial Philosophies
The Cruzan concurrences and dissents provide the "policy arguments" you'll use for any Substantive Due Process essay.
Rehnquist (Majority): Balancing Test. He didn't use "Strict Scrutiny." Instead, he balanced the individual's liberty against the State's interest in life. Since an incompetent person can't "choose," the State's interest in preventing an erroneous death is at its peak.
Scalia (Concurrence): The "Originalist/Deferential" View. Scalia argued the federal courts have no business in this field. To him, there is no difference between "starving oneself to death" and "suicide," and since the Constitution is silent on the matter, the people of Missouri should decide the rules, not nine judges.
O’Connor (Concurrence): The "Surrogate" Warning. She agreed with the majority but hinted that in the future, the Court might be required to honor a "Health Care Proxy" or designated surrogate as a matter of Due Process.
Brennan (Dissent): The "Dignity" View. He argued that the right to avoid unwanted medical intrusion is fundamental. He believed the State has no interest in a life "abstracted from the person living that life."

3. The "Spectrum of Death" (Crucial for Exams)
Your notes highlight four distinct legal categories. You must be able to distinguish these for any fact pattern:
4. Strategic Note: Bodily Integrity vs. Decisional Autonomy
The Court’s reasoning often hinges on why the right exists:
Bodily Integrity: The right to not have someone "touch" you or pump fluids into you against your will. This is a very strong, traditional legal right (Common Law Battery).
Decisional Autonomy: The right to control the timing and manner of your death. This is a "newer" Substantive Due Process right (like the right to choose an intimate partner) and is generally seen as weaker by the current Court.
State interest:
Preserving life
Preventing suicide
Preventing abuse of vulnerable poor and elderly populations 
Protecting the role and reputation of the medical profession; and 
Preventing a slippery slope to voluntary or involuntary euthanasia. 
Wiz Tip for the Final
If you see a case about a patient in a coma or a terminally ill patient wanting to end their life:
Check Competency: If the patient is competent, their liberty interest is high.
Check Evidence: If they are incompetent, tell the professor that the State can demand "clear and convincing evidence" under Cruzan.
Check the Method: Is the doctor removing a tube (Passive - Cruzan) or providing a pill (Active - Glucksberg)? The Court is much more likely to protect the removal of treatment than the provision of a death-inducing substance.
Washington v. Glucksberg
521 U.S. 702, 117 S. Ct. 2258, 138 L. Ed. 2d 772 (1997).
The Facts
In the State of Washington, it was a felony to knowingly cause or aid another person to attempt suicide. Four physicians (including Dr. Harold Glucksberg), three terminally ill patients, and a non-profit organization challenged this ban.
They argued that terminally ill, mentally competent adults have a liberty interest under the 14th Amendment's Due Process Clause in a "threshold of death" that includes the right to a physician’s assistance in hastening that death. They relied heavily on the "autonomy" language from Planned Parenthood v. Casey, which described the right to define one's own concept of existence.
The Ninth Circuit Court of Appeals agreed with the plaintiffs, using a balancing test to find the ban unconstitutional as applied to the terminally ill. The State of Washington appealed to the Supreme Court.
Issue
Does the Washington statute prohibiting "aided suicide" violate the Due Process Clause of the 14th Amendment by infringing upon a purported fundamental liberty interest of a competent, terminally ill adult to commit physician-assisted suicide?
Rule
The Due Process Clause protects only those fundamental rights which are:
Objectively, deeply rooted in this Nation’s history and tradition; and
Implicit in the concept of ordered liberty, such that neither liberty nor justice would exist if they were sacrificed.
Additionally, a "careful description" of the asserted fundamental liberty interest is required. If a right is not fundamental, the law need only satisfy Rational Basis Review (rationally related to a legitimate government interest).
Application
Chief Justice Rehnquist, writing for the Court, applied a strict historical lens to the claim:
History and Tradition: The Court found that for over 700 years, Anglo-American common law has punished or disapproved of assisting suicide. There was no historical support for a "right to die" with assistance.
Defining the Right: The Court rejected the plaintiffs' broad "right to self-sovereignty." Instead, they defined the right narrowly as a "right to commit suicide with another's assistance."
Legitimate State Interests: Since the right was not "fundamental," Washington only needed a "rational basis." The Court identified several:
Preserving human life.
Protecting the integrity and ethics of the medical profession.
Protecting vulnerable groups (the elderly/disabled) from pressure or coercion.
Avoiding a "slippery slope" toward voluntary or involuntary euthanasia.
Conclusion
No. The Supreme Court reversed the Ninth Circuit. There is no fundamental right to physician-assisted suicide. Therefore, Washington's ban is constitutional as it is rationally related to legitimate government interests.
Wiz Tip for 1L Exams
You must contrast Glucksberg with Obergefell or Lawrence in your notes.
The Glucksberg Test: Very restrictive and backward-looking. If it wasn't a right in 1868 (when the 14th Amendment was ratified), it's probably not a fundamental right now.
The Lawrence/Obergefell Approach: More fluid and forward-looking. It looks at "evolving" standards of dignity and autonomy.
Professor's Secret: On a Con Law exam, if you want a law to be upheld, cite the Glucksberg "History and Tradition" test. If you want a law struck down, cite the Lawrence/Obergefell "Dignity and Autonomy" framework.
The Meaning and Implications of Glucksberg
1. The Two Methodologies of Liberty
There are two ways the Court looks at "Liberty" under the 14th Amendment.
The Glucksberg Approach (The "Narrow" View):
Focus: Specificity and History.
The Test: A right must be "deeply rooted in this Nation’s history and tradition."
Proponent: Chief Justice Rehnquist (and later the Dobbs majority).
Logic: This prevents judges from "discovering" rights that the public hasn't already accepted for centuries. It grounds the right to refuse treatment in the ancient law of battery (unwanted touching).
The Harlan/Souter Approach (The "Broad" View):
Focus: Principles and Arbitrary Restraint.
The Test: Tradition is a "living thing." We look at the broad principle of "freedom from arbitrary restraint."
Proponent: Justice Souter (citing Justice Harlan’s dissent in Poe v. Ullman).
Logic: The Constitution protects a "continuum of liberty." Even if a specific act (like assisted suicide) wasn't protected in 1868, the principle of bodily autonomy might cover it today.
2. Equal Protection and the "Right to Hasten Death"
In Vacco v. Quill, the Court rejected the argument that it is "unfair" to let some people die (those on life support) while others cannot (those who need a pill).
The Distinction (Causation and Intent):
Refusing Treatment: The patient dies of the underlying disease. The doctor’s intent is to stop "futile" treatment.
Assisted Suicide: The patient dies from the medication. The doctor’s intent is to "make the patient dead."
The "Rational" Line: The Court held this distinction is not "arbitrary." It is rooted in centuries of legal and medical ethics. Because there is no "fundamental right" to hasten death, the state only needs a Rational Basis to draw this line.
3. The "Palliative Care" Loophole
A "shadow majority" exists in the Glucksberg/Quill concurrences (O’Connor, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer).
The Rule of Double Effect: These Justices suggested that while the State can ban assisted suicide, it probably cannot ban a doctor from giving massive doses of painkillers to a dying patient, even if those painkillers happen to hasten death.
The Logic: The intent is to ease pain, not to kill. This is "palliative care."
Terminal Sedation: This has led to the legal practice of "terminal sedation"—knocking a patient unconscious with drugs and then withdrawing food/water (which is legal under Cruzan).
4. Distinguishing "Good" vs. "Bad" Substantive Due Process
Your notes raise the "Lochner Ghost." Critics (like Justice Scalia) argue that when the Court protects rights not in the text, it is acting like the Dred Scott Court or the Lochner Court.
The Challenge: How do we tell the difference between Griswold (Privacy/Good) and Dred Scott (Property in People/Bad)?
The Modern Answer: The current Court (Dobbs) argues the only "Good" ones are those with a clear historical pedigree (Glucksberg). The "Bad" ones are those where judges use "moral reasoning" to invent rights.
Wiz Summary: The "Death Spectrum" Table

Wiz Tip for the Final
If you have a case involving a "New Right" (e.g., a right to bio-hack your body or a right to a specific medical procedure):
Cite Glucksberg: Perform a "History and Tradition" search. Was this right protected in 1868? (Usually No).
The "Dignity" Counter-Move: Cite the Lawrence/Obergefell "Synergy." Argue that the right is part of a broader "personal relationship" or "autonomy" that the Court has recognized more recently.
The Dobbs Reality Check: Mention that Dobbs has re-installed Glucksberg as the "king" of the methodology, making it much harder to win these cases today.
Legal Source: Washington v. Glucksberg, 521 U.S. 702 (1997); Vacco v. Quill, 521 U.S. 793 (1997); Dobbs v. Jackson Women's Health Organization, 597 U.S. 215 (2022).

Section 1. Minimum Rationality Review of Economic Regulation
Railway Express Agency v. New York
336 U.S. 106, 69 S. Ct. 463, 93 L. Ed. 533 (1949).
The Facts
New York City had a traffic regulation that prohibited advertising on the side of delivery vehicles. However, there was a major catch: the law allowed vehicle owners to advertise their own business on their trucks but prohibited them from selling that space to other advertisers.
Railway Express Agency (REA), which operated a fleet of trucks, was convicted for selling advertising space on its vehicles for other businesses (like cigarettes or radio stations). REA argued that the law was discriminatory and violated the Equal Protection Clause of the 14th Amendment because the "distinction" between self-advertising and third-party advertising had nothing to do with traffic safety.
Issue
Does a municipal regulation that prohibits "advertising for hire" on vehicles, while allowing "owner-occupied" advertising, violate the Equal Protection Clause of the 14th Amendment?
Rule
Under the Rational Basis Test, a classification is constitutional if it is rationally related to a legitimate state interest. The court does not require the law to be perfectly logical or address every part of a problem at once.
Application
The Court applied a highly deferential standard to the City:
Legitimate Interest: The City’s goal was traffic safety (reducing distractions for drivers).
Rational Relation: The City argued that the type of advertising REA engaged in might be more distracting than an owner's simple business name.
The "Underinclusive" Argument: REA argued the law was unfair because an owner's truck could be just as distracting as a hired ad. Justice Douglas famously responded that the legislature may hit at an evil "where it is most felt" and does not have to strike at all evils at the same time.
Conclusion
No. The Supreme Court upheld the regulation. As long as there is a plausible reason for the classification related to a legitimate goal (traffic safety), the court will not second-guess the legislature's wisdom.
Wiz Tip for 1L Exams
When you see a case involving economic regulation (like business licenses, advertising, or taxes), the standard is almost always Rational Basis. In these cases, the government almost always wins because the "bar" is set so low. As Justice Douglas put it:
"It is no requirement of equal protection that all evils of the same genus be eradicated or none at all."
NFC RE: Judicial Deference to Economic Regulation
1. The Scope of Deference: "Any Plausible Reason"
In standard economic regulation, the Court doesn't care if the legislature was actually thinking of a specific reason when they passed a law. If the Court can hypothesize a reason later, the law stands.
Key Case: U.S. Railroad Retirement Board v. Fritz (1980)
The Takeaway: It is "constitutionally irrelevant" whether the hypothesized reasoning actually underlay the decision.
The "Line-Drawing" Justification: The Court argues that because legislatures must draw lines somewhere (e.g., who gets a benefit and who doesn't), those lines will always seem a bit arbitrary. Therefore, the Court grants maximum deference.
The Nepotism Exception: Kotch v. Board of River Port Pilot Commissioners (1947)
This is a "shocker" for many 1Ls. The Court upheld a law that essentially required you to be friends/family of incumbents to get a job.
The Logic: The Court deferred to "local tradition" and the idea that a "close-knit community" might perform better in high-stakes maritime work. This shows that under Rational Basis, even "fairness" is often sacrificed for "plausibility."
2. Underinclusivity: The "One Step at a Time" Doctrine
A common challenge to a law is: "Why does this law apply to me but not to my neighbor who is doing the exact same thing?" (This is the "underinclusivity" argument).
The Rule (Williamson v. Lee Optical, 1955): The legislature does not have to solve the whole problem at once. They can pick one "phase" of a problem and ignore others.
The Death of "Morey": * In Morey v. Doud, the Court tried to set a limit by saying you can't just name a specific company (American Express) for an exemption.
BUT, the Court later overruled this in New Orleans v. Dukes. They decided that "grandfather clauses" (exempting old businesses but banning new ones) are a rational way to preserve the "charm" of a city while slowly implementing new rules.
3. The "Animus" Exception: Rational Basis with "Bite"
This is the most important "counter-move" in your 1L arsenal. Sometimes, the Court uses the words "Rational Basis" but actually looks quite closely at the law. This usually happens if they suspect the law was born out of malice or prejudice (animus) against a group.
The "Hippie" Case (USDA v. Moreno, 1973): The Court struck down a law that denied food stamps to households with unrelated people.
Why? The legislative history showed it was intended to hurt "hippies."
People lived in the house together but they aren’t family so they were denied food stamps. 
Soley related to those things that qualify rational basis is for review
Not anything relating to the caroline 
Even if there’s animus and the legislation is motivated by animus, but hey can articulate a good economic rational basis for having it it will probably be upheld.
The Principle: A "bare desire to harm a politically unpopular group" is never a legitimate government interest.
The Counter-Example (NYC Transit Auth. v. Beazer, 1979): The Court upheld a ban on hiring methadone users. Even though it looked like "animus" to the dissent, the majority found a plausible "safety/uncertainty" reason. This shows how hard it is to prove animus.
4. The "Internal Inconsistency" Exception
In rare cases, a law is struck down because it is internally illogical based on the state's own existing rules.
The Tax Case (Allegheny Pittsburgh Coal, 1989): West Virginia’s constitution required uniform property taxes, but the assessor was only updating values when property was sold. This led to a 35x difference in taxes for identical neighbors.
The Limitation (Nordlinger v. Hahn, 1992): When California did the same thing via Proposition 13, the Court upheld it.
The Distinction: In Allegheny, the state's own law required uniformity, so the practice was irrational. In Nordlinger, the state wanted to reward long-term homeowners, which the Court deemed a "plausible" (if controversial) goal.

Village of Willowbrook v. Olech
The Facts
Grace Olech wanted to connect her property to the municipal water supply. The Village of Willowbrook agreed, but on one condition: she had to grant the Village a 33-foot easement (access to her land).
The Catch: Olech pointed out that every other property owner in the neighborhood only had to provide a 15-foot easement.
Olech sued, alleging that the Village was demanding the extra land out of "spite" and "ill will" because she had previously sued the Village over a different matter. She claimed the Village was treating her differently than everyone else for no rational reason, violating the Equal Protection Clause.
Issue
Can an individual bring a claim under the Equal Protection Clause as a "class of one" where they allege they have been intentionally treated differently from others similarly situated, and that there is no rational basis for the difference in treatment?
Rule
The Equal Protection Clause protects not only groups but individuals against arbitrary and irrational state action. A plaintiff can prevail as a "class of one" if they show:
They were intentionally treated differently from others similarly situated.
There is no rational basis for the difference in treatment.
Application
The Supreme Court issued a brief per curiam (by the whole court) opinion. They didn't even look at the "ill will" or "spite" argument initially.
They focused strictly on the irrationality: If the Village needs 15 feet from everyone else for water, but demands 33 feet from Olech without a technical reason, that is "irrational" and "wholly arbitrary."
The Court affirmed that the purpose of the Equal Protection Clause is to secure every person against intentional and arbitrary discrimination, whether it's directed at a whole class or just one person.
Conclusion
Yes. The Court ruled in favor of Olech. An individual can indeed be a "class of one." The case was allowed to proceed because the Village's demand for a larger easement lacked any "rational basis" compared to its treatment of her neighbors.
When a legistture treats on differet than on eits a classification it may deny equal protection to one group and they might be be upheld or not 
	In the social economic relm is rational basis. 
Wiz Tip: The "Olech" Limit
While this case was a huge win for individuals, the Court put a "leash" on it a few years later in Engquist v. Oregon Dept. of Agriculture (2008).
In Engquist, the Court ruled that the "class of one" theory does not apply to public employment. Why? Because personnel decisions (hiring/firing) are inherently subjective and discretionary. If every fired government employee could sue as a "class of one," the courts would be flooded.
For your exam: * Use Olech for regulatory/property issues (zoning, permits, licenses).
Remember Engquist if the fact pattern involves a government job.
Should Rationality Review Be Stricter?
1. The "Political Process" Theory (Solicitude for Minorities)
This theory stems from the famous Footnote 4 of Carolene Products. It asks: Is the group losing in the legislature because they are a "discrete and insular minority" who can't protect themselves?
The Problem: In a democracy, we usually let the majority win. But if a group (like the opticians in Lee Optical) is a "permanent loser" because they lack the numbers or money to ever win a vote, the Court might need to step in to prevent "majoritarian tyranny."
The Counter-Argument: Are economic groups (like opticians or milk haulers) actually "discrete and insular"?
The "Pluralist" View: Most economic groups aren't permanent losers; they just haven't found the right political allies yet. They can hire lobbyists and try again next year.
The "Public Choice" View (Mancur Olson): Small, well-organized groups (like the "Big Sugar" lobby) often have more power than large, diffuse groups (like "all consumers"). Therefore, the Court shouldn't protect "economic minorities" because they are often the ones actually winning through "rent-seeking."
2. The "Public Values" Theory (Civic Republicanism)
This theory argues that the Constitution isn't just a set of rules for a "political wrestling match"—it's a document meant to promote the public good.
"Naked Preferences": This is the idea that a legislature cannot pass a law just to help their friends or donors. For example, "Law X: Give $100 to Steve." That is a "naked preference."
The Requirement of a "Public Value": Under this theory (championed by Cass Sunstein), every law must be justified by a public purpose (e.g., health, safety, or morals).
Application: In Lee Optical, the Court didn't say "we are helping optometrists because we like them." They said "we are helping optometrists because it protects consumers' eye health." This "public value" provides the veneer of legitimacy required by Civic Republicanism.
3. The "Means-Focused" Theory (Rationality with "Bite")
This is often called the "Gunther Model" (after Professor Gerald Gunther). It suggests that the Court should stop being "lazy" and actually check the legislature’s homework.
The Problem with Hypothesizing: Currently, under Railway Express, the Court will invent a reason for a law if the legislature didn't provide one. Gunther argues this makes the Equal Protection Clause "toothless."
The Proposed Solution: The Court should only look at the actual purpose stated by the legislature at the time the law was passed.
No Post-Hoc Rationalizations: Lawyers shouldn't be allowed to make up "safety reasons" ten years later during a lawsuit.
The "Bite": By forcing the government to prove the law actually works (the "means" fit the "ends"), the Court disciplines the legislature and prevents "special interest" laws from slipping through.
Comparison Table: Three Theories of Scrutiny
Wiz Tip for Your Outline
When you're writing a ConLaw essay and you want to argue that a law should be struck down under Rational Basis (which is hard to do!), use these "Lenses":
"Class of One" Lens (Olech): "My client is being singled out for no reason."
"Animus" Lens (Moreno): "The legislature just wanted to hurt a group they don't like."
"Gunther Lens" (Policy): "The Court should not hypothesize a reason here; the law's actual means don't fit its actual ends."
Section 2. Race Discrimination
The Unconstitutionality of Racial Segregation
The difference in classification has been advanced.
Why would rational review with bite be enough?
	The supreme court uses strict scrutiny for Caroline products FN4 cases. 
1. The Early High Point: Strauder v. West Virginia (1880)
Immediately following the 14th Amendment, the Court took a robust view of racial equality, but only in the political/civic sphere.
The Fact: A West Virginia law explicitly barred Black men from jury service.
The Holding: The law is unconstitutional.
The Reasoning: The 14th Amendment was designed specifically to prevent "branding" a recently emancipated race with a "badge of inferiority."
The Limit: Justice Strong noted that states could still discriminate based on gender, property ownership, or education—just not race. This set the stage for the Court to later distinguish between "civil rights" and "social rights."
2. The Betrayal: Plessy v. Ferguson (1896)
This case created the "Separate but Equal" doctrine, which governed the U.S. for nearly 60 years.
The Legal Fiction: The Court argued that "social equality" cannot be forced by law. If Black citizens felt "inferior" because of segregated train cars, Justice Brown claimed it was because they "chose to put that construction upon it."
The Police Power: The Court held that segregation was a "reasonable" exercise of state power to preserve "public peace and good order" based on "established usages and traditions."
The Statistics of the Era: At the time of Plessy, the Black population was roughly 8 million compared to 60 million whites. This numerical disparity was used by Justice Harlan (in his dissent) to argue that the majority had nothing to fear from integration.
The Famous Harlan Dissent
Justice John Marshall Harlan is the hero of 1L ConLaw, but he is a complex figure.
The "Color-Blind" Constitution: He famously wrote, "Our Constitution is color-blind, and neither knows nor tolerates classes among citizens."
The Complexity: As your notes point out, Harlan was not a modern progressive. He believed the white race was—and would remain—dominant in "prestige, achievements, and wealth." His argument was that the law shouldn't need to help the "dominant" race by oppressing the minority.
The Anti-Chinese Sentiment: Harlan joined decisions like Chae Chan Ping v. United States (1889) upholding the Chinese Exclusion Act. His "color-blindness" in Plessy was likely a specific response to the unique history of Black Americans as former slaves and citizens, whereas he viewed Chinese immigrants as "unassimilable" aliens.
3. The Strategy: Attacking "Equal" (1938–1950)
Under Thurgood Marshall, the NAACP Legal Defense Fund realized they couldn't kill Plessy in one blow. Instead, they sued to force states to make "Separate" actually "Equal"—knowing the states couldn't afford to build two identical sets of elite professional schools.
4. The Transition to Brown v. Board
By 1950, the Court was admitting that "Separate" could never be "Equal" in graduate education because of "intangibles." The only question left for Brown (1954) was: Does this apply to children in K-12 schools too?
Key Takeaways for Your Exams:
Political vs. Social: Strauder protected "political" rights (juries); Plessy refused to protect "social" rights (trains/schools).
The "Badge of Inferiority": Strauder said segregation was a badge of inferiority; Plessy said it wasn't; Browneventually said it was.
Intangibles: Sweatt v. Painter is your best case for arguing that "separate" is inherently unequal because you cannot replicate "prestige" or "networks."
Wiz Stats for Context
To understand the "reasonableness" the Plessy court was looking at, consider the educational spending gap in the South circa 1910–1930:
In many Southern counties, the state spent $15.00 per white student for every $1.00 spent per Black student.
The "Separate but Equal" doctrine was, in practice, "Separate and Grossly Unequal."
Brown v. Board of Education [Brown I]
347 U.S. 483, 74 S. Ct. 686, 98 L. Ed. 873 (1954).
The Facts
The lead plaintiff, Oliver Brown, filed a class-action suit against the Board of Education of Topeka, Kansas, because his daughter, Linda, had to walk through a dangerous railroad switchyard to catch a bus to a segregated school, despite a white elementary school being much closer to their home.
The Legal Tension: The lower courts felt bound by Plessy v. Ferguson (1896). Because the physical facilities (buildings, curricula, teacher salaries) were found to be "substantially equal," the lower courts ruled that segregation was constitutional. The NAACP, led by Thurgood Marshall, appealed to the Supreme Court, arguing that "separate" can never be "equal."
Issue
Does the segregation of children in public schools solely on the basis of race, even though the physical facilities and other "tangible" factors may be equal, deprive the children of the minority group of equal educational opportunities in violation of the Equal Protection Clause of the 14th Amendment?
Rule
The 14th Amendment’s Equal Protection Clause prohibits states from denying any person "equal protection of the laws." In the context of public education, the doctrine of "separate but equal" has no place.
Legal Source: U.S. Const. amend. XIV, § 1; Brown v. Board of Education, 347 U.S. 483 (1954).
Application
Chief Justice Earl Warren wrote the unanimous opinion, focusing on several key pillars:
The Role of Education: The Court noted that education is perhaps the most important function of state and local governments. It is "the very foundation of good citizenship."
The "Intangible" Inequality: Building on Sweatt v. Painter, the Court held that even if physical facilities are identical, segregation deprives children of the benefits they would receive in a racially integrated school system.
Social Science Evidence: The Court famously cited the "Doll Test" and other psychological studies to show that segregation generates a "feeling of inferiority" in Black children that may affect their hearts and minds in a way unlikely ever to be undone.
Inherent Inequality: Because segregation is used to signify the "inferiority" of one group, the act of separation itself is a violation of equality.
Conclusion
Yes. The Supreme Court held that "separate educational facilities are inherently unequal." Therefore, the plaintiffs were deprived of the equal protection of the laws guaranteed by the 14th Amendment.
Wiz Tip: The "Two-Part" Brown
It is vital for your 1L exams to distinguish between Brown I and Brown II.
Brown I (1954): Declared segregation unconstitutional (The "Right").
Brown II (1955): Addressed the remedy. Because the Court knew the South would resist, they ordered that desegregation proceed with "all deliberate speed." (The "Implementation").
Professor's Note: Many historians argue that the phrase "all deliberate speed" was a mistake because it allowed Southern states to delay integration for decades. When you read Brown I, notice how short it is. Warren purposefully kept it simple and non-accusatory to ensure a 9–0 unanimous vote, which he believed was necessary for the country to accept such a massive social change.
It is vital for your 1L exams to distinguish between Brown I and Brown II.
Brown I (1954): Declared segregation unconstitutional (The "Right").
Brown II (1955): Addressed the remedy. Because the Court knew the South would resist, they ordered that desegregation proceed with "all deliberate speed." (The "Implementation").
Professor's Note: Many historians argue that the phrase "all deliberate speed" was a mistake because it allowed Southern states to delay integration for decades. When you read Brown I, notice how short it is. Warren purposefully kept it simple and non-accusatory to ensure a 9–0 unanimous vote, which he believed was necessary for the country to accept such a massive social change.
The Meaning and Implications of Brown v. Board
1. The Federal Expansion: Bolling v. Sharpe (1954)
Because the 14th Amendment’s Equal Protection Clause only applies to States, the Court had a problem: what about segregated schools in Washington, D.C. (a federal enclave)?
The "Reverse Incorporation" Doctrine: The Court held that the 5th Amendment Due Process Clause contains an equal protection component.
The Logic: It would be "unthinkable" for the Constitution to impose a stricter duty on the states than on the Federal Government.
The Rule: Discrimination can be so "unjustifiable" that it violates the concept of "liberty" itself. Thus, the 5th Amendment now essentially "incorporates" the 14th Amendment’s equal protection principles against the feds.
2. The "Domino Effect" (1955–1963)
After Brown, the Court didn't write long opinions for other facilities. They issued Per Curiam (unsigned, brief) orders.
The Result: Segregation was struck down in beaches, buses, golf courses, and parks.
The Takeaway: While Brown focused on the unique "importance of education," these subsequent cases proved that all state-mandated segregation was unconstitutional, regardless of the setting.
3. Four Competing Theories of Brown
When you argue a case today, you must decide which "version" of Brown you are using:
Color-Blindness (The Formalist View): Race is never a valid category. (Used today by conservatives to argue against affirmative action).
Caste/Anti-Subordination (The Functionalist View): Race is only a problem when it creates a "superior" and "inferior" class. (Used by progressives to argue for affirmative action).
White Supremacy (The Political View): The law was bad because it was passed by a white majority to keep a Black minority down.
Integration (The Social View): The goal is "diverse classrooms" because they produce better citizens and social outcomes.
4. The "Internal Drama": How 9–0 Happened
Chief Justice Vinson: Initially, the Court was divided. Vinson (from Kentucky) was reluctant to overrule Plessy.
Justice Frankfurter’s "Act of God": When Vinson died and was replaced by Earl Warren, the momentum shifted. Frankfurter’s dark joke about Vinson's death reflected his belief that a new Chief was necessary to save the Court's legitimacy.
The Unanimity Strategy: Warren knew a 5–4 or 6–3 split would lead to a Civil War-level resistance. He lobbied the dissenters until he achieved a 9–0 vote.
5. The Social Science Controversy (Footnote 11)
Warren cited psychological studies (the "Doll Tests" by Kenneth and Mamie Clark) to prove segregation caused a "feeling of inferiority."
The Critique: Critics like Justice Clarence Thomas argue this was a mistake. If you base a constitutional right on psychology, what happens if a new study comes out saying children are "fine" in segregated schools?
The Alternative: Many argue the Court should have simply said: "The state cannot categorize by race, period," regardless of the psychological effect.
6. Neutral Principles vs. Social Reality
The Wechsler Critique: Professor Herbert Wechsler asked: If both white and Black children are barred from each other's schools, isn't that "neutral"? He argued the Court struggled to find a "neutral principle" to prefer "integration" over "freedom of association."
The Charles Black Response: He famously replied that everyone knows segregation wasn't about "association"—it was about oppression. To pretend the two races were treated "equally" just because both were separated was a legal "fiction" that deserved laughter.
7. The Redemption of Hugo Black
Justice Hugo Black’s story is a classic 1L "character arc."
The Stain: He was a former member of the KKK.
The Voting Record: He became the most adamant supporter of desegregation on the Court, likely to "wash away" his past dishonor.
The Cost: His son was forced out of his law firm in Alabama due to the family's "traitorous" stance on civil rights.
Wiz Tip for Exams: The "Stigma" Argument
If you get a fact pattern about a government policy that treats groups differently, ask yourself: "Does this create a stigma or a feeling of inferiority?"
If YES  Cite Brown and the "Caste" theory.
If the government says, "But it's for their own benefit!"  Counter with Sweatt v. Painter (the intangibles) and Bolling v. Sharpe (it's a deprivation of liberty).
Implementing Brown v. Board
1. The Delay: Brown II (1955)
The Court faced a dilemma: how do you force a hostile South to integrate?
The Standard: "With all deliberate speed."
The Result: This phrase became a shield for delay. For a decade after Brown, very little actual integration occurred.
The Shift: Implementation was handed to local District Courts. This was a "double-edged sword"—local judges knew the terrain, but they were also under immense social and political pressure to go slow.
2. The Mandate: "Root and Branch" (1968–1971)
Frustrated by a decade of "freedom of choice" plans that resulted in zero actual integration, the Court finally lost its patience.
Green v. County School Board (1968)
The Rule: It is not enough to stop discriminating; school boards have an affirmative duty to take whatever steps are necessary to convert to a "unitary system."
The "Green Factors": To determine if a system is "unitary" (integrated), courts look at six factors: student assignment, faculty, staff, transportation, extracurriculars, and facilities.
Key Phrase: Discrimination must be eliminated "root and branch."
Swann v. Charlotte-Mecklenburg (1971)
The Tool: Busing. The Court approved the use of busing and "drastic gerrymandering" of school zones to achieve integration.
The Limit: The Court noted that not every school must perfectly reflect the district’s racial composition, but there is a "presumption against" one-race schools.
3. Expansion and Retrenchment: North vs. South
The Court then had to decide if these rules applied to Northern cities that didn't have "Jim Crow" laws but were still segregated by housing patterns.
4. The Limits of Judicial Power
In the 1990s, the Rehnquist Court began pulling back the "broad equitable powers" granted in the 1970s.
Taxation (Missouri v. Jenkins I, 1990): A federal court cannot directly raise taxes to fund schools, though it can order the school district to do it and ignore state-law caps.
Student Achievement (Missouri v. Jenkins II, 1995): The Court ruled that low test scores (compared to national norms) are not a justification for an indefinite court order. The goal is to fix the legal violation, not to solve every social problem or educational gap.
The Exit Strategy (Board of Ed. v. Dowell, 1991): Federal supervision was meant to be temporary. Once a district has complied in "good faith" for a reasonable time, the court must "dissolve" the decree and return control to the local board—even if the schools eventually become segregated again due to private housing choices.
5. The Great Debate: Stigma vs. Local Control
Your notes highlight a fundamental philosophical split between Justice Rehnquist and Justice Marshall.
The Rehnquist View (Majority): The Constitution only prohibits intentional government acts. Once the government stops those acts and fixes the immediate mess, its job is done. Values of "local control" outweigh the desire for perfect social outcomes.
The Marshall View (Dissent): The "stigma" and "badge of inferiority" from 65 years of state-sponsored racism don't disappear in 13 years of busing. To Marshall, the effects of past discrimination are "chargeable" to the state until they are actually gone.
Wiz Summary Table: The Remedial Arc
Wiz Tip for Exams
If you see a school desegregation problem on an exam, check the geography:
Intra-district (Inside one city): Courts have broad power (Swann).
Inter-district (City vs. Suburbs): Courts are almost powerless unless there's proof the suburbs "caused" the city's segregation (Milliken).
Modern Day: If the district has been "unitary" for a few years, the school board can likely go back to neighborhood schools even if it results in re-segregation (Dowell).

Loving v. Virginia
388 U.S. 1, 87 S. Ct. 1817, 18 L. Ed. 2d 1010 (1967).
The Facts
In 1958, Mildred Jeter (a Black woman) and Richard Loving (a white man) were married in the District of Columbia. They then moved back to their home in Caroline County, Virginia.
Shortly after, they were awakened in their bed by the local sheriff and arrested. They were charged with violating Virginia's Racial Integrity Act of 1924, which prohibited intermarriage between white and non-white persons.
The Lovings pleaded guilty and were sentenced to one year in jail. However, the trial judge suspended the sentence on the condition that the Lovings leave Virginia and not return together for 25 years. The judge famously justified his decision by stating:
"Almighty God created the races white, black, yellow, malay and red, and he placed them on separate continents... The fact that he separated the races shows that he did not intend for the races to mix."
The Lovings eventually sued, arguing the law violated the 14th Amendment.
Issue
Does a statutory scheme that prevents marriages between persons solely on the basis of racial classifications violate the Equal Protection and Due Process Clauses of the 14th Amendment?
Rule
Equal Protection: Restricting a fundamental right (marriage) based on race is subject to Strict Scrutiny. The law must be "necessary to the accomplishment of some permissible state objective, independent of the racial discrimination."
Due Process: The right to marry is a fundamental liberty, and the state cannot deprive a citizen of that liberty without a compelling reason.
Legal Source: U.S. Const. amend. XIV; Loving v. Virginia, 388 U.S. 1 (1967).
Application
Virginia made a "neutral" argument: because the law punished the white spouse and the Black spouse equally, there was no discrimination. The Court rejected this "Equal Application" theory.
Strict Scrutiny: Chief Justice Warren noted that there is no legitimate overriding purpose here. The law's only goal was White Supremacy (notably, the law only prohibited marriages between whites and non-whites; it didn't care if a Black person married an Asian person).
Fundamental Right: The Court held that marriage is one of the "basic civil rights of man," fundamental to our very existence and survival. To deny this right based on race is "subversive of the principle of equality at the heart of the Fourteenth Amendment."
Conclusion
Yes. The Supreme Court unanimously struck down the Virginia law. The Court held that the 14th Amendment requires that the freedom of choice to marry not be restricted by invidious racial discriminations.
Wiz Tip for 1L Exams
Loving is a "two-fer" case—it is cited in both Equal Protection and Substantive Due Process sections of your outline.
Equal Protection: Use Loving to show that "equal application" of a racist law is still unconstitutional. (Example: "We ban everyone from doing X" is still racist if the motivation is white supremacy).
Due Process: Use Loving as the foundation for the Fundamental Right to Marry. This case was a crucial precedent used decades later in Obergefell v. Hodges (2015) to strike down bans on same-sex marriage.
1. The "Private Bias" Rule: Palmore v. Sidoti (1984)
This is a high-yield case for 1L exams because it creates a hard line between what society thinks and what the law allows.
The Holding: A court cannot take a child away from a mother solely because she entered an interracial marriage, even if the court believes the child will face social stigma as a result.
The "Palmore Doctrine": "The Constitution cannot control such prejudices but neither can it tolerate them. Private biases may be outside the reach of the law, but the law cannot, directly or indirectly, give them effect."
The "Best Interests" vs. Equal Protection: While the "best interests of the child" is a compelling state interest, using race as a proxy for that interest is not "narrowly tailored" and is ultimately unconstitutional.
The Adoption Debate (Notes)
Post-Palmore, the legality of "race-matching" in adoption became a major academic and legal battleground.
The Multiethnic Placement Act (MEPA) of 1994: This federal law (and its 1996 amendments) actually codifies the Palmore logic by prohibiting state agencies from delaying or denying foster/adoptive placements based solely on the race of the child or the prospective parents.
The Conflict: Scholars like Elizabeth Bartholet argue that race-matching traps Black children in foster care longer, while others argue that "enculturation" is a valid reason for same-race placement. Under current law, race can almost never be the sole factor.
2. The Death of "Equal Application" as a Defense
One of the most common "tricks" in ConLaw is the argument that a law isn't discriminatory because it "burdens both races equally."
The Rejection: Starting with Loving and solidified in Johnson v. California, the Court has explicitly rejected this.
The Logic: Even if both races are treated the same way (e.g., both are segregated), the act of classifying by race is what triggers strict scrutiny. As the Court noted in Johnson, "separate can [never] be equal—or 'neutral'."
3. Race in "High-Apex" Power: Johnson v. California (2005)
This case is critical because it tests whether there are any exceptions to strict scrutiny—even in the most dangerous environments.
The Policy: California segregated new inmates by race for 60 days to prevent race-based gang violence.
The Standard of Review: The State argued for a "Reasonableness" standard (the Turner standard usually used for prison regulations). The Supreme Court said NO.
The Holding: Even in prison, Strict Scrutiny applies to racial classifications.
The Rationale: 1.  Reinforcement: Segregating by race might actually breed more hostility and reinforce the very gang divisions the state wants to stop. 2.  The "Apex" Argument: Searching judicial review is most necessary where the government's power is at its highest (like in a prison).
The Dissenting Views
Justice Stevens: Thought the record was so bad the Court should have just struck the law down immediately without remanding it.
Justice Thomas (The Deference View): Argued that "the Constitution has always demanded less within the prison walls." He believed the safety of inmates' lives (avoiding stabbings) outweighed the "indignity" of temporary segregation.
4. Summary: Is the Constitution "Color-Blind" or "Anti-Subordination"?
Your notes touch on a deep schism in the current Court.
Current Status: The "Anti-Classification" (Color-Blind) view is currently the dominant philosophy of the Supreme Court majority. This is why even "neutral" or "safety-based" racial classifications are now treated with extreme suspicion.
Wiz Tip for Exams: The "Strict Scrutiny" Checklist
If you see a government action involving race, follow this flowchart:
Is there a racial classification? (Even if it applies "equally" to both races?)  YES.
Is there a Compelling Interest? (e.g., Prison safety is compelling; "Best interests of the child" is compelling).
Is it Narrowly Tailored? (This is where the government usually loses. Could they have used non-racial means? e.g., segregating by gang affiliation instead of race?)  If NO, the law fails.
Facial Discrimination Against Racial Minorities
1. The Birth of Strict Scrutiny: Korematsu v. United States (1944)
Paradoxically, the most famous test for protecting minorities was born in a case that allowed for their mass internment.
The Facts: Following Pearl Harbor, 112,000 people of Japanese ancestry (70,000 of whom were U.S. citizens) were forcibly removed from the West Coast and placed in "relocation centers" (internment camps). Fred Korematsu refused to leave his home and was convicted.
The "Suspect Classification" Rule: Justice Black wrote the words that changed ConLaw forever: "All legal restrictions which curtail the civil rights of a single racial group are immediately suspect... courts must subject them to the most rigid scrutiny."
The "Pressing Public Necessity": Despite the "rigid scrutiny," the Court upheld the order. Why? They deferred entirely to the military's claim that there was no time to separate the "loyal" from the "disloyal" and that the West Coast was in "immediate danger."
2. The Powerful Dissents: Foreshadowing History
The Korematsu dissents are arguably more famous today than the majority opinion.
Justice Murphy (The "Legalized Racism" View): He argued the exclusion had no "reasonable relation" to safety. He pointed out that the military’s judgment was based on "misinformation and half-truths" about Japanese-Americans, rather than actual evidence of sabotage.
Justice Jackson (The "Loaded Weapon" View): This is a classic exam quote. Jackson warned that by validating this order, the Court created a "loaded weapon" that any future "urgent" authority could pick up to justify discrimination. He argued that even if the military must act unconstitutionally in war, the Court should not "sanitize" it with a legal opinion.
Justice Roberts: Clarified that this wasn't just about "leaving a zone"—it was a choice between staying and being arrested, or leaving and being imprisoned in a camp.
3. The Expansion of "Suspect" Groups
While the 14th Amendment was written for Black Americans, Korematsu and subsequent cases expanded "Strict Scrutiny" to include:
National Origin & Ancestry: Irish, Chinese, Mexican-American, and Native Hawaiian classifications all trigger the same "grave concerns" as race.
Key Case (Hernandez v. Texas, 1954): Struck down the exclusion of Mexican-Americans from juries, noting that "community prejudices are not static" and new groups may need protection over time.
4. The "Overruling" of Korematsu
For 74 years, Korematsu sat on the books as "bad law" that was never technically reversed.
Trump v. Hawaii (2018): In the "Travel Ban" case, Chief Justice Roberts finally wrote: "Korematsu was gravely wrong the day it was decided... and has no place in law under the Constitution."
The Sotomayor Critique: Justice Sotomayor argued that the majority was doing the exact same thing as the Korematsu court: deferring blindly to a "national security" claim to uphold a policy she viewed as motivated by religious animus (against Muslims).
5. Immigration: The Last Stronghold of Discrimination?
Your notes raise a terrifying question for a 1L: Does the Equal Protection Clause apply to immigration?
The "Plenary Power" Doctrine: Historically, the Court held in the Chinese Exclusion Case (1889) that the government has "sovereign power" to exclude aliens for any reason—including race.
Modern Reality: While Trump v. Hawaii applied a very low level of "Rational Basis" to the travel ban, it suggested that the government still has much broader power to discriminate at the border than it does inside the country.
The "Inside/Outside" Distinction: * Inside the U.S.: Full Strict Scrutiny for everyone (Johnson v. California).
At the Border: Significant judicial deference to the Executive Branch.
Wiz Summary: Korematsu Numbers & Facts
To understand the "pressing necessity" the Court bought into, consider these facts:
Number Interned: ~120,000.
Citizenship: 2/3 were American citizens by birth.
Espionage Convictions: Zero. No Japanese-American was ever convicted of sabotage or espionage during WWII.
The "Mistake": In 1984, it was revealed the government suppressed evidence from the FBI and ONI that confirmed Japanese-Americans posed no threat. This led to Korematsu's conviction being vacated (though the Supreme Court precedent remained until 2018).
Wiz Tip for Exams
If you have a fact pattern where the government discriminates based on race but claims "National Security" or "Emergency":
State that Strict Scrutiny applies (Korematsu/Loving).
Argue that under Korematsu, the Court used to defer to the government.
HOWEVER, argue that modern law (Trump v. Hawaii) says Korematsu was "gravely wrong."
Apply the Narrow Tailoring test: Could the government have achieved safety without using a racial dragnet? (Usually, the answer is yes).
Racially Discriminatory Purpose and Effect
The "Discriminatory Heart" Roadmap
For a facially neutral law (one that looks "fair" on paper) to be struck down under Strict Scrutiny, you must prove Discriminatory Intent/Purpose. Impact alone is not enough.
The Three Ways to Prove Intent
Discriminatory Administration (Yick Wo): The law is fine, but the people in charge only lock up one race.
Statistic: In Yick Wo, 240 Chinese applicants were denied permits while 80 non-Chinese applicants were granted them.
Unexplainable Impact (Gomillion): The law's effect is so weird it could only be explained by race.
Concrete Number: The Tuskegee boundary change removed 395 out of 400 Black voters but 0 white voters.
Discriminatory History (Griffin): The timing and context (closing schools right after a desegregation order) reveal the true motive.
Washington v. Davis
426 U.S. 229, 96 S. Ct. 2040, 48 L. Ed. 2d 597 (1976).
The Facts
Two Black applicants for the Washington D.C. police department challenged "Test 21," a written personnel test used to measure verbal ability, vocabulary, and reading comprehension. They argued the test violated the Equal Protection component of the 5th Amendment (via Bolling v. Sharpe) because a higher percentage of Black applicants failed the test than white applicants.
There was no evidence that the police department intended to discriminate; the test was a standard civil service exam. The plaintiffs argued that the disproportionate impact alone should trigger Strict Scrutiny.
Issue: Does a facially neutral law or official act violate the Equal Protection Clause solely because it has a disproportionate impact on a particular race, without proof of discriminatory intent?
Rule: * The Intent Requirement: To trigger Strict Scrutiny for a facially neutral law, the plaintiff must prove discriminatory purpose or intent.
Impact is Evidence, Not the End: Disproportionate impact is relevant evidence of intent, but it is not, by itself, enough to prove a constitutional violation.
Application: Justice White, writing for the Court, rejected the "Impact Only" rule.
The "Floodgates" Concern: If impact alone triggered Strict Scrutiny, thousands of neutral laws (taxes, welfare, licensing) would be unconstitutional because they happen to affect different races differently.
The Test 21 Context: The Court found the test was "neutral on its face" and served the legitimate goal of ensuring police officers had a certain level of literacy. Because there was no proof the Department wanted to exclude Black officers, the law was subject only to Rational Basis Review, which it easily passed.
Conclusion: No. A racially disproportionate impact is not enough to establish a constitutional violation. You must prove the government acted because of, not merely in spite of, the adverse effects on a protected group.
The Palmer v. Thompson Confusion
You mentioned Palmer v. Thompson (the pool closing case). This is a tricky "outlier" case.
The Logic: Justice Black argued that even if the city closed the pools because of "ideological opposition to integration," the effect was symmetrical (no one, Black or white, got to swim).
Wiz Analysis: Most scholars see Palmer as a weak decision. Usually, if you can prove a "bad motive" (like in Griffin), the law should fail. Palmer shows that sometimes the Court is willing to overlook "bad motives" if the state just stops providing the service entirely.
Summary Table: Purpose vs. Impact
Wiz Tip for Exams: The "Davis" Defense
Whenever you see a law that "hurts minorities more" (e.g., a law requiring a high school diploma for a job, or a law increasing penalties for crimes more common in urban areas), you must look for Intent.
If the plaintiff cannot prove the legislature was "out to get" them  Rational Basis (Government wins).
If the plaintiff can prove the legislature acted with "animus" or "hostility"  Strict Scrutiny (Plaintiff wins).
Washington v. Davis
426 U.S. 229, 96 S. Ct. 2040, 48 L. Ed. 2d 597 (1976).
The Facts
Two Black applicants for the District of Columbia Metropolitan Police Department challenged the department's recruiting procedures. Specifically, they targeted "Test 21," a written personnel test developed by the Civil Service Commission to measure verbal ability, vocabulary, and reading comprehension.
The Evidence:
Statistics showed that a significantly higher percentage of Black applicants failed the test than white applicants.
The plaintiffs argued that the test was not "job-related" and that the disproportionate impact on Black candidates was enough to prove a violation of the Equal Protection component of the 5th Amendment's Due Process Clause (applying Bolling v. Sharpe).
The Lower Court: The Court of Appeals agreed with the plaintiffs, holding that the lack of discriminatory intent was irrelevant—the "discriminatory payoff" (the result) was what mattered.
Issue
Does a facially neutral law or official act (like a standardized test) violate the Equal Protection Clause solely because it has a disproportionate impact on a particular race, regardless of whether there is proof of discriminatory intent?
Rule
To trigger Strict Scrutiny under the Equal Protection Clause, a plaintiff must prove that the government acted with a discriminatory purpose or intent. A law that is neutral on its face and serves a legitimate government end is not invalid simply because it affects a greater proportion of one race than another.
Application
Justice White, writing for the 7-2 majority, reversed the lower court. The Court’s reasoning rested on two major pillars:
Constitutional Standard vs. Statutory Standard: The Court clarified that while Title VII (an employment law) might allow a "disparate impact" claim, the Constitution requires more. To find a constitutional violation, the state must have intended to discriminate.
The "Floodgates" Argument: The Court feared that if "impact alone" were the standard, a massive number of valid government actions (taxes, welfare, regulatory hurdles) would be struck down because they inevitably impact different demographic groups differently.
Application to the Facts: Because Test 21 was used to ensure police officers had a certain level of literacy (a legitimate goal) and there was no evidence the police department intended to exclude Black applicants, the test was constitutional under Rational Basis Review.
Conclusion
No. Disproportionate impact is not enough. Without proof of discriminatory intent, a facially neutral law only receives Rational Basis Review and will likely be upheld.
Concurrence Justice Stevens:
Justice Stevens’s concurrence in Washington v. Davis is the "common sense" middle ground of the case. He argued that courts shouldn't try to be mind-readers of a legislator’s subjective intent, as proving a "bad motive" is nearly impossible; instead, he believed intent should be inferred from objective evidence and the natural, foreseeable consequences of a policy. To Stevens, if a law has a stark and dramatic disproportionate impact, that impact is often the most convincing evidence of a discriminatory purpose. While he agreed that "Test 21" was constitutional because it was a standard, neutral literacy tool used across the government, his opinion provides 1L students with the "objective impact" argument: if the result of a law is "unexplainable on other grounds," the court should treat the intent as apparent.
Wiz Tip for 1L Exams
This is a "Make or Break" concept for your final. If your exam prompt describes a law that "accidentally" hurts a minority group:
Step 1: Look for Facial Discrimination. (Does the law mention race? No.)
Step 2: Look for Discriminatory Intent. (Did the city council say, "Let’s pass this to keep [Group] out"? No.)
Step 3: If you only have Disparate Impact, you cannot use Strict Scrutiny. You must apply Rational Basis Review.
Professor's Warning: Be careful! Disproportionate impact is evidence that can help prove intent (like in Gomillion), but it is not the "smoking gun" by itself.
The Meaning and Implications of Washington v. Davis
1. The "Arlington Heights" Factors (How to Prove Intent)
When a law is facially neutral, the Court uses a "sensitive inquiry" into circumstantial evidence to find discriminatory purpose.
The Starting Point: Disproportionate impact (though rarely sufficient on its own).
Historical Background: Is there a "series of official actions" taken for invidious purposes?
Sequence of Events: Did the government suddenly change the rules (e.g., rezoning) only after learning an integrated project was planned?
Procedural Departures: Did the government skip its usual meetings or notice requirements to push a decision through?
Substantive Departures: Did the government ignore the factors it usually considers important (e.g., environmental safety) to reach a specific result?
Legislative History: Contemporary statements in minutes, reports, or floor debates.
2. The Burden-Shifting Rule (Mt. Healthy / Arlington Heights)
If a plaintiff proves race was a "motivating factor," the law isn't automatically struck down. Instead, the burden shifts:
The "But-For" Defense: The government can avoid invalidation if it proves the same decision would have been made even if the discriminatory purpose hadn't been considered.
The Policy Logic: This prevents the "futility" of striking down a law that the legislature would just pass again for "pure" reasons.
3. Application in Voting & Crime (Rogers and Hunter)
Rogers v. Lodge (1982): The Court upheld a finding of intent based on "sociological evidence" in an at-large voting system. Key evidence included the fact that 0 Black candidates had ever been elected despite Black residents being 53.6% of the population, combined with a history of bloc voting.
Hunter v. Underwood (1985): Struck down a 1901 Alabama law disenfranchising people for "moral turpitude" crimes.
The "Smoking Gun": The 1901 Convention record showed a "zeal for white supremacy."
The Stats: The law disenfranchised 10x as many Black citizens as whites.
The Strategy: The Convention specifically chose crimes (like "vagrancy" or "wife beating") they believed Black people were more likely to commit.
4. Statutory vs. Constitutional Standard
There is a massive divide between what the Constitution (14th Amendment) requires and what Statutes (Title VII, Fair Housing Act) require.
Statutory Disparate Impact: Under some federal laws, you can sue based on impact alone (e.g., Texas Dept. of Housing).
The "Scalia/Kennedy" Tension: Some Justices (Scalia in Ricci) argue that even statutory disparate impact rules might be unconstitutional.
The Argument: If an employer changes a policy because they fear "too many minorities are failing," they are making a race-based decision, which Scalia argues is itself a form of discrimination against the majority.
