Causality Requirement: To keep these laws constitutional, Justice Kennedy required a "robust causality requirement"—plaintiffs must point to a specific policy causing the disparity, not just a general statistical imbalance.
Wiz Tip for the Final
If you have a facially neutral law on your exam, do not stop at Washington v. Davis. 1. Use the Arlington Heights list to hunt for "shady" procedural moves or historical "smoke." 2. If you find any "smoke," state that the burden shifts to the government to prove they would have done it anyway (Mt. Healthy defense).
Section 3. Sex Discrimination
I. The Constitutional "Silence" and Global Context
Unique Omission: The U.S. Constitution lacks an explicit sex equality provision (unlike France, Germany, India, Canada, and South Africa).
Original Text: Women were never explicitly mentioned in the original framing. Early protections (habeas corpus, etc.) applied implicitly, but no federal shield existed against sex-specific laws.
The 14th Amendment Gap: The 14th Amendment’s second section actually introduced the word "male" into the Constitution regarding voting rights.
Source: Sullivan, Constitutionalizing Women’s Equality, 90 Cal. L. Rev. 735 (2002).
II. The "Separate Spheres" Era (Late 19th - Mid 20th Century)
Early Rejections: * Bradwell v. Illinois (1873): The Court ruled that the "Privileges or Immunities" Clause did not grant women the right to practice law. Justice Bradley’s concurrence famously cited "divine ordinance" and the "domestic sphere" as a woman's true destiny.
Minor v. Happersett (1874): Held that being a citizen did not automatically grant women the right to vote.
The 19th Amendment (1920): Granted the franchise (voting) but was initially construed narrowly, failing to protect women in other areas like jury service.
The "Rationality" Shield: Even as late as 1948 (Goesaert v. Cleary), the Court upheld laws banning women from bartending (unless related to the owner) under a "rational basis" standard, deferring to the legislature's "romantic paternalism."
Source: Bradwell v. Illinois, 83 U.S. 130 (1873); Goesaert v. Cleary, 335 U.S. 464 (1948).
III. The 1970s Shift: Heightened Scrutiny
Reed v. Reed (1971): A turning point. For the first time, the Court struck down a sex-based preference (estate administration) using the Rational Basis test. While it claimed to use "rationality," the Court was actually applying a "bit with teeth"—refusing to accept administrative convenience as a valid excuse for sex discrimination.
Frontiero v. Richardson (1973):
The Plurality: Justice Brennan argued sex should be a Suspect Classification (Strict Scrutiny) because sex is an "immutable characteristic" that frequently bears no relation to ability.
The Pushback: Justice Powell argued the Court should wait for the Equal Rights Amendment (ERA) to be ratified before making such a leap.
IV. The Failed Equal Rights Amendment (ERA)
The Proposal: Proposed in 1972, it would have explicitly prohibited the denial of rights "on account of sex."
The Failure: It fell three states short of the required 38 for ratification by the 1982 deadline.
Impact: Some scholars argue the social movement for the ERA influenced the Court to strengthen protections under the 14th Amendment even without the amendment’s passage.
V. The "Intermediate" Compromise
Terminology: Strategic shift from using the word "sex" to "gender" (pioneered by Ruth Bader Ginsburg) to avoid distracting biological associations and focus on social/legal status.
Craig v. Boren (1976): The Court officially settled on Intermediate Scrutiny.
The Test: To be constitutional, gender-based classifications must serve important governmental objectives and be substantially related to those objectives.
Pro-Tip for your 1L Exams: When you see a sex-based classification in a fact pattern, remember that it doesn't get the "Strict Scrutiny" of race, but it gets much more than the "Rational Basis" of age or wealth. You are looking for an "exceedingly persuasive justification."
How are we feeling about the distinction between "Important" and "Compelling" governmental interests so far?
Affirmative Action and Race Preferences
Welcome back to the lecture hall. We are now pivoting to one of the most contentious "battlegrounds" in Constitutional Law: Affirmative Action. This is the study of "Benign" Racial Classifications. The central tension here is whether the 14th Amendment is "Color-Blind" (prohibiting all racial lines) or "Anti-Subordination" (prohibiting only lines that hurt disadvantaged groups).
Here are your detailed notes on the evolution of this doctrine.
I. The Threshold Question: What Level of Scrutiny?
For decades, the Court debated whether a "good" motive (helping minorities) should get a lower standard of review than a "bad" motive (discriminating against them).
The "Benign" Argument: Some (like Justice Marshall) argued for Intermediate Scrutiny, suggesting the 14th Amendment’s original intent was to protect Black Americans, so laws helping them shouldn't be treated as harshly as Jim Crow laws.
The "Political Process" Argument: John Hart Ely argued that when a "majority" (white people) chooses to disadvantage itself to help a "minority," the Court doesn't need to be suspicious because the majority can protect itself at the ballot box.
The Final Rule (Strict Scrutiny): Ultimately, the Court (led by Justice O'Connor and later Chief Justice Roberts) settled on Strict Scrutiny for ALL racial classifications, regardless of whether they are "benign" or "hostile."
Logic: The Court feared "benign" preferences might actually be based on stereotypes or cause "racial resentment."
II. Permissible vs. Impermissible Justifications
Under Strict Scrutiny, a racial preference must serve a Compelling Interest. The Court has been very picky about what counts:
1. Remedying Past State Discrimination (Permissible)
If a specific government entity (like a city’s school board) was caught discriminating in the past, they can use race-based remedies to fix their own specific mess.
2. Remedying "Societal" Discrimination (Impermissible)
The Court has flatly rejected using race preferences to fix "general societal unfairness."
Reasoning: Societal discrimination is too "amorphous" and lacks a clear stopping point. It would allow race preferences to last forever.
3. Diversity in Higher Education (The "Expired" Interest)
For 45 years (from Bakke to Grutter), the Court held that diversity in the classroom was a compelling interest because it prepared students for a diverse workforce.
The End of an Era: In 2023, the Court effectively ended this justification, ruling that diversity programs at Harvard and UNC lacked "measurable" goals and relied on prohibited stereotypes.
Source: SFFA v. Harvard, 600 U.S. 181 (2023).
III. The 14th Amendment’s "Original Purpose" vs. Evolution
Originalist View: Proponents of affirmative action point to the fact that the same Congress that passed the 14th Amendment also passed race-conscious relief laws (like the Freedmen’s Bureau Act).
Modern Individualist View: The current Court views the 14th Amendment as protecting individuals, not groups. Therefore, any individual—regardless of race—has a right not to be judged by their skin color.
IV. Statistics: The Disparity Context
To understand why these cases reach the Court, one must look at the disparities that affirmative action sought to address. For example:
Educational Attainment: As of 2022, approximately 42% of White adults (aged 25+) held a bachelor's degree or higher, compared to roughly 28% of Black adults and 21% of Hispanic adults.
Economic Indicators: The median household income for Black families in 2022 was approximately $52,860, while for White (non-Hispanic) families, it was roughly $81,060.
Source: U.S. Census Bureau, Current Population Survey Reports (2022-2023).
V. Takeaways for your 1L Outline
Export to Sheets
Professor's Sidebar: > When writing your exam, never use the phrase "reverse discrimination." The Court views all race-based government action the same way. The question is always: Is there a compelling reason, and is the law narrowly tailored? (Hint: Since 2023, the answer is almost always "No").
Does the shift from "Class-Based" protection (protecting groups) to "Individual-Based" protection (protecting people from being grouped) make sense as a progression of Constitutional Law?
Regents of the University of California v. Bakke is the foundational "grandfather" case of affirmative action. It is famous for its "split the baby" outcome: the Court struck down the specific program in question but opened the door for race-conscious admissions that lasted for the next 45 years.
Regents of Univ. of California v. Bakke
438 U.S. 265, 98 S. Ct. 2733, 57 L. Ed. 2d 750 (1978).
This case is also a 1L's rite of passage because of its complex plurality—Justice Powell wrote the "judgment of the Court," but he was often a "majority of one."
The Medical School of the University of California at Davis had a dual-track admissions system.
The General Track: For most applicants.
The Special Track: Reserved 16 out of 100 seats in the entering class for "disadvantaged" minority students (Blacks, Chicanos, Asians, and American Indians).
Allan Bakke, a white male, was rejected twice despite having significantly higher MCAT scores and GPAs than many students admitted through the "Special Track." Bakke sued, alleging that this "set-aside" or quota violated the Equal Protection Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment and Title VI of the Civil Rights Act of 1964.
Issue
Is a public university's admissions program that reserves a specific number of seats for minority applicants unconstitutional under the Equal Protection Clause?
Rule: Strict Scrutiny
Level of Scrutiny: Racial classifications are "inherently suspect" and must be reviewed under Strict Scrutiny, regardless of whether the group being "helped" is a minority.
The Test: To be constitutional, the program must:
Serve a Compelling Governmental Interest; and
Be Narrowly Tailored to achieve that interest.
Analysis (The "Powell" Compromise)
Justice Powell analyzed the University's four stated interests:
Reducing the deficit of minorities in medicine: Rejected. (The Court called this "preferring one group for no reason other than race," which is "discrimination for its own sake.")
Remedying "societal discrimination": Rejected. (The Court held that a university cannot fix general societal unfairness; it can only fix its own specific past discrimination.)
Increasing the number of physicians in underserved communities: Rejected. (There was no evidence that minority students would actually go practice in those specific areas.)
Attaining a Diverse Student Body: ACCEPTED. Powell famously ruled that the "attainment of a diverse student body" is a Compelling Interest because of the "academic freedom" protected by the First Amendment.
The "Narrow Tailoring" Problem: Even though "diversity" was a valid goal, the quota (the 16-seat set-aside) was not "narrowly tailored." Powell argued that race could be used as a "plus factor" in an individual's file, but it could not be used to insulate an applicant from comparison with all other candidates.
Conclusion
Yes and No. * The Program was Unconstitutional: The 16-seat quota was struck down because it was not narrowly tailored.
The Principle was Upheld: The Court held that a university may consider race as one of many factors in an admissions process to achieve diversity, provided it does not use a rigid quota system.
Key Takeaways for your 1L Outline
Export to Sheets
Professor's Sidebar
Note for the Final: Bakke was a 4-1-4 decision. Four Justices wanted to strike down the program because race should never be used (Statutory grounds). Four Justices wanted to uphold it using Intermediate Scrutiny (Remedial grounds). Justice Powell sat in the middle, applying Strict Scrutiny but finding Diversitycompelling. This remained the law of the land until it was formally overturned in SFFA v. Harvard in 2023.
Does the distinction between a "16-seat quota" and a "plus factor" feel like a real legal difference, or does it feel like "administrative wordplay" to you?
Justices Brennan, White, Marshall, and Blackmun (Concurring in part, Dissenting in part)
These four Justices wanted to uphold the UC Davis program. Their logic was rooted in the idea that the 14th Amendment's purpose is to eliminate the effects of racial caste.
The Standard: They argued that Intermediate Scrutiny—not Strict Scrutiny—should apply to "benign" racial classifications.
The Logic: They believed that because the program was designed to remedy past societal discrimination and didn't "stigmatize" any group, it should be easier to justify.
Justice Marshall’s Individual Opinion: In a powerful separate opinion, Marshall argued that it was "more than a little ironic" that the Court was using the 14th Amendment (passed to help Black Americans) to strike down a program meant to integrate them into the medical profession.
Justice Blackmun’s Famous Quote: "In order to get beyond racism, we must first take account of race. There is no other way. And in order to treat some persons equally, we must treat them differently."
II. The "Conservative" Bloc: The Statutory View
Justices Stevens, Burger, Stewart, and Rehnquist (Concurring in part, Dissenting in part)
These four Justices wanted to strike down the program, but they famously avoided the Constitutional question altogether.
The Basis: They relied strictly on Title VI of the Civil Rights Act of 1964, which states: "No person... shall, on the ground of race, color, or national origin, be excluded from participation in... any program or activity receiving Federal financial assistance."
The Logic: They argued that the plain language of the statute was "color-blind." Since Bakke was excluded "on the ground of race," the UC Davis program violated the law.
Outcome: Because they found the program violated the statute, they felt the Court didn't need to decide if it also violated the 14th Amendment.
III. Summary of the Split
Justice Powell acted as a bridge between these two groups, creating a "Judgment of the Court" that satisfied pieces of both.
Export to Sheets
Bakke and Amicus Briefs
"Battle of the Amici" and how a single third-party brief transformed the legal landscape of higher education. For your 1L Constitutional Law exam, it is vital to understand that Bakke wasn't just decided on the record of UC Davis; it was decided on the "prestige" of the Harvard Plan.
I. The Amicus Brief: "Friend" or "Propagandist"?
The Bakke case saw an unprecedented surge in amicus participation (58 briefs were filed, the most in history at that time). This sparked a philosophical debate within the Court about the role of outside influence:
The Pro-Amicus Bloc (Black, Brennan, Marshall, Douglas):
Rationale: They viewed the Supreme Court as a "public" institution. Because the Court's rulings affect millions of people not named in the lawsuit, the public should have a voice.
Goal: To prevent the "brethren" from living in a vacuum and keep them connected to the "views and attitudes" of society.
The Anti-Amicus Bloc (Frankfurter):
Rationale: Frankfurter feared the Court would become a "soapbox" or an "advertising medium."
Goal: To preserve the Court as a purely legal body that only decides cases based on the "immediate record parties" and strict legal doctrine, rather than social pressure.
II. The Harvard Brief and the "Plus Factor"
Justice Powell—a Harvard Law alumnus—found the solution to the Bakke deadlock in the amicus brief submitted by Harvard University.
The Harvard Model: Harvard explained that they did not use "quotas." Instead, they treated race as one of many "plus factors" (alongside athletic ability, geographic origin, or musical talent).
The Legal "Hook": Powell used this model to create a middle path. He argued that while the UC Davis quota was unconstitutional, a Harvard-style individualized review was permissible because it didn't insulate any applicant from competition.
III. The Significance of the "Plus Factor" Framework
By adopting the language of an amicus brief, Powell changed the standard for every university in America:
Individualized Consideration: Each applicant must be treated as an individual. Race cannot be the defining feature of the application, but a "tip" on the scale.
No Fixed Numbers: The moment a school says "we need 16 of X," they have moved from a "plus factor" to an unconstitutional quota.
Academic Freedom: Powell tied the "diversity" interest to the First Amendment, arguing that universities have a right to select a student body that will provide the "robust exchange of ideas."
IV. Modern Context: The "Harvard Plan" in 2023
It is a profound historical irony that the "Harvard Plan," which Justice Powell used to save affirmative action in 1978, was the very program struck down in 2023.
Then (1978): The Harvard Plan was the "Gold Standard" of constitutional admissions.
Now (2023): In SFFA v. Harvard, the Court ruled that the "plus factor" system had become too opaque, lacked a "logical end point," and resulted in unconstitutional stereotyping.
Professor's Sidebar for your Outline
When discussing Bakke, don't just focus on Allan Bakke’s scores. Focus on the "Harvard Amicus." It is the ultimate example of how "Elite Institutional Knowledge" can shape Constitutional law. Justice Powell wasn't just looking at the 14th Amendment; he was looking for a way to let universities keep doing what they (the elite schools) thought was best for education.
Does the fact that Justice Powell relied so heavily on his own alma mater’s brief raise any questions for you about "judicial neutrality," or do you see it as a pragmatic way to solve a difficult social problem?
Race Preferences in Employment and Contracting
Craig v. Boren
429 U.S. 190, 97 S. Ct. 451, 50 L. Ed. 2d 397 (1976).
The Facts: Beer, Boys, and Bias
In the mid-1970s, Oklahoma had a curious liquor law. It prohibited the sale of "non-intoxicating" 3.2% beer to males under the age of 21, but allowed females to buy it at age 18.
The state’s justification? Statistical evidence. Oklahoma argued that young men were more likely to drive drunk and get into traffic accidents than young women. Curtis Craig (a male between 18 and 21) and a female beer vendor challenged the law, arguing it violated the Equal Protection Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment.
Issue
Does a state statute that denies the sale of 3.2% beer to males under 21, while allowing the sale to females under 21, constitute unconstitutional discrimination against males in violation of the Equal Protection Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment?
Rule: Intermediate Scrutiny
To withstand a constitutional challenge, classifications by gender must:
Serve important governmental objectives; and
Be substantially related to the achievement of those objectives.
Analysis
The "Important Objective" prong: The Court conceded that traffic safety is indeed an "important" governmental interest. So far, so good for Oklahoma.
The "Substantially Related" prong: This is where the state's case went flat. Justice Brennan, writing for the majority, scrutinized the statistics Oklahoma provided. The data showed that 2% of 18-20-year-old males were arrested for drunk driving, compared to only 0.18% of females in the same age group.
The Court’s Critique: While the disparity was real, the Court found it too thin to justify a broad gender-based prohibition. 2% is a tiny fraction of the male population; punishing the other 98% of young men based on a "gross stereotypical distinction" was not "substantially related" to the goal.
The Loophole: The law only prohibited the sale of the beer to young men, not the possession or consumption, further weakening the argument that the law actually improved road safety.
Conclusion
Yes. The law was unconstitutional. The Court held that the gender-based differential lacked a sufficient "fit" between the means (discriminating against men) and the ends (traffic safety).
ProFessor's Sidebar
Note for your Outline: Before Craig, the Court was using "Rational Basis with Teeth" (see Reed v. Reed). Craig v. Boren is your citation for the birth of Intermediate Scrutiny. It was also a landmark because it proved that the Equal Protection Clause protects men from discrimination as well as women—a point Ruth Bader Ginsburg (who filed an amicus brief here) was very keen on establishing to prove that gender stereotypes hurt everyone.
Justice Powell: The Hesitant Middle
Agreed with the result but was uncomfortable with creating a formal "third tier" of scrutiny.
Key Concern: He worried that by creating "Intermediate Scrutiny," the Court was unnecessarily complicating constitutional law.
The "Reed" Preference: He would have preferred to decide the case simply by using the "rational basis" test from Reed v. Reed, arguing that Oklahoma’s statistics were just too weak to pass even that lower bar.
Justice Stevens: The Standard Skeptic
The "One Standard" Theory: Stevens famously argued that there is really only one Equal Protection Clause. He disliked the "multi-tiered" approach (Rational, Intermediate, Strict).
The "Unfairness" Focus: He focused on the fact that the law penalized an entire class of citizens (young men) based on the conduct of a tiny minority (the 2% who drank and drove). To him, this was simply "objectionable" and unfair.
Justice Stewart: The "Invidious" Label
The Result: He concurred because he felt the law was "totally irrational."
The Reasoning: He didn't think the Court needed a new test; he simply believed the law was "invidious discrimination" that served no legitimate purpose other than to rely on an old stereotype.
The Dissents (The "Leave it to the States" Opinions)
Justice Rehnquist: The "Men Don't Need Help" Dissent
Rehnquist wrote a blistering dissent that focused on two main points:
Men aren't a "Discrete and Insular Minority": He argued that intermediate or strict scrutiny should only be used to protect groups that have historically been discriminated against (like women or racial minorities). Since men held the political power in Oklahoma, he felt they didn't need the Court’s "special protection."
Rational Basis is Enough: He believed the Court should have used the most deferential standard (Rational Basis). Under that standard, Oklahoma’s statistics—however flawed—provided enough of a "rational" reason to allow the state to try and save lives on the highway.
Source of Chaos: He predicted that the "Intermediate" standard would be "vague" and lead to inconsistent rulings in future cases.
Chief Justice Burger: The Judicial Restraint Dissent
Agreed with Rehnquist that the law was not "invidious."
Procedural Grumble: He was annoyed that a beer vendor was allowed to bring the case (standing issues) and felt the Court was "meddling" in state legislative matters that didn't involve a truly "suspect" class.
Do you see how the "substantial relationship" test requires much more evidence than the "rational relationship" test we see in economic cases?
Sex Equality After Craig v. Boren
I. The Shift: From "Intermediate" to "Exceedingly Persuasive"
The Evolution: Starting with Mississippi University for Women v. Hogan (1982), the Court began requiring an "exceedingly persuasive justification" for gender-based laws.
The Debate: Justice Scalia argued this was a "covert" shift to strict scrutiny. The majority maintained it was simply a rigorous application of intermediate scrutiny to ensure laws weren't based on "archaic" stereotypes.
Burden of Proof: The burden rests entirely on the State to show the classification is not only important but also actually serves that purpose (not just a post-hoc rationalization).
II. Mississippi University for Women v. Hogan (1982)
The Facts: MUW (all-female) denied Joe Hogan admission to its nursing program. He was told he could only "audit" classes but not earn a degree.
The State's Argument: Mississippi claimed the policy was "educational affirmative action" meant to compensate women for past discrimination.
The Court’s Ruling (Justice O'Connor):
Anti-Stereotyping: O'Connor ruled that the policy actually hurt women by perpetuating the stereotype that nursing is a "woman's job."
The "Audit" Flaw: The State's claim that men would disrupt the female learning environment was "fatally undermined" by the fact that they let men audit the classes anyway.
The Dissent (Justice Powell): Argued that single-sex education is a "respected choice" and that the Court shouldn't strike down a policy that expands choices for women.
III. J.E.B. v. Alabama ex rel. T.B. (1994)
The Issue: Can the state use peremptory strikes to remove jurors based solely on gender? (Specifically, the state struck all men in a paternity suit).
The Ruling: No. The Court held that gender-based peremptory challenges violate the Equal Protection Clause.
Key Logic: The Court refused to accept the "very stereotype the law condemns"—the idea that men and women have inherently different "outlooks" that make them biased in legal proceedings.
The Scalia Dissent: Argued that the system was "even-handed" because if the state struck men, the defense could strike women. He viewed the ruling as judicial overreach into a long-standing trial tradition.
IV. United States v. Virginia (VMI) (1996)
The Facts: The Virginia Military Institute (VMI) was an all-male public academy. When challenged, Virginia tried to create a separate "leadership" program for women (VWIL) at a different college.
The Ruling (Justice Ginsburg):
Inadequate Remedy: The women's program was not equal in prestige, faculty, or "adversative" training methods.
The Standard: Reaffirmed the "exceedingly persuasive justification" requirement. The state cannot exclude women based on generalizations about what "most women" can or cannot do.
Comparison of Levels of Scrutiny
Export to Sheets
Professor's Sidebar: Notice the trend? The Court is increasingly suspicious of "biological" or "sociological" arguments that suggest men and women are fundamentally different in their professional or civic capabilities. If a state wants to separate the sexes, it better have a reason that doesn't rely on "the way things have always been."
Does the "exceedingly persuasive justification" phrase feel like a clear rule to you, or does it seem "amorphous" like Justice Scalia claimed?
United States v. Virginia
518 U.S. 515, 116 S. Ct. 2264, 135 L. Ed. 2d 735 (1996).
The Facts: The "Adversative" Wall
The Virginia Military Institute (VMI) was the only single-sex public higher education institution in Virginia. It used a "rat line" and an "adversative" method (extreme physical and mental stress) to train "citizen-soldiers." VMI strictly excluded women, claiming their presence would destroy the school’s unique atmosphere and that women were not suited for the rigors of the program.
After a female high school student challenged the policy, the Fourth Circuit ruled against VMI. In response, Virginia proposed a "separate but equal" alternative: the Virginia Women’s Institute for Leadership (VWIL) at Mary Baldwin College. However, VWIL lacked VMI’s prestige, its "adversative" method, its funding, and its influential alumni network.
Issue
Does Virginia’s exclusion of women from a public military academy (VMI) violate the Equal Protection Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment?
If so, does the creation of a separate, female-only leadership program (VWIL) provide a constitutionally sufficient remedy?
Rule: Intermediate Scrutiny (Enhanced)
To justify a gender-based classification, the State must show an "exceedingly persuasive justification."
The classification must serve important governmental objectives.
The discriminatory means must be substantially related to the achievement of those objectives.
The Justification must be genuine: It cannot be a post-hoc rationalization invented for litigation, and it cannot rely on overbroad generalizations about the different talents or preferences of males and females.
Analysis
The Justification: Virginia argued that single-sex education provides a pedagogical benefit (diversity of educational options) and that admitting women would fundamentally alter the "adversative" training.
The Court’s Rejection: Justice Ginsburg wrote that while "diversity" is important, VMI was founded to exclude women, not to provide "diversity" in a broad sense. More importantly, she noted that while "most" women might not like the adversative method, the State cannot exclude all women simply because many might not fit the mold. The law must account for the "talented few" who are capable.
The Remedy (VWIL): The Court compared VMI and VWIL and found them worlds apart. VWIL was a "pale shadow" of VMI. It lacked the same degree-granting power, faculty, and—crucially—the "prestige" and alumni connections that make a VMI degree valuable. In Constitutional Law, "Separate" is almost never "Equal" when the tangible and intangible benefits are so lopsided.
Conclusion
Yes and No.
The exclusion of women was unconstitutional because Virginia failed to provide an "exceedingly persuasive justification" for the total bar.
The VWIL program was an inadequate remedy because it did not provide women with the same opportunities or environment afforded to men at VMI.
The Scalia Dissent (1L Gold)
Justice Scalia issued a lone, scathing dissent. He argued:
Standard of Review: The majority had secretly changed Intermediate Scrutiny into Strict Scrutiny by using the "exceedingly persuasive" language.
Tradition: He believed the Court should respect long-standing traditions (like all-male military schools) rather than following "current social fashion."
Legislative Deference: He felt that if the people of Virginia wanted a single-sex school, the Court had no business stopping them.
Professor's Sidebar
For your final, remember the "VMI Standard": If a state wants to exclude a gender, they cannot rely on "generalizations about what is appropriate for most [men/women]." They must prove that the exclusion is necessary even when considering individuals who don't fit the stereotype.
Does it strike you as ironic that the Court used a case about a "masculine" military school to cement the rights of women to access any public institution?
Sex Equality, Sex Differences and the Question of Gender
I. The "Real Differences" vs. "Stereotypes" Debate
The central tension in modern sex equality law is between Formal Equality (treat everyone exactly the same) and Substantive Equality (acknowledge differences to fix underlying inequalities).
The "Voice" Debate: Carol Gilligan’s work suggested women might have a different "moral voice" focused on care rather than abstract justice.
The VMI Conflict: Virginia tried to use Gilligan's work to justify a "separate but equal" school for women, but the Court rejected this in U.S. v. Virginia, ruling that the state cannot exclude the "exceptional woman" based on generalizations about what "most women" prefer.
Educational Trend: Despite the VMI ruling, single-sex public classrooms grew from <24 in 2002 to 366 in 2022, often justified as a way to address specific gender-based learning gaps.
II. Biology as a "Non-Sex" Classification: Pregnancy
One of the most controversial "loopholes" in Equal Protection history is the treatment of pregnancy.
Geduldig v. Aiello (1974)
The Holding: Excluding pregnancy from a state disability insurance program is not sex discrimination.
The Logic: Justice Stewart famously (and controversially) argued that the law didn't divide people into "men and women," but into "pregnant women" and "non-pregnant persons." Since the second group includes both men and women, the Court applied only Rational Basis review.
The Dissent: Justice Brennan argued this was "dissimilar treatment" based on a characteristic inextricably linked to one sex.
Note: While Geduldig remains the Constitutional rule, Congress effectively overrode its result in the employment context via the Pregnancy Discrimination Act (PDA).
III. "Biological Sanctions" and Statutory Rape
Michael M. v. Superior Court (1981)
The Issue: Can a law punish only the male participant in underage sex?
The Plurality: Upheld the law. Justice Rehnquist argued that because the "harmful consequences" (pregnancy) fall naturally on the female, a male-only criminal penalty "equalizes" the deterrents.
The Dissent: Justice Stevens argued it was irrational to exempt 50% of the potential violators if the goal was truly to prevent the conduct.
IV. The Military and "Similarly Situated"
Rostker v. Goldberg (1981)
The Holding: Upheld a male-only draft registration.
The Logic: Because women were (at the time) legally barred from combat, the Court ruled that men and women were not "similarly situated" for the purpose of a draft.
Critical Shift: Today, since the combat ban has been lifted, the "similarly situated" logic of Rostker is legally fragile.
V. Parental Rights and Citizenship
How the Court views "biological reality" in the context of unwed parents has shifted significantly over time.
1. Nguyen v. INS (2001) – The "Biological Reality" Peak
Rule: Upheld a law making it harder for citizen-fathers (vs. citizen-mothers) to pass citizenship to children born abroad/out-of-wedlock.
Logic: Justice Kennedy argued the mother’s relationship is "verifiable at birth," whereas a father's is not. He claimed acknowledging this was not stereotyping, but "basic biological differences."
2. Sessions v. Morales-Santana (2017) – The Modern Correction
The Shift: Justice Ginsburg struck down a similar residency disparity.
The Logic: She ruled the law was based on the "stunningly anachronistic" stereotype that unwed mothers are the "natural and sole guardians" while fathers care little for their children.
The "Leveling Down" Remedy: Crucially, instead of giving fathers the "better" 1-year rule, the Court took the 1-year rule away from mothers, making everyone follow the stricter 5-year residency rule.
VI. The New Frontier: Transgender Rights and SB1
The Court is currently grappling with whether "Sex Discrimination" includes "Gender Identity."
The Theory: Discriminating against a transgender person is necessarily sex discrimination because the discriminator is reacting to the person's biological sex in relation to their gender identity.
The Precedent: Price Waterhouse v. Hopkins (1989) established that "sex stereotyping" (punishing a woman for not being "feminine" enough) is prohibited.
United States v. Skrmetti (2024/25): This case (concerning bans on gender-affirming care for minors) will likely decide:
Does "Transgender Status" get Intermediate Scrutiny?
Is a ban on gender-affirming care a "sex-based" classification (because it allows testosterone for boys but bans it for girls)?
Summary for the 1L Outline
Export to Sheets
Professor's Sidebar: The "Leveling Down" remedy in Morales-Santana is a vital 1L concept. It shows that the Equal Protection Clause guarantees equality, not necessarily benefits. If a law is unequal, the Court can fix it by making it equally bad for everyone.
Which of these "biological" justifications—pregnancy, combat, or birth presence—feels most like a "real" difference to you, and which feels more like a "stereotype" in disguise?
Sex-Based Purpose and Effect
In Personnel Administrator of Mass. v. Feeney, the Court took the strict rules it developed for race discrimination in Washington v. Davis and applied them to sex discrimination. This case is the reason why many laws that seem "unfair" to women stay on the books—because "unfair" isn't the same as "unconstitutional" in the eyes of the Supreme Court.
Here are your detailed notes on the "Feeney Standard."
I. The Core Legal Standard: Intent, Not Just Impact
The Rule: A facially neutral law (one that doesn't mention "men" or "women") is not unconstitutional just because it happens to hurt one sex more than the other (Disparate Impact).
The Requirement: To trigger heightened scrutiny, a challenger must prove Discriminatory Purpose.
The Definition of "Purpose": Justice Stewart famously defined this as more than just "awareness of consequences." The legislature must have acted "because of," not merely "in spite of," the adverse effects on women.
II. Personnel Administrator of Mass. v. Feeney (1979)
The Facts: The "Absolute" Preference
The Law: Massachusetts gave an "absolute lifetime preference" to veterans for civil service jobs. This meant any qualifying veteran—no matter their score—was hired before any non-veteran.
The Result: At the time, 98.2% of veterans were male. Helen Feeney, a non-veteran woman who scored very high on exams, was repeatedly bumped by lower-scoring male veterans.
The Numbers:
Disadvantaged Men: ~1,867,000
Disadvantaged Women: ~2,954,000
The Majority’s Reasoning (Justice Stewart)
Is it Gender-Based? No. The law distinguishes between "veterans" and "non-veterans," not men and women.
Is it a Pretext? No. Because so many men (nearly 2 million) were also disadvantaged by the law, the Court found it impossible to say the law was a "secret" plan to help men as a class.
Foreseeability is not Intent: Even though the legislature knew women would be excluded (because of federal military limits on women), they didn't pass the law to exclude them. They passed it to help veterans.
III. The Dissent: A "Hierarchy of Gender" (Justice Marshall)
The "Inexorable" Consequence: Marshall argued that when a law's impact is this extreme and foreseeable, the burden should shift to the State to prove sex wasn't a factor.
Historical Context: He pointed out that until 1971, the law actually exempted "traditionally female" jobs (like secretarial work) from the preference. To Marshall, this proved the state was intentionally funneling women into low-level "pink-collar" jobs while reserving high-level roles for men.
Less Discriminatory Alternatives: Marshall argued that if the State really just wanted to help veterans, it could have given them "bonus points" rather than an "absolute" preference that totally shut women out.
IV. Key Takeaways for your 1L Outline
1. The Two-Step Inquiry for Neutral Laws
When a law is neutral on its face but has a lopsided impact:
Step 1: Is the classification truly neutral, or is it a "covert" gender classification?
Step 2: If neutral, does the adverse effect reflect invidious (intentional) discrimination?
2. The "Because Of" Test
This is the most important phrase from the case. To win, a plaintiff must prove the government wanted the bad result to happen. Knowing the bad result would happen (volition/awareness) is legally insufficient.
3. Impact as Evidence
Disparate impact is a "starting point," but unless the impact is so bizarre that it can only be explained by sex (like a "Yick Wo" situation for race), it won't be enough to win.
Professor's Sidebar:
Feeney is often criticized because it ignores how other discriminatory systems (like the military’s historical exclusion of women) feed into "neutral" state laws. For your exam, if you see a policy like "hiring only people with 6-foot-tall parents," remember: that will hurt certain ethnicities more than others. Under Feeney, that’s totally fine—unless you can prove the legislature hates short ethnicities and chose that rule specifically to keep them out.
Do you see the "logic" in Justice Stevens' concurrence? He basically says: "If the law hurts 1.8 million men, how can we say it's a plot to help men?" Does that feel like a fair way to measure discrimination to you?
Sex-Based Preferences: Affirmative Action for Women
This section of your Constitutional Law studies explores the "Mirror Image" problem: When does a law that seems to help women (and hurt men) actually violate the Equal Protection Clause?
The trend here is a shift from "Romantic Paternalism" (the "pedestal that is actually a cage") to a rigorous requirement that any "benign" preference must be based on real economic data, not social stereotypes.
I. The "Lady" vs. "Woman" Distinction
A useful way to organize these cases for your outline is to ask: Is the law helping a "Lady" (reinforcing the stereotype that women are fragile/dependent) or is it helping a "Woman" (remedying actual economic or professional barriers)?
Unconstitutional "Lady" Preferences: Laws based on the assumption that women are financially dependent on husbands or too "delicate" for certain roles (Orr, Wiesenfeld, Goldfarb, Wengler).
Constitutional "Woman" Preferences: Laws that use hard data to prove women were economically disadvantaged in the workforce and provide a direct fix (Webster, Ballard).
II. Case Summaries: The "Benign" Classification Tracker
1. The Financial Dependency Cases (Struck Down)
The Court is highly suspicious of laws that assume a wife is a "dependent" and a husband is a "provider."
Orr v. Orr (1979): Alabama's "husbands only" alimony law was struck down. Since courts already hold individualized hearings to determine financial need, using gender as a "proxy" for need is unnecessary and reinforces stereotypes.
Weinberger v. Wiesenfeld (1975) & Califano v. Goldfarb (1977): Social Security laws that gave benefits automatically to widows but required widowers to prove they were dependent. The Court found this discriminated against female wage earners because their taxes bought less protection for their families than men's taxes did.
Wengler v. Druggists Mutual (1980): Struck down a law requiring widowers (but not widows) to prove physical or mental incapacity to get workers' comp.
2. The Economic Remediation Cases (Upheld)
The Court allows preferences if the government can prove the goal is to fix a specific, documented economic disparity.
Califano v. Webster (1977): Upheld a Social Security formula that allowed women to exclude more "low-earning years" than men. Reason: The law was directly tied to the fact that women, due to historical discrimination, were paid less in the private sector.
Schlesinger v. Ballard (1975): Upheld a Navy policy giving female officers more time to achieve promotion before mandatory discharge. Reason: Because women were barred from combat, they had fewer opportunities for "career-fast-tracking," so the extra time leveled the playing field.
3. The "Old School" Deferential Exception
Kahn v. Shevin (1974): Upheld a Florida tax exemption for widows only. This was decided before the Intermediate Scrutiny standard of Craig v. Boren (1976). Today, many scholars believe Kahn might be decided differently because it relied on the "fair and substantial relation" test rather than "exceedingly persuasive justification."
III. Comparing Race and Sex Affirmative Action
One of the most paradoxical areas of 1L ConLaw is the comparison between race-based and sex-based affirmative action.
Export to Sheets
IV. Key Takeaways for the 1L Outline
The "Actual Purpose" Test: In Wiesenfeld, the Court ruled that the government can't just make up a "benign" reason after they get sued. The "remedial" purpose must have been the actual reason Congress passed the law.
Individualized Hearings vs. Gender Proxies: If the government can easily determine a person's need (like through a tax return or a hearing), they cannot use gender as a shortcut.
The "Double-Edged Sword": Justice Ginsburg's strategy was often to represent men (Wiesenfeld, Craig) to show that gender stereotypes hurt everyone. By winning for men, she successfully dismantled the "paternalistic" laws that kept women in the domestic sphere.
Professor's Sidebar:
Watch out for the "Remedy" in these cases. In many of the Social Security and benefits cases, the Court didn't take the benefit away from women; they "leveled up" by extending the benefit to men. This is the opposite of the "leveling down" we saw in Morales-Santana.
Source: Califano v. Webster, 430 U.S. 313 (1977); Orr v. Orr, 440 U.S. 268 (1979).
Does the distinction between "compensating for lower pay" (Webster) and "assuming a wife is dependent" (Orr) feel clear, or does it seem like the Court is splitting hairs?
Section 4.Discrimination Based on Other Potentially “Suspect” Classifications
Welcome to the next segment of your 1L journey. We are transitioning from Sex to Alienage (citizenship status).
The "Law Wiz" summary for your outline: While the Equal Protection Clause protects "persons" (not just citizens), the Supreme Court handles alienage like a legal seesaw. When States discriminate, the Court is very suspicious; when the Federal Government discriminates, the Court is very deferential.
I. The Fundamental Split: State vs. Federal Power
The standard of review for alienage depends entirely on who is doing the discriminating:
Export to Sheets
II. State Discrimination: The General Rule (Strict Scrutiny)
In Graham v. Richardson (1971), the Court declared that classifications based on alienage are inherently suspect.
Welfare Benefits: States cannot deny welfare to legal aliens (Graham).
Professional Licenses: States cannot bar aliens from being lawyers (In re Griffiths).
Menial Civil Service: States cannot bar aliens from all civil service jobs, like sanitation or clerical work (Sugarman v. Dougall).
III. The "Political Function" Exception (The Loophole)
The Court carved out an exception: If a job involves discretionary power over public policy or the "heart of representative government," the State only needs a Rational Basis to exclude aliens.
Police Officers: Upheld exclusion. Police have high discretion and "clothe" the state's power (Foley v. Connelie).
Public School Teachers: Upheld exclusion. Teachers influence the "social responsibilities" and "civic values" of future voters (Ambach v. Norwick).
Probation Officers: (Related to Foley) Also upheld as a political function.
LIMIT - Notaries Public: Struck down. This job is "clerical and ministerial," not a high-level political function (Bernal v. Fainter).
IV. Federal Discrimination: Broad Deference
Because the Federal Government has the power to admit or exclude people from the country, they have much more "breathing room" to create rules for them.
Medicare Eligibility: Congress can require aliens to live in the U.S. for 5 years before getting benefits, even though a State could not (Mathews v. Diaz).
The "Hampton" Nuance: In Hampton v. Mow Sun Wong, the Court struck down a ban on aliens in federal jobs—not because the ban was unconstitutional, but because the Civil Service Commission didn't have the authority to make that call. The Court hinted that if the President or Congress had signed the order, it likely would have been upheld.
V. Preemption: The "Hidden" Theory
Many scholars (and Justice Brennan in Toll v. Moreno) argue that these cases aren't just about Equal Protection; they are about the Supremacy Clause.
The Logic: Since the Federal Government decides who gets to be a resident alien, a State cannot add "extra burdens" (like higher tuition or no welfare) that Congress didn't intend.
Example: Maryland could not deny "in-state" tuition to certain visa holders because it interfered with federal immigration policy (Toll v. Moreno).
VI. Undocumented Aliens vs. Legal Residents
General Rule: Undocumented aliens are not a suspect class. Most state/federal restrictions on them receive only Rational Basis review.
The Plyler Exception: The Court used a "heightened" rational basis to strike down a Texas law barring undocumented children from public schools.
Reasoning: Children aren't responsible for their status, and creating a "permanent underclass" of uneducated people is a disaster for society (Plyler v. Doe).
Key Takeaways for the Final Exam
Spot the Actor: If it's a State law, start with Strict Scrutiny.
Check the Job: Is the state law about a "political function" (cop, teacher)? If yes, drop to Rational Basis.
Federal Law? Move straight to Rational Basis (Deference).
Immutability Debate: Remember Justice Rehnquist’s dissent—he argued that since an alien can eventually become a citizen (it’s not "unalterable" like race), it shouldn’t get strict scrutiny at all.
Professor's Sidebar: > Think of the "Political Function" exception as the "Political Community" rule. If you aren't a citizen, the Court says the State can legally keep you out of the "clubhouse" where the rules of society are made (the voting booth, the police force, the teacher's lounge).
How do you feel about the Court's distinction between a Lawyer (Strict Scrutiny—must admit aliens) and a Police Officer (Rational Basis—can exclude aliens)? Does one feel more "political" than the other to you?
Disability, Age, Poverty
thought In this final section of your Equal Protection outline, we tackle the "Non-Suspect" classifications: Age, Disability, and Poverty.
Even though these traits feel "immutable" (you can't change your birth date or always cure a disability), the Court has famously refused to grant them the same "Strict" or "Intermediate" status as Race or Sex. However, as you’ll see in the Cleburne case, the Court sometimes uses "Rational Basis with Teeth" to strike down laws that seem born of pure "animus" or prejudice.
I. Why These Aren't "Suspect" Classes
The Court generally rejects heightened scrutiny for these groups for several reasons:
The "Slippery Slope": If the Court protected every group that is "powerless" to change a trait, almost every law (which inevitably draws lines) would require strict scrutiny.
Legitimate Regulation: The Court believes that age and disability often actually relate to a person’s ability to perform a task (unlike race), making "Rational Basis" a more appropriate fit for the legislature.
II. The Cleburne Standard: "Rational Basis with Teeth"
City of Cleburne v. Cleburne Living Center, Inc. (1985)
The Facts: The city of Cleburne, Texas, denied a special use permit for a group home for the mentally disabled. The city claimed it was worried about the "fears" of neighbors, the "harassment" by students from a nearby school, and the home being located on a "flood plain."
The Issue: Is "mental retardation" (the terminology used at the time) a "quasi-suspect" classification requiring intermediate scrutiny?
The Holding: No. The Court officially held that disability is not a quasi-suspect class.
The "But": Even under Rational Basis Review, the Court struck the law down.
The Logic: The Court found that the city’s justifications were "purely irrational" and based on "irrational prejudice" or "neighborhood fear." Because the city allowed other high-density buildings (like fraternities and nursing homes) in the same area, singling out the disabled home proved the city was acting out of animus, not legitimate safety concerns.
III. Age and Poverty (The "Low Scrutiny" Tier)
1. Age: Mass. Board of Retirement v. Murgia (1976)
The Holding: Upheld a law requiring police officers to retire at age 50.
The Logic: Applied Rational Basis. The Court noted that we all "age," so it isn't a "discrete and insular minority" situation. Since physical ability declines with age, the line drawn at 50 was "rational" enough for the state's interest in a fit police force.
2. Poverty / Wealth: San Antonio Indep. School Dist. v. Rodriguez (1973)
The Holding: Poverty is not a suspect classification.
The Logic: The Court refused to apply strict scrutiny to a school funding system based on local property taxes (which favored wealthy districts). The Court argued that the "poor" are not a unified group that has been subjected to a history of purposeful discrimination in the same way as racial groups.
IV. Summary Table for your Outline
Export to Sheets
V. Key Takeaways for the 1L Exam
Spotting "Animus": If you see a fact pattern where a city allows a "frat house" but bans a "disability home," use the Cleburne analysis. Even under Rational Basis, if the only reason for the law is "dislike" of a group, the law fails.
The "Real" Difference Defense: In Age and Disability cases, the government will almost always win if they can show a physical/biological reason for the rule (e.g., "pilots must have 20/20 vision").
Fundamental Rights vs. Suspect Class: Remember that even if a group isn't "suspect" (like the poor), a law might still get strict scrutiny if it affects a Fundamental Right (like the right to vote or travel).
Professor's Sidebar:
Cleburne is a bit of a "judicial ghost." The Court says, "We aren't giving this group special protection," and then they proceed to protect them anyway. It's the Court’s way of saying: "We don't want to make a new rule for everyone, but we know a bully when we see one."
Source: City of Cleburne v. Cleburne Living Center, Inc., 473 U.S. 432 (1985).
Does it feel consistent to you that the Court protects "Aliens" with Strict Scrutiny but only gives "the Disabled" Rational Basis review? If you were the professor, how would you justify that?
City of Cleburne v. Cleburne Living Center, Inc.
473 U.S. 432, 105 S. Ct. 3249, 87 L. Ed. 2d 313 (1985).
The Facts: The Home That Wasn't Allowed
In 1980, the Cleburne Living Center (CLC) sought to open a group home for thirteen men and women with intellectual disabilities. The home would be supervised 24 hours a day. The City of Cleburne had a zoning ordinance that required a special use permit for "hospitals for the insane, feeble-minded, or alcoholics."
The City Council held a hearing and then denied the permit. However, the same zoning area allowed boarding houses, fraternity houses, and nursing homes without a special permit. The City justified its denial by citing:
The "fears" and "negative attitudes" of nearby property owners.
The home’s proximity to a junior high school (fear that students might harass the residents).
The home being located on a "100-year flood plain."
The number of people living in the house (size).
IRAC Analysis
Issue
Is "mental retardation" (intellectual disability) a "quasi-suspect" classification that triggers Intermediate Scrutiny?
Does the City of Cleburne's denial of the special use permit violate the Equal Protection Clause?
Rule: Rational Basis (Purported)
Level of Scrutiny: The Court held that individuals with intellectual disabilities are not a suspect or quasi-suspect class. Therefore, the law only needs to be rationally related to a legitimate state interest.
The "Animus" Limitation: Even under Rational Basis, a law cannot be based on a "bare desire to harm a politically unpopular group." Private biases or "irrational prejudice" are not legitimate state interests.
Analysis
The Rejection of Heightened Scrutiny: Justice White argued that because the disabled are a large and diverse group with different needs, the legislature needs "technical" flexibility to help them. He also noted that since lawmakers had already passed protective legislation (like the Rehabilitation Act), the group was not "politically powerless."
Applying "The Teeth": The Court then looked at the City's reasons and found them completely inconsistent.
Neighbor's Fears: The Court ruled that "vague, undifferentiated fears" of neighbors are never a legitimate reason to discriminate.
The School: The Court found it absurd to assume students would harass residents, noting that many disabled children already attended that very school.
Flood Plain & Size: If the city was truly worried about flooding or density, why did they allow nursing homes or fraternities in the same spot? The under-inclusiveness of the law proved the City's "reasons" were pretexts for bias.
Conclusion
No. Disability is not a quasi-suspect class.
Yes. The permit denial was unconstitutional because it rested on an irrational prejudice against the mentally disabled, failing even the most deferential "Rational Basis" test.
The Concurrence / Dissent (The "Call It Like It Is" Opinion)
Justice Marshall (joined by Brennan and Blackmun) concurred in part but disagreed with the Court's refusal to label the group "quasi-suspect." He argued that the Court was clearly not applying "Rational Basis." He famously remarked that the majority was using a "searching" review while pretending it was "deferential," which creates confusion for lower courts.
Professor's Sidebar for the 1L Exam
The "Cleburne Trap": On an exam, if a professor gives you a fact pattern where a group is being treated differently for "safety" reasons, but other similar groups are exempted from the rule, you are in Cleburneterritory. You must argue: "While the Court typically uses Rational Basis for this group, the lack of fit between the means and the ends suggests the law is actually motivated by animus, which is never a legitimate government interest."
Source: City of Cleburne v. Cleburne Living Center, Inc., 473 U.S. 432 (1985).
Does it feel like a "win" for the plaintiffs that they won the case, even though the Court refused to give their group "protected status" for future cases?
WEEK 14
Monday—April 13, 2026
CHAPTER 10
Congress’s Civil Rights Enforcement Powers
I. The State Action Dichotomy: The "No State Shall" Constraint
Professor Fisher emphasizes the precise textual exegesis of the Constitution. The Fourteenth Amendment does not grant a universal right against discrimination; it imposes a specific restraint on the sovereign.
The Textual Anchor: The prohibitions of § 1 of the Fourteenth Amendment are explicitly addressed to the state ("No State shall").
The Essential Dichotomy: There is a rigid legal barrier between State Action, which is subject to constitutional scrutiny, and Private Conduct, which—regardless of how discriminatory or wrongful—is shielded from the Fourteenth Amendment’s reach.
The Regulatory Blur: In the modern administrative state, the line between private and public has become "blurred" as government involvement in the private sector becomes more pervasive.
Fisher’s Focus (The Inquiry): The core legal question is: At what point does the government's involvement with a private actor "trigger" constitutional restraints, or require the government to withdraw its support?.
II. The Congressional Enforcement Power: Sections 5, 2, and 2
While the courts interpret the limits of the Fourteenth Amendment, the Constitution grants Congress affirmative power to protect civil rights through "appropriate legislation".
Source of Power: The Thirteenth, Fourteenth, and Fifteenth Amendments each contain an enforcement clause granting Congress the power to enforce the amendment by appropriate legislation.
Competing Bases for Power:
The Commerce Clause: Historically used to forbid private racial discrimination (e.g., the Civil Rights Act of 1964).
The Reconstruction Amendments: These represent a more "far-reaching" power specifically born out of a constitutional concern with racial discrimination.
The Structural Conflict: You must distinguish between the Court’s role in defining constitutional limits and Congress’s role in enforcing them.
🏛️ Professor Fisher’s "Virtual Auditor" Notes
Fisher’s Checklist for Class:
Textualism: Always start with the words "No State shall." If there is no state action, the Fourteenth Amendment analysis ends immediately.
Institutional Competence: Be prepared to argue whether the Court or Congress is the more appropriate body to address "seemingly private" conduct.
The "Slipped Link" Trap: Watch for scenarios where a private entity performs a "public function." Fisher will expect you to cite the specific test used to determine if the private entity has effectively become an "arm of government".
🎓 1L Exam Tip (Short & Happy Method)
When analyzing a ConLaw hypo involving a private club or business, run the Standing check first. If the defendant is purely private, you cannot win on a 14th Amendment claim unless you prove State Action (e.g., significant encouragement or entwinement). The "Why": The Court maintains this wall to protect a sphere of individual liberty and state autonomy, preventing the Constitution from swallowing all private interaction.

Section 1.The Civil Rights Statutes of the Reconstruction Era
Section 2.The Requirement of State Action
Civil Rights Cases
109 U.S. 3, 3 S. Ct. 18, 27 L. Ed. 835 (1883).
FACTS: Congress passed the Civil Rights Act of 1875, which prohibited racial discrimination in "public accommodations," including inns, public conveyances (trains/boats), and theaters. The Act imposed both criminal and civil penalties for violations. This decision consolidated five separate cases where African-American citizens were excluded from hotels, theaters, and railroads by private owners. None of the exclusions were directly mandated by a specific state law; they were the " wrongful acts of individuals".
ISSUE: Does Congress have the constitutional authority under the 13th or 14th Amendments to prohibit racial discrimination by private individuals and corporations in public accommodations?.
I — ISSUE
The central legal question is whether the Enforcement Clauses (Section 2 of the 13th Amendment and Section 5 of the 14th Amendment) grant Congress the "primary and direct" power to regulate the conduct of private citizens, or whether that power is limited to correcting official State Action.
R — RULE
The 14th Amendment Rule: The 14th Amendment is addressed exclusively to State Action ("No State shall"). It prohibits "State action of a particular character," not the "individual invasion of individual rights". Congressional power under Section 5 is purely corrective—it can only nullify or remedy wrongful State laws or acts.
The 13th Amendment Rule: While the 13th Amendment applies to private conduct (it is an "absolute declaration" against slavery), Congress’s power is limited to abolishing the "badges and incidents of slavery".
A — APPLICATION
14th Amendment Analysis: The Court finds the 1875 Act unconstitutional because it "steps into the domain of local jurisprudence" by laying down rules for private conduct without reference to any State action. Bradley argues that if a private person commits a wrong unsupported by State law, it is a "private wrong" or a "crime" to be handled by State law, not Federal constitutional law.
13th Amendment Analysis: The Court rejects the argument that being denied a hotel room is a "badge of servitude". Bradley famously asserts that a person who has emerged from slavery must eventually cease to be the "special favorite of the laws" and take the rank of a "mere citizen" subject to ordinary state litigation for private wrongs.
Harlan’s Dissent (The Counter-Strike): Justice Harlan argues the majority's view is "narrow and artificial". He contends that:
Public Functions: Innkeepers and railroads exercise "quasi-public functions" under state license and are therefore "agents of the State".
Affirmative Rights: The 14th Amendment’s grant of citizenship is an affirmative right that Congress can protect directly.
C — CONCLUSION
The Supreme Court held that Sections 1 and 2 of the Civil Rights Act of 1875 are unconstitutional. Congress cannot use the Reconstruction Amendments to regulate purely private discrimination; such conduct remains the exclusive "domain of local [State] jurisprudence".
🏛️ Professor Fisher’s "Virtual Auditor" Notes
Fisher’s "Checkmate" Strategy: Bradley’s logic creates a "catch-22" for 1L students. If the State has "just laws" on the books, Bradley says the federal government has no business interfering in a private wrong. If the State has "bad laws," Congress can only correct those laws, not take over the state's role in policing individuals.
The Commerce Clause Hint: Look at page 911. Bradley leaves the door open: Could Congress use the Commerce Power to regulate these same railroads? (Fisher will expect you to know that 80 years later, in Heart of Atlanta Motel, Congress finally used that exact "Article I" side-door to do what the 14th Amendment couldn't).
Exam Tip: If you see a private actor on an exam, run the "Bradley Corrective Test." Ask: Is Congress trying to fix a bad state law (Legal), or is it trying to create a new federal law for private citizens (Illegal per Civil Rights Cases)?

The Scope and Limits of State Action After the Civil Rights Cases
I. Policy Underpinnings of the State Action Requirement
Professor Fisher will expect you to justify the State Action Doctrine using the two pillars mentioned in your notes:
Federalism: Preserving the prerogatives of States to govern their own citizens through local tort, criminal, and common carrier laws rather than federal oversight.
Individual Liberty: Maintaining a "private sphere" where individuals can act free from the rigid constraints of constitutional norms (e.g., the right to choose guests in one's home).
II. The "Public Function" Exception
This doctrine holds that if a private entity exercises powers "traditionally and exclusively" reserved to the State, it becomes a state actor subject to the Fourteenth Amendment.
A. The "Company Town" (Marsh v. Alabama)
The Rule: A private corporation owning a town cannot infringe on First Amendment rights if the town has all the characteristics of a municipality.
Key Phrase: "The more an owner, for his advantage, opens up his property for use by the public in general, the more do his rights become circumscribed by the constitutional and statutory rights of those who use it".
B. The Shopping Center Deviation
History: Logan Valley briefly held shopping centers were the "functional equivalent" of business districts.
The Fisher "Checkmate": Logan Valley is no longer the law. Per Hudgens v. NLRB, shopping center owners are not engaged in state action.
C. Parks and Municipal Services (Evans v. Newton)
The Rule: A park serves a "municipal" nature similar to fire or police departments.
The Hold: Even with private trustees, a park "traditionally serves the community" and cannot be operated on a segregated basis.
D. The White Primary Cases
This is a classic Fisher exam area—how "private" political parties can be state actors:
Evolution: Texas tried delegating primary power to party committees (Nixon v. Condon) and then to the party convention (Grovey v. Townsend) to avoid "state action".
The Hold: In Smith v. Allwright, the Court found that selecting nominees for the general ballot is a delegation of a state function, making the party an agency of the State.
The Limit (Terry v. Adams): Even "voluntary clubs" (the Jaybirds) are state actors if they operate as an "auxiliary" that effectively chooses public officials.
III. The Modern "Exclusive Prerogative" Limitation
In the 1970s, the Court (led by Justice Rehnquist) "cabined" this doctrine:
Jackson v. Metropolitan Edison: A private utility is not a state actor because supplying utility service is not a power "traditionally exclusively reserved to the State".
Flagg Bros. v. Brooks: A warehouseman’s lien (private debt resolution) is not state action because there are many historical ways to resolve such conflicts besides the State.
IV. State Enforcement of Private Agreements
This represents a different "side-door" to state action: when the State's Judiciary enforces a private contract.
The Scenario: Private owners sign "racially restrictive covenants" (contracts) agreeing not to sell to minorities.
The Trigger: When a neighbor sues to enforce that contract in a State Court, the Court's injunction constitutes the State Action necessary to trigger the Fourteenth Amendment.
🏛️ Professor Fisher’s "Virtual Auditor" Notes
Fisher’s "Exclusive" Trap: Pay attention to the word "Exclusively" in the modern test. It is not enough that a private company does something the government also does (like education or utilities). To be a state actor under the Public Function test, the entity must do something only the government traditionally does (like holding elections or running a town).
🎓 1L Exam Tip (Short & Happy Method)
When analyzing a "Public Function" hypo:
Is it an election? (Always State Action per Smith and Terry).
Is it a town? (State Action per Marsh).
Is it a shopping center or utility? (No State Action per Hudgens and Jackson).
Is it a private contract being enforced by a judge? (State Action per the upcoming Shelley v. Kraemer analysis).
Shelley v. Kraemer
334 U.S. 1, 68 S. Ct. 836, 92 L. Ed. 1161 (1948).
FACTS: In 1911, thirty out of thirty-nine property owners in a St. Louis neighborhood signed a racially restrictive covenant. The contract stated that no part of the property should be occupied by "people of the Negro or Mongolian race" for fifty years. In 1945, the Shelleys, an African-American family, purchased a parcel of land in the neighborhood without knowledge of the covenant. Owners of other restricted properties (the Kraemers) brought suit to prevent the Shelleys from taking possession of the property. The Supreme Court of Missouri directed the trial court to grant the relief, effectively evicting the Shelleys.
ISSUE: Does the judicial enforcement of a private, racially restrictive covenant by a state court constitute "State Action" under the Fourteenth Amendment?
I — ISSUE
Whether the Equal Protection Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment inhibits judicial enforcement by state courts of restrictive covenants based on race or color.
R — RULE
Private Conduct: The Fourteenth Amendment "erects no shield against merely private conduct, however discriminatory or wrongful".
State Action: The Amendment is violated only when the discrimination is supported by State authority in the form of laws, customs, or judicial/executive proceedings.
Judicial Action: Action of state courts and judicial officers in their official capacities is to be regarded as action of the State.
A — APPLICATION
The Agreement: The restrictive covenants, standing alone, were purely private contracts. Had the neighbors voluntarily adhered to them, there would be no state action.
The Intervention: The "State Action" occurred when the respondents turned to the power of the state courts to enforce the terms of the agreement.
The Deprivation: By granting the injunction, the state court used its coercive power to deprive the Shelleys of their property rights solely because of their race. The Court noted that but for the active intervention of the state courts, the Shelleys would have been free to occupy the property.
The Reasoning: The Court rejected the argument that because the judges were merely enforcing a private contract, they were "neutral." Judicial action is never neutral when it validates a racially discriminatory outcome that a State legislature would be forbidden from enacting into law.
C — CONCLUSION
The Supreme Court reversed the state court decisions. It held that in granting judicial enforcement of the restrictive covenants, the States denied the petitioners the equal protection of the laws. While the covenants themselves were not invalid as private contracts, they were unenforceable in any court of law.
🏛️ Professor Fisher’s "Virtual Auditor" Notes
Fisher’s "Institutional" Warning: Students often over-read Shelley. If Shelley meant that any court enforcement of a private preference is state action, the "private sphere" would disappear.
The Fisher Distinction: > Fisher will ask: "If I kick a trespasser out of my house because of their race, and the police arrest them, is that Shelley state action?"
The Answer: Most courts say No. In Shelley, the state court was asked to nullify a willing sale between a white seller and a Black buyer. The state stepped in to thwart a private transaction. In a simple trespass case, the state is enforcing a neutral property right (the right to exclude), not a specific racial covenant.
🎓 1L Exam Tip (Short & Happy Method)
When you see a court order in a property hypo:
Identify the underlying right: Is the judge enforcing a neutral law (like trespass or breach of contract) or a discriminatory intent embedded in the contract?
Apply Shelley: If the court is the necessary engine that makes the discrimination work, you have State Action.
State Action After Shelley v. Kraemer
I. The Scope and Limits of Shelley v. Kraemer
The Court has struggled to define the boundary of Shelley. If every court enforcement is state action, the private sphere vanishes. 
Extension to Damages: In Barrows v. Jackson, the Court extended Shelley to prevent state courts from awarding money damages for the breach of a racially restrictive covenant. 
The Reverter Exception (Evans v. Abney): * Facts: A trust for a "whites-only" park failed because the restriction was illegal. The state court ruled the property reverted to the heirs by operation of law. 
Holding: No state action. The court was merely effectuating the testator's intent by "eliminating the park itself," a loss shared equally by all races. 
Fisher Note: Abney is a "passive" enforcement of a private intent that ends the public service altogether, whereas Shelley was "affirmative" enforcement of discrimination within an active transaction. 
Testamentary Trusts (Pennsylvania v. Board of Trusts):
State action was found where a city served as a trustee for a "whites-only" school because the board was an agency of the State. 
II. Significant State Involvement: The "Symbiotic Relationship"
Under Burton v. Wilmington Parking Authority, private discrimination becomes state action if the state and private actor are sufficiently intertwined.
The Symbiotic Test:
The restaurant was located in a publicly owned parking building.
The state (Authority) and restaurant conferred mutual benefits; specifically, the restaurant's profits (built on discrimination) contributed to the success of the state agency.
Holding: The state "elected to place its power, property and prestige behind the admitted discrimination."
Justice Stewart’s "Rule of Law" Alternative:
Stewart focused not on the lease, but on the state court's interpretation of a law that authorized businesses to exclude people based on color.
Fisher Strategy: Stewart’s approach is a "neutrality" check—did the state create a law that specifically permits racial classification?
III. State "Encouragement" of Discrimination
In Reitman v. Mulkey, the Court addressed whether a state "encourages" discrimination by changing its laws.
Proposition 14: California voters amended the state constitution to prohibit the state from interfering with a person's "absolute discretion" to refuse to sell or rent property.
Holding: This was unconstitutional state action. The Court found the intent was to "authorize" and "encourage" private racial discrimination by making the right to discriminate part of the State's basic charter.
Harlan’s Dissent: Argued this was merely a "repeal" of a law. A state's failure to prohibit discrimination is not the same as state-sponsored discrimination.
IV. The Licensing Limitation (Moose Lodge v. Irvis)
The Court "cabined" the doctrine here, refusing to find state action based solely on a state license.
Rule: Detailed state regulation or "state-furnished services" (water, electricity, police) do not "significantly involve" the state in private discrimination.
Distinction from Burton: There was no "symbiotic relationship" or lease of public property here.
The "Emasculation" Argument: Justice Rehnquist argued that if licensing were state action, every private entity would be subject to constitutional norms, destroying the private conducting distinction.
V. The "Cabining" of Public Function (Jackson v. Metropolitan Edison)
This is the case your notes conclude with—a critical limit on the "Public Function" test.
Facts: A private utility company (Metropolitan Edison) cut off a customer's service without a hearing. The customer sued under 42 U.S.C. § 1983.
The Test: To be a state actor, the private entity must exercise powers "traditionally exclusively reserved to the State."
Holding: Supplying utility service (electricity) is not an exclusive prerogative of the state. Therefore, no state action occurred.
🏛️ Professor Fisher’s "Virtual Auditor" Checkmate Summary
Export to Sheets
Exam Tip: In § 1983 litigation, always start with the Jackson "Exclusivity" bar. If the private actor's job isn't historically reserved only to the state (like elections or running a town), your state action argument likely fails.
Jackson v. Metropolitan Edison Co.
419 U.S. 345, 95 S. Ct. 449, 42 L. Ed. 2d 477 (1974).
FACTS: Metropolitan Edison was a privately owned and operated utility corporation. It held a certificate of public convenience from the Pennsylvania Public Utility Commission, granting it a near-monopoly to deliver electricity in its service area. The plaintiff, Catherine Jackson, had her service terminated for non-payment without notice or a hearing. She sued under 42 U.S.C. § 1983, claiming the utility’s action was "State Action" because of the extensive state regulation and the public nature of providing electricity.
ISSUE: Does the termination of service by a heavily regulated private utility with a state-granted monopoly constitute "State Action" under the Fourteenth Amendment?
I — ISSUE
Whether there is a sufficiently close nexus between the State and the challenged action of the regulated entity so that the action of the latter may be fairly treated as that of the State itself.
R — RULE
State Action Requirement: The 14th Amendment applies only to the State, not to private entities.
Public Function Test (Refined): State action exists only if a private entity exercises powers "traditionally exclusively reserved to the State".
Nexus Test: Mere state regulation, even if "pervasive," or the grant of a monopoly, does not convert private action into state action.
A — APPLICATION
Public Function: The Court, per Justice Rehnquist, held that while utility service is a "public interest," the supplying of electricity has not been traditionally and exclusively a government function. Many private companies have historically provided such services.
Monopoly Power: The Court found that a state-granted monopoly does not make the entity's every move "State Action," especially where the state was not involved in the specific decision to terminate service.
State Approval: Even though the utility filed its general termination procedures with the state commission, the commission’s failure to overturn them was not "active encouragement" or approval by the State.
Dissent (Marshall): Justice Marshall argued that the "essential nature" of the service, the monopoly status, and the state regulation together created a symbiotic relationship similar to Burton.
C — CONCLUSION
The Supreme Court held there was no state action. The termination was a private business decision not attributable to the State of Pennsylvania, even if the state regulated the industry.
🏛️ Professor Fisher’s "Virtual Auditor" Notes
Fisher’s "Checkmate" Strategy: Professor Fisher will likely ask you to compare this to Burton. In Burton, the State profited from the restaurant’s lease (symbiosis). In Jackson, the state merely regulated the utility. Regulation  Symbiosis.
The Key Word is "Exclusively": This is the death knell for most Public Function arguments. If a student says, "But the government could provide electricity," Fisher will respond, "Yes, but have they always and only been the ones to do it?" If the answer is no, the claim fails the Jackson test.
Exam Tip: When analyzing a § 1983 claim against a private entity (like a private prison or hospital), you must identify if the function is "traditionally exclusively reserved to the state". Note that "running an election" passes this test (Terry), but "providing social services" or "utilities" usually fails (Jackson).
The State Action Doctrine After Moose Lodge and Jackson
I. The Limits of "Authorization" and "Encouragement"
