Professor Fisher will emphasize that the State is not responsible for everything it permits.
State Acquiescence is Not State Action (Flagg Bros. v. Brooks):
The Rule: A state's "mere acquiescence" in a private action—such as a warehouseman selling goods under the UCC—does not convert that act into state action.
The Stevens Dissent: Warned that this "permission vs. compulsion" distinction could allow states to authorize "outrageous" self-help (like repossessing goods from private homes) without constitutional check.
Subsidies and Regulation Are Not Enough (Blum v. Yaretsky & Rendell-Baker v. Kohn):
The Nexus Test: Constitutional standards are only invoked when the State is responsible for the specific conduct complained of.
Nursing Homes (Blum): Private nursing homes were not state actors even though they were 90% state-funded and heavily regulated. The transfer decisions were based on private medical judgments, not state standards.
Private Schools (Rendell-Baker): A school funded almost entirely by public money was not a state actor when firing employees. It was treated like a "private contractor" whose business depends on the government.
II. The "Negative Liberty" Shield (DeShaney)
This is a critical "Institutional Competence" case for Fisher’s class.
No Affirmative Duty to Protect (DeShaney v. Winnebago County):
The Fact Pattern: Social workers failed to intervene despite reports of child abuse, leading to Joshua DeShaney's permanent brain damage.
The Holding: The Due Process Clause is a limitation on State power, not a guarantee of safety from private actors (like a father).
The "Special Relationship" Exception: An affirmative duty to protect arises only when the State takes a person into custody (e.g., prison or mental health facility).
The Blackmun Dissent: Famously cried "Poor Joshua!" and argued that state intervention had triggered a duty to aid once the danger was known.
III. When State Action Is Found: The "Joint Participation" Exceptions
Despite the trend of limiting the doctrine, several "side-doors" remain open.
Joint Participation with Officials (Lugar v. Edmondson Oil Co.):
The Distinction: While Flagg Bros. involved a private sale, Lugar involved a private creditor using a state clerk and Sheriff to seize property.
The Rule: Invoking state-created procedures that require the active participation of state officials (like a Sheriff) constitutes state action.
Courtroom Forums (Edmonson v. Leesville Concrete Co.):
The Fact Pattern: A private litigant used peremptory challenges to exclude Black jurors.
The Holding: Peremptory challenges are state action because they involve "overt, significant participation" of the judge and occur within the "quintessential governmental body" of the court.
The "Entwinement" Test (Brentwood Academy):
The Holding: A private athletic association was a state actor because of the "public entwinement" in its management (84% of members were public schools, and employees used the state retirement system).
IV. Comparative Context: Vertical vs. Horizontal
The U.S. "Vertical" Model: Constitutional rights generally regulate only state conduct.
The "Horizontal" Model: Nations like South Africa and Ireland allow the Constitution to bind private "natural or juristic persons" directly.
🏛️ Professor Fisher’s "Virtual Auditor" Notes
Fisher’s "Checkmate" Strategy: For the exam, you must distinguish between Custody and Knowledge. Per DeShaney, the State's knowledge of a private danger is legally irrelevant. Only the State's physical limitation of an individual's freedom (Custody) creates a duty.
The § 1983 Nexus Drill: If a private party discriminates, ask:
Is a Sheriff or Judge helping them? (Yes  Lugar/Edmonson  State Action). 2. Is the State just letting it happen? (Yes  Flagg Bros.  NO State Action).
Is it a medical/professional judgment? (Yes  Blum  NO State Action).
Exam Tip: In any tort-like ConLaw hypo (like DeShaney), emphasize that the harm was inflicted by a private actor. The State's failure to act is almost never a constitutional violation unless the victim was in a prison or state-run hospital.
Section 3.Congressional Power to Reach Private Interference with Constitutional Rights
I. The Statutory Framework: Criminal & Civil Pairs
Congress has provided two distinct "surgical tools" for civil rights enforcement, divided by the presence or absence of state action.
1. The "Color of Law" Group (Requires State Nexus)
These provisions are the statutory enforcement of the 14th Amendment's "State Action" requirement.
Criminal: 18 U.S.C. § 242 covers deprivations of rights occurring "under color of law".
Civil: 42 U.S.C. § 1983 provides the civil remedy for constitutional violations committed by state actors or those acting under color of state law.
2. The "Private Conspiracy" Group (No Facial State Nexus)
These statutes target private behavior directly, raising significant constitutional questions regarding the 14th Amendment.
Criminal: 18 U.S.C. § 241 targets conspiracies by two or more persons to interfere with federal rights.
Civil: 42 U.S.C. § 1985(3) provides civil damages for private conspiracies to deprive persons of equal protection or privileges and immunities.
II. The Constitutional Source of Power: 13th vs. 14th
The extent to which Congress can reach private actors depends heavily on which Amendment they invoke as their source of power.
1. The 14th Amendment: The "State Action" Hurdle
The Conflict: Because 14th Amendment rights (Due Process/Equal Protection) apply only to state interferences, it is unclear if Congress can use § 5 to punish private actors.
The Problem: Can §§ 241 and 1985(3) reach private citizens who interfere with 14th Amendment rights? The Court in Guest discussed this but left it unresolved.
Expansion: Some "seemingly private" behavior is reachable if the Court finds a state nexus (e.g., Public Function or Symbiosis), but Congress’s power to go beyond the Court's definition of state action is a point of deep tension.
2. The 13th Amendment: The Private Conduct "Green Light"
Absolute Power: Unlike the 14th, the 13th Amendment is not limited to state action; it applies to private interferences generally.
Jones v. Mayer: This case established that under § 2 of the 13th Amendment, Congress may deal broadly with private acts of racial discrimination.
The Limit: While powerful, this source is typically limited to addressing racial discrimination (the "badges and incidents of slavery").
III. Alternate Sources: Commerce and Spending Powers
Congress is not limited to the Reconstruction Amendments to protect civil rights.
Statutory Rights: Many civil rights laws protect rights under the "laws of the United States" rather than just the Constitution.
Commerce Power: Congress used the Commerce Power to enact the Civil Rights Act of 1964, allowing it to reach purely private interferences in public accommodations.
🏛️ Professor Fisher’s "Virtual Auditor" Notes
Fisher’s "Checkmate" Strategy: For the exam, always categorize the defendant immediately. 1. If the defendant is a State Official, your trigger is § 1983 (Civil) or § 242 (Criminal) via the 14th Amendment. 2. If the defendant is a Private Citizen, you must pivot. Does the claim involve race? If so, use the 13th Amendment as your "unlimited" power source via § 1981 or § 1982. 3. If it is a private conspiracy withoutrace, you must look for an Article I power (Commerce/Spending) because the 14th Amendment "State Action" wall is difficult to breach.
🎓 1L Exam Tip (Short & Happy Method)
When a hypo asks if a private "neighborhood watch" can be sued for blocking a street, check the statute used:
Under § 1983? You will likely fail because there is no "color of law".
Under § 1985(3)? You might succeed, but only if you can show a "conspiracy" and potentially a racial/class-based animus that ties back to the 13th Amendment or a specific federal law.
United States v. Guest
383 U.S. 745, 86 S. Ct. 1170, 16 L. Ed. 2d 239 (1966).
Case Brief: United States v. Guest (1966)
FACTS: Six defendants (Guest and others) were indicted under 18 U.S.C. § 241, a criminal conspiracy statute, for conspiring to deprive Black citizens of the free exercise of rights secured by the Constitution. The indictment alleged that the defendants conspired to (1) interfere with the use of state facilities by causing the arrest of Black citizens on false reports of criminal activity, and (2) interfere with the Right to Interstate Travel by using threats and intimidation on the highways. The District Court dismissed the indictment, reasoning that § 241 could not reach private conspiracies to interfere with Fourteenth Amendment rights.
ISSUE: Can a private conspiracy be prosecuted under § 241 if it involves the active participation of state officials or interferes with a fundamental right that does not require state action, such as the Right to Travel? 
⚖️ IRAC Analysis
I — ISSUE
Does 18 U.S.C. § 241 reach a purely private conspiracy to deprive persons of Fourteenth Amendment rights (which typically require state action) and the Right to Interstate Travel? 
R — RULE
State Action Requirement: The Fourteenth Amendment protects only against state action; it does not protect against "individual invasion of individual rights".
Nexus/Participation: A private conspiracy can trigger the Fourteenth Amendment if it involves the active participation of state officials.
Fundamental Rights: Some constitutional rights, specifically the Right to Interstate Travel, are "fundamental" and protect against private as well as state interference.
A — APPLICATION
The State Action Nexus: The Court found that the indictment's allegation—that the defendants caused the false arrest of Black citizens—sufficiently involved state action. Because the state’s "arresting apparatus" was triggered by the private conspiracy, the state became a participant in the deprivation of rights.
The Right to Travel: The Court held that the Right to Interstate Travel is a right that the Constitution protects against private interference as well as state interference. Therefore, no state action was required to prosecute that specific count of the conspiracy.
Section 5 Power: While the majority opinion avoided deciding if Congress could reach any private conspiracy under the Fourteenth Amendment, six Justices (in concurring/dissenting opinions) suggested that Section 5 grants Congress the power to punish private conspiracies that interfere with Fourteenth Amendment rights, regardless of state involvement.
C — CONCLUSION
The Supreme Court reversed the dismissal of the indictment. It held that § 241 was applicable because the conspiracy involved the active connivance of state agents (false arrests) and interfered with the fundamental, non-state-action-bound Right to Interstate Travel.
🏛️ Professor Fisher’s "Virtual Auditor" Notes
Fisher’s "Checkmate" Strategy: Professor Fisher will focus on the Institutional Competence shift in the concurrences. Although the majority stuck to the state action requirement, the Guest concurrences signaled to Congress that the "State Action" wall from the Civil Rights Cases was beginning to crumble.
The False Arrest Nexus: This is a precursor to Lugar. If a private citizen uses the police as a "tool" to commit a constitutional wrong (like a false report leading to arrest), they have "jointly participated" with the state, satisfying the 14th Amendment trigger.
🎓 1L Exam Tip (Short & Happy Method)
When analyzing a private conspiracy:
Is it Race-based? (Pivots to 13th Amendment power).
Does it involve the Police or Courts? (Triggers State Action via Guest or Lugar).
Does it involve Travel? (Checkmate—no state action required per Guest).
Criminal Sanctions for Private Interference with Fourteenth Amendment Rights
I. The "Joint Activity" Doctrine (United States v. Price)
Price provides the definitive "Checkmate" for reaching private individuals under 18 U.S.C. § 242 (the "under color of law" statute).
The "Color of Law" Expansion: Private persons act "under color of law" if they are willful participants in joint activity with the State or its agents.
No Official Status Required: It is irrelevant that the private defendants are not state employees; the "joint adventure" with law enforcement (releasing victims from jail to intercept and kill them) satisfies the state action nexus.
Fisher’s Strategic Point: Under § 241, if state officials collaborate with private persons to deprive someone of 14th Amendment rights, the presence of those officials removes any "constitutional difficulty" regarding the state action requirement.
II. The "Specific Intent" Shield (Screws v. United States)
To prevent federal civil rights statutes from being "void for vagueness," the Court introduced a strict scienter (mental state) requirement.
The Vagueness Problem: Local officers shouldn't be criminally liable for every act that a court later decides violated "due process".
The Fix (Specific Intent): To be convicted under § 242, a defendant must act "willfully"—meaning they had a specific intent to deprive a person of a federal right that has been made "definite" by the Constitution or court decisions.
Fisher Note: High-level bad purpose or "evil intent" is not enough; the prosecutor must prove the defendant specifically aimed to override a clearly established constitutional right.
III. Section 5: Congress as a "Co-Equal" Interpreter?
The Guest concurrences (specifically Justice Brennan’s) introduced a "Corollary" theory that suggests Congress can reach further than the Court.
1. The "Corollary" Right Theory
The Duty: The 14th Amendment commands the State to provide equal access to facilities.
The Corollary: Citizens have a corresponding right to use those facilities without discrimination.
The Reach: Under Section 5, Congress may punish private interferences that hamper this state-victim relationship.
Targeting the Victim: Brennan argues Congress can reach private actors who intimidate victims to prevent them from exercising their rights, not just those who intimidate officials.
2. The Potential "Slippery Slope"
Your notes highlight the unresolved boundaries of this power:
Intent vs. Effect: Is it enough that a private act (like murder) has the effect of stopping someone from using a state facility, or must the defendant intend to interfere with that access? 
The "Outer Wall": The Guest rationale likely does not allow Congress to regulate access to private facilities (like private housing) under the 14th Amendment, even if it claims doing so protects access to public housing.
🏛️ Professor Fisher’s "Virtual Auditor" Notes
Fisher’s "Checkmate" Strategy: In a criminal civil rights hypo, the "Specific Intent" requirement from Screws is your best defense. If the law was not "clearly established" at the time of the act, the defendant cannot have the requisite "specific intent" to violate it, and the statute would be unconstitutionally vague as applied to them.
The "Joint Participation" Trigger: Look for any "wink or a nod" from state officials. Under Price, if a private citizen takes advantage of a state officer's participation in a "joint adventure," they are legally indistinguishable from the officer for 14th Amendment purposes.
🎓 1L Exam Tip (Short & Happy Method)
When analyzing 18 U.S.C. § 242:
Is there "Color of Law"? (Look for state officials or "joint activity" per Price).
Is there "Specific Intent"? (Ask: Was the right "made definite" by a court decision before the act? per Screws).
Is it a conspiracy? (Check § 241 if two or more people are involved).
Congressional Power to Reach Private Conduct Under the Thirteenth Amendment
I. Civil Remedies Against Private Conspiracies: 42 U.S.C. § 1985(3)
While 42 U.S.C. § 1983 requires "color of law," § 1985(3) is the vehicle for reaching purely private conspiracies.
A. The Transition from Collins to Griffin
The Old Rule (Collins v. Hardyman): Initially, the Court viewed § 1985(3) as reaching only conspiracies under color of state law, famously stating that private discrimination is not "inequality before the law" without some "manipulation of the law or its agencies".
The New Rule (Griffin v. Breckenridge): A unanimous Court discarded Collins, holding that § 1985(3) does cover purely private conspiracies.
The Constitutional Anchor: Justice Stewart bypassed the 14th Amendment's state action hurdle by relying on Section 2 of the Thirteenth Amendment. He concluded Congress has the power to create a cause of action for victims of racially discriminatory private action aimed at depriving them of basic rights.
B. The "Animus" Requirement (The Barrier to "General Tort Law")
To prevent § 1985(3) from becoming a "general federal tort law," the Court established a strict mental state requirement:
Discriminatory Animus: There must be some racial, or perhaps otherwise class-based, invidiously discriminatory animus behind the conspirators’ action.
Economic/Commercial Animus: In United Brotherhood of Carpenters v. Scott, the Court refused to extend § 1985(3) to conspiracies motivated by economic or commercial animus (e.g., union vs. non-union violence).
Abortion Clinic Blockades (Bray v. Alexandria Women’s Health Clinic):
The Court held that animus toward abortion is not class-based animus toward women.
Justice Scalia noted that a "class" cannot be defined solely by a common desire to engage in conduct the defendant disfavors.
II. Civil Remedies Under "Color of Law": 42 U.S.C. § 1983
Section 1983 is the primary tool for redressing constitutional wrongs by state officials.
A. Tort-Style Liability (Monroe v. Pape)
The Rule: Unlike the criminal specific intent requirement in Screws, § 1983 is read against a background of tort liability.
The Standard: An official is responsible for the "natural consequences of his actions" regardless of whether they had a specific intent to deprive a person of a federal right.
B. Defining "Custom or Usage" (Adickes v. S.H. Kress & Co.)
Majority View (Harlan): "Custom" requires state involvement and must have the force of law through the persistent practices of state officials. It is not merely a "social habit".
Dissenting View (Brennan): Argued "custom" should mean a widespread practice of the people of a State, even if not explicitly backed by the force of the State.
🏛️ Professor Fisher’s "Virtual Auditor" Notes
Fisher’s "Checkmate" Strategy: You must distinguish the "Mental State" for the two civil statutes.
For § 1983: Use the Monroe "Natural Consequences" (Tort) standard. It is a lower bar than criminal law.
For § 1985(3): Use the Griffin/Bray "Invidious Class-Based Animus" standard. It is a very high bar designed to protect federalism and prevent the federal courts from becoming "local police".
🎓 1L Exam Tip (Short & Happy Method)
When analyzing a § 1985(3) claim:
Identify the conspiracy: Are there two or more private actors? 
Identify the Animus: Is it Race? (If yes, strongest case). Is it Economic or Anti-Abortion? (If yes, the case likely fails per Scott and Bray) .
Identify the Right: Does the right protected require state action? If so, you must prove state involvement in the conspiracy unless it involves the Right to Travel.
Jones v. Alfred H. Mayer Co.
392 U.S. 409, 88 S. Ct. 2186, 20 L. Ed. 2d 1189 (1968).
Case Brief: Jones v. Alfred H. Mayer Co. (1968)
FACTS: The petitioners, Joseph Lee Jones and his wife (an African American couple), alleged that the Alfred H. Mayer Co. refused to sell them a home in a private community in St. Louis County solely because Mr. Jones was Black. They brought suit under 42 U.S.C. § 1982, which provides that all citizens shall have the same right as white citizens to "purchase, lease, sell, hold, and convey real and personal property". The lower federal courts dismissed the complaint, relying on the Civil Rights Cases to hold that § 1982 applied only to state action and not to private refusals to sell.
ISSUE: Does 42 U.S.C. § 1982 prohibit purely private racial discrimination in the sale of property, and if so, does Congress have the constitutional power under the 13th Amendment to enact such a law? 
⚖️ IRAC Analysis
I — ISSUE
Whether Congress intended 42 U.S.C. § 1982 to reach private acts of discrimination and whether the Enforcement Clause of the 13th Amendment (§ 2) empowers Congress to regulate such private conduct.
R — RULE
Statutory Interpretation of § 1982: The statute bars all racial discrimination—private as well as public—in the sale or rental of property.
13th Amendment Power: Unlike the 14th Amendment, the 13th Amendment is not limited to state action; it is an "absolute declaration" that slavery shall not exist.
Badges and Incidents of Slavery: Under § 2 of the 13th Amendment, Congress has the power to pass all laws necessary and proper for abolishing the "badges and incidents of slavery".
A — APPLICATION
The Text of § 1982: Justice Stewart’s majority opinion found that the plain language of the statute (originally the Civil Rights Act of 1866) contained no state action requirement. To limit it to state action would render the right to "purchase" property "a mere form of words".
Defining "Badges of Slavery": The Court held that the inability to buy or rent property based on race is a "badge of slavery". If Congress cannot prevent private citizens from denying Black citizens the right to hold property, the 13th Amendment’s promise of freedom would be a "hollow shibboleth".
Institutional Shift: The Court explicitly distinguished this from the 14th Amendment. Because the power comes from the 13th Amendment, the "State Action" wall erected in the Civil Rights Cases (1883) does not apply here.
C — CONCLUSION
The Supreme Court reversed the lower court. It held that § 1982 prohibits private racial discrimination in property transactions and that the 13th Amendment validly supports this exercise of Congressional power.
🏛️ Professor Fisher’s "Virtual Auditor" Notes
Fisher’s "Checkmate" Strategy: You must appreciate the Historical Exegesis here. Professor Fisher will focus on the fact that the Civil Rights Act of 1866 was passed before the 14th Amendment was ratified. Thus, § 1982 had to be supported by the 13th Amendment.
The Monopoly of Interpretation: > Fisher will ask: "Who gets to define what a 'badge of slavery' is?" Under Jones, the answer is Congress. As long as Congress can rationally determine that a certain practice (like housing discrimination) is a badge of servitude, the Court will defer to that judgment.
🎓 1L Exam Tip (Short & Happy Method)
When analyzing a private discrimination hypo:
Step 1: Check the Amendment. If the plaintiff is using the 14th Amendment, they will lose due to the State Action requirement.
Step 2: Pivot to the 13th. If the plaintiff is using § 1981 (contracts) or § 1982 (property), state action is not required per Jones v. Mayer.
The Limitation: Remember, the 13th Amendment power is typically limited to racial discrimination. It does not reach gender or age discrimination in the same "absolute" private way.
Thirteenth Amendment Powers and the 1866 Civil Rights Act
I. The Post-Jones Scope of Congressional Power
The recognition of broad Thirteenth Amendment power in Jones fundamentally shifted the landscape of civil rights enforcement.
Superfluous Commerce Power?: The expansion of the Thirteenth Amendment raises the question of whether the Commerce Power (used for the 1964 Civil Rights Act) became unnecessary for racial discrimination. However, the Commerce Power remains a distinct and vital source for reaching non-racial discrimination (e.g., gender, religion).
The Power of § 1981: This statute guarantees all persons the "same right... to make and enforce contracts" as enjoyed by white citizens. Because almost all private interactions (employment, private school enrollment, retail) are contractual, § 1981 provides a massive federal reach into the private sector.
The "Badges of Servitude" Limit: A core question remains: Is there any racial discrimination that Congress may not rationally categorize as a "badge of servitude"?. Currently, the Court defers heavily to Congress’s determination of what constitutes an incident of slavery.
Non-Black Groups: While born of the "freedom of the slave race," the 1866 Act protections have been discussed in the context of whether they extend to other ethnic or ancestral groups.
II. Expanding the 1866 Act: Property and Schools
The Court has consistently pushed the 1866 Act into private domains where the Fourteenth Amendment could not go.
1. Community Facilities (Sullivan v. Little Hunting Park)
The Fact Pattern: A white homeowner (Sullivan) tried to assign his community park membership share to his Black tenant (Freeman). The board refused based on race.
The Holding: The Court found this was an interference with the right to "lease" property under § 1982.
The Exclusivity Test: The Court rejected the "private social club" defense because the park was open to any white person in the area; there was no selective element other than race.
Harlan’s Dissent: He warned of "grave constitutional issues" when federal law intrudes too far into private associations, echoing the federalism concerns of the Civil Rights Cases.
2. Private Schools (Runyon v. McCrary)
The Holding: § 1981 prohibits private, commercially operated, nonsectarian schools from denying admission to students based on race.
The Constitutional Balance: The Court held that enforcing § 1981 here did not violate the parents' rights to privacy or free association.
White’s Dissent: Justice White argued that § 1981 was only meant to stop legal disabilities (laws preventing contracts), not private refusals to contract. He warned that this path forces the judiciary into "sensitive policy considerations" regarding private associations.
Legislative Note: Congress explicitly codified the majority’s broad interpretation in the 1991 Civil Rights Act.
🏛️ Professor Fisher’s "Virtual Auditor" Notes
Fisher’s "Checkmate" Strategy: You must distinguish between Social Rights and Contractual/Property Rights.
Social Clubs: If a club is truly "exclusive" and selective (not just "all-white"), it may still be protected by the First Amendment right of association.
Commercial Entities: If a private entity is "commercially operated" and open to the general public (like the schools in Runyon), the 13th Amendment through § 1981 is a "Checkmate" against their discriminatory policies.
🎓 1L Exam Tip (Short & Happy Method)
When analyzing a private school or club hypo:
Check the "Selective Element": Is it a truly private social group, or is it a business that just excludes based on race?.
Apply Runyon: If it is a commercial operation, § 1981 applies via the Thirteenth Amendment—no state action is required.
Identify the Conflict: Be prepared to argue the "Harlan/White" point: At what point does the federal government’s power to end "badges of slavery" infringe upon a citizen’s right to private, even if biased, association?.
Section 4.Congressional Power to Enforce Civil Rights Under the Fourteenth and Fifteenth Amendments
Remedial power of Congress to the more controversial Prophylactic power. For Professor Fisher, this is a critical structural distinction: it addresses whether Congress is merely a "law clerk" for the Supreme Court or a "co-equal" architect of constitutional protections.
I. The Remedial Theory: The "Judicial Follower"
Historically, Congressional enforcement of the 14th and 15th Amendments was viewed through a narrow, reactive lens.
Definition: Congress acts only to provide mechanisms to implement rights that the Judiciary has already declared.
"Under Color of Law": The post-Civil War provisions (like § 1983) are the primary examples of this; they offer remedies for state-sponsored deprivations of rights as interpreted by the Court.
The Guest Expansion: While Guest suggested Congress could reach some private behavior, it still did not claim the power to change the substantive content of the rights being protected.
II. The Prophylactic Theory: The "Proactive Architect"
During the 1960s civil rights era, Congress began utilizing a broader "prophylactic" understanding of its § 5 (14th Amendment) and § 2 (15th Amendment) powers.
Definition: Congress does not need to wait for a specific, judicially-recognized violation to occur before it acts.
Proactive Measures: Under this theory, Congress can enact broad, creatively structured laws designed to preventpotential future violations.
Constitutional Basis: The Court has generally interpreted the enforcement clauses of the 14th and 15th Amendments identically regarding this authority.
🏛️ Professor Fisher’s "Virtual Auditor" Notes
Fisher’s "Checkmate" Strategy: Professor Fisher will likely ask: "Who has the final word on what the Constitution means?" > 1. If Congress is limited to Remedial power, the Court remains the sole interpreter (per Marbury and City of Boerne). 2. If Congress has Prophylactic power, it can effectively expand the "zone of protection" beyond what the Court has strictly mandated.
🎓 1L Exam Tip (Short & Happy Method)
When analyzing a new civil rights statute on an exam:
The Remedial Question: Does the law simply punish someone for a known constitutional violation? (e.g., suing a cop for a beating).
The Prophylactic Question: Does the law ban something that isn't necessarily unconstitutional in itself (like a literacy test) in order to prevent a larger constitutional evil (like racial discrimination in voting)?.
The Fisher Pivot: Be prepared to argue whether Congress has exceeded its "appropriate" enforcement power by trying to redefine the right rather than just protecting it.
Congressional Protection of Voting Rights
These notes trace the evolution of voting rights enforcement from a narrow remedial model to the prophylactic model championed by the Warren Court. For Professor Fisher, the key is the tension between State Sovereignty (Article I, § 2)and Congressional Enforcement (15th Amendment, § 2).
I. Historical Context: The Failure of Case-by-Case Litigation
Prior to 1965, federal voting rights efforts were strictly reactive.
The Remedial Model: Congress provided mechanisms for courts to enforce rights after a violation was proven.
The Problem of "Ingenious Defiance": States like Alabama and Mississippi used "neutral" literacy tests, grandfather clauses, and "good character" requirements specifically to disenfranchise Black voters.
Ineffectiveness of Litigation: Case-by-case suits were "exceedingly slow" and "onerous". Even when the government won, states simply "switched to discriminatory devices not covered by the federal decrees".
II. The "Neutrality" of Literacy Tests (Lassiter)
Before the 1965 Act, the Court held that literacy tests were not per se unconstitutional.
Rule from Lassiter v. Northampton County (1959): States have broad power to set non-discriminatory voter qualifications.
Rationality: The Court found that literacy has a "relation to standards designed to promote intelligent use of the ballot" and is "neutral on race".
The Fisher "Institutional" Note: Justice Douglas emphasized that the Court does not "sit in judgment on the wisdom" of state policy. However, Lassiter acknowledged that such tests could be used for discrimination.
III. The Prophylactic Shift: South Carolina v. Katzenbach (1965)
The Voting Rights Act of 1965 moved beyond judicial findings to proactive federal oversight.
The "Coverage Formula" (§ 4(b)): Applied to states that used a "test or device" and had less than 50% voter turnout.
Preclearance (§ 5): Required covered jurisdictions to get federal approval before changing any voting law.
The Holding: Chief Justice Warren held that Congress may use "any rational means" to effectuate the 15th Amendment.
The McCulloch Standard: The Court applied the broad McCulloch v. Maryland "Necessary and Proper" test to the enforcement of civil rights.
IV. Nationwide Expansion and Uniformity (Oregon v. Mitchell)
Nationwide Ban: In 1970, Congress expanded the literacy test ban to the entire country, not just the "covered" South.
Judicial Consensus: The Court was unanimous in upholding this nationwide ban.
Reasoning: Even the restrictive Justice Harlan agreed that Congress could determine that racial prejudice was "prevalent throughout the Nation" and that literacy tests "unduly lend themselves to discriminatory application".
🏛️ Professor Fisher’s "Virtual Auditor" Notes
Fisher’s "Checkmate" Strategy: You must distinguish the Standard of Review. 1. In Lassiter, the Court used a deferential Rational Basis to uphold the test. 2. In Katzenbach, the Court used a deferential Rational Basis to uphold the ban on the test. The Takeaway: When Congress acts under the 15th Amendment, the Court shifts the "advantage of time and inertia" to the victims of discrimination rather than the perpetrators.
🎓 1L Exam Tip (Short & Happy Method)
When a hypo involves a state qualification (like a residency or age requirement):
Start with Lassiter: Is the requirement neutral on its face? 
Apply Katzenbach: Did Congress pass a law banning it? If so, does Congress have a "rational basis" to believe the requirement is being used for discrimination? 
Modern Limit: Be aware that Shelby County v. Holder (2013) eventually struck down the coverage formula, effectively stopping preclearance because the "historical experience" of 1965 no longer matched modern data.
Katzenbach v. Morgan
384 U.S. 641, 86 S. Ct. 1717, 16 L. Ed. 2d 828 (1966).
FACTS: Section 4(e) of the Voting Rights Act of 1965 provided that no person who successfully completed the sixth grade in an accredited school in Puerto Rico (where the language of instruction was Spanish) could be denied the right to vote because of an inability to read or write English. Registered voters in New York challenged this, arguing it bypassed New York’s English literacy requirement. Under the Court’s prior ruling in Lassiter, such literacy tests were generally constitutional. New York argued that Section 4(e) was unconstitutional because the Court had never held that an English literacy requirement violated the Fourteenth Amendment.
ISSUE: May Congress, under Section 5 of the Fourteenth Amendment, prohibit the enforcement of a state law (like an English literacy test) that the Supreme Court has not itself found to be unconstitutional? 
I — ISSUE
Does the Section 5 "power to enforce" grant Congress the authority to enact legislation that either prevents constitutional violations (the Prophylactic/Remedial prong) or defines the substantive scope of the Amendment (the Substantive prong)? 
R — RULE
The McCulloch Standard: Section 5 is a positive grant of legislative power. The test for "appropriate" legislation is whether the law is a "plainly adapted" means to a legitimate end under the Constitution.
Rational Basis: Congress may act under Section 5 if there is a rational basis to find that the law protects Fourteenth Amendment rights.
A — APPLICATION
Justice Brennan’s majority opinion provided two alternative rationales for the law's validity:
Rationale 1: The Remedial/Prophylactic Prong: Congress could have rationally concluded that granting the right to vote to the Puerto Rican community would empower them to better resist "prejudice" and "discriminatory treatment" in the provision of public services like housing, schools, and law enforcement. By securing the vote, Congress protects the community's Equal Protection rights in other areas of state life.
Rationale 2: The Substantive/Interpretive Prong: Even if the Court in Lassiter found literacy tests constitutional, Congress may independently "enforce" the Amendment by concluding that New York's specific requirement constituted an "invidious discrimination". Congress's superior fact-finding allows it to determine that the test was not actually a neutral tool for "intelligent use of the ballot" but a discriminatory hurdle.
C — CONCLUSION
The Supreme Court held Section 4(e) to be a valid exercise of Congressional power. Congress is not limited to merely following the Court’s specific findings of unconstitutionality; it may proactively secure rights through any rational means adapted to the ends of the Fourteenth Amendment.
🏛️ Professor Fisher’s "Virtual Auditor" Notes
Fisher’s "Checkmate" Strategy: The One-Way Ratchet: Professor Fisher will focus on the "Ratchet Theory" found in Brennan's Footnote 10. Congress can use Section 5 to expand rights beyond the Court’s floor, but it cannot use Section 5 to restrict or dilute rights established by the Court.
The Judicial Supremacy Conflict: Note the Harlan Dissent. Harlan argued that the Court, not Congress, has the final authority to determine if a state law (like a literacy test) is "rational" or "discriminatory". He warned that if Congress can redefine "Equal Protection," the Fourteenth Amendment becomes a tool for federal overreach into state sovereignty.
🎓 1L Exam Tip (Short & Happy Method)
When analyzing a Section 5 federal law:
Check for a Court Precedent: Does the law ban something the Court previously allowed? (Like the literacy test here). 
Apply Morgan: Argue that Congress is using its superior fact-finding to prevent broader discrimination or to protect a "corollary" right to public services. 
Fisher Pivot: Be ready to counter with the "Substantive" vs. "Remedial" debate—is Congress enforcing the right or re-writing it? 
The Meaning and Aftermath of Katzenbach v. Morgan
City of Boerne v. Flores
521 U.S. 507, 117 S. Ct. 2157, 138 L. Ed. 2d 624 (1997).
FACTS: St. Peter Catholic Church in Boerne, Texas, was denied a building permit to enlarge its structure because the building was located in a historic preservation district. The Archbishop (Flores) sued under the Religious Freedom Restoration Act of 1993 (RFRA). Congress had passed RFRA specifically to overturn the Supreme Court’s decision in Employment Division v. Smith, which held that "neutral laws of general applicability" do not violate the Free Exercise Clause even if they burden religion. RFRA attempted to restore the "compelling interest" test, forbidding the government from substantially burdening religion unless it used the least restrictive means to further a compelling interest. Congress claimed it had the power to pass RFRA under Section 5 of the Fourteenth Amendment to "enforce" the Free Exercise Clause (incorporated against the states).
ISSUE: Does Congress have the substantive power under Section 5 to define the meaning of constitutional rights, or is its power strictly limited to remedial and prophylactic measures?
I — ISSUE
Whether Congress exceeded its enforcement power under Section 5 of the Fourteenth Amendment by enacting RFRA to impose a higher standard of protection for religious exercise than the Supreme Court had required in its own interpretations.
R — RULE
Remedial, Not Substantive: The Section 5 power to "enforce" is remedial; it does not include the power to decree the substantive meaning of constitutional rights. "Congress does not enforce a constitutional right by changing what the right is".
The "Congruence and Proportionality" Test: To be a valid exercise of Section 5 power, there must be a congruence and proportionality between the injury to be prevented (the constitutional violation) and the means adopted to that end (the legislation).
Judicial Supremacy: The power to interpret the Constitution remains exclusively with the Judiciary per Marbury v. Madison.
A — APPLICATION
Lack of Proportionality: The Court, per Justice Kennedy, found that RFRA failed the test. The law was so out of proportion to any supposed "pattern of unconstitutional discrimination" against religion that it could not be considered remedial.
Lack of Congruence: RFRA was a "sweeping" and "disproportionate" intrusion into state and local government. Unlike the Voting Rights Act (which targeted specific, documented racial bias), RFRA applied to every level of government and every law, regardless of whether there was evidence of religious bigotry.
Substantive Change: By trying to force the "compelling interest" test back onto the states after the Court had rejected it in Smith, Congress was not enforcing the 14th Amendment—it was attempting a substantive reinterpretation.
C — CONCLUSION
The Supreme Court held that RFRA is unconstitutional as applied to the states. Congress exceeded its Section 5 authority because the law was not a congruent or proportional response to a documented pattern of constitutional violations; rather, it was an attempt to redefine the right itself.
🏛️ Professor Fisher’s "Virtual Auditor" Notes
Fisher’s "Institutional" Checkmate: This case is the final word on Section 5. Professor Fisher will expect you to contrast this with Katzenbach v. Morgan. In Morgan, the Court was deferential (the "Ratchet"). In Boerne, the Court is hostile to any attempt by Congress to be the "final arbiter" of rights.
The Federalism Link: Remember the Seminole Tribe rule: Congress can only abrogate a state's 11th Amendment immunity using Section 5. Because Boerne makes it much harder to use Section 5, it effectively makes it much harder for citizens to sue states for money.
🎓 1L Exam Tip (Short & Happy Method)
When a federal law forces a state to protect a right (like disability or age) more than the Court does:
Start with Boerne: Does Congress have a "documented record" of the state violating the Constitution?
Apply "Congruence and Proportionality": Is the federal law a "surgical" fix for those specific violations, or is it a "sledgehammer" that bans all state laws?
The Checkmate: If the federal law is a sledgehammer, it is substantive and unconstitutional.
The Meaning and Scope of Boerne
These notes explore the fallout of the Boerne decision and its impact on the Separation of Powers, Federalism, and the future of the Voting Rights Act. For Professor Fisher, you must be prepared to argue whether the Court has overstepped its own bounds by stripping Congress of its "legislative centrality" in civil rights enforcement.
I. Boerne and the Battle for Interpretation
The Boerne decision creates a tension between two constitutional values: Horizontal Separation of Powers (who interprets) and Vertical Federalism (state vs. federal power).
Horizontal vs. Vertical: While Boerne appeared to be a reaction to Congress's attempt to overturn a specific Court decision (Smith), the fact that RFRA remains valid against the federal government suggests the ruling was primarily a federalism measure. It limits Congress’s power to interfere with state prerogatives under the guise of Section 5.
Separation of Powers: Critics argue Boerne violates the spirit of Marbury by making the Court the "final arbiter" in a way that prevents Congress from protecting rights more broadly than the Court's own "floor".
II. Reconciling Boerne with Morgan and Katzenbach
Justice Kennedy insisted Boerne did not overrule the broad prophylactic wins of the 1960s, but he fundamentally redefined their justification.
Remedial vs. Substantive: Morgan is now seen strictly as a remedial case. Congress can only expand protections if it is "remedying" a pattern of state violations that the Court has already recognized as unconstitutional.
The Fact-Finding Threshold: Congress still has power to forestall violations, but its "preventive measures" must be backed by a significant likelihood that the state laws being targeted are unconstitutional.
Historical Irony: Some scholars point out that the 39th Congress (which drafted the 14th Amendment) specifically intended for legislative centrality because they did not trust the federal courts following the Dred Scott decision. Boerne effectively inverts this intended hierarchy.
III. The "Congruence and Proportionality" Standard
This is the new "Fisher-level" scrutiny for Section 5 legislation.
Ratcheting Up Scrutiny: Unlike the deferential McCulloch "Rational Basis" test, "Congruence and Proportionality" acts more like Intermediate Scrutiny.
Definition:
Congruent: The remedy must be closely related to the identified constitutional injury.
Proportional: The remedy must not be overly broad (overinclusive) or too narrow (underinclusive) in relation to the harm.
IV. The VRA Warning Shot: Northwest Austin (2009)
The Court used the "Canon of Constitutional Avoidance" to delay the inevitable clash over the Voting Rights Act.
The "Serious Doubts": Chief Justice Roberts warned that the preclearance requirements of the VRA might no longer meet the Boerne standard because the "historical experience" of 1965 was outdated.
The Bailout: By allowing the utility district to "bail out" of the preclearance requirement through statutory interpretation, the Court gave Congress a "warning shot" to update the law. Congress's failure to do so led directly to the invalidation of the coverage formula in Shelby County.
🏛️ Professor Fisher’s "Virtual Auditor" Notes
Fisher’s "Checkmate" Strategy: Professor Fisher will focus on the Institutional Inversion. If the 14th Amendment was intended to give Congress power over the States, Boerne uses the 14th Amendment to give the Court power over Congress.
The Standard of Review: In any hypo regarding a new civil rights law, you must apply Congruence and Proportionality. If Congress hasn't built a "massive record" of state-sponsored constitutional violations, the law is likely an unconstitutional "Substantive" change.
🎓 1L Exam Tip (Short & Happy Method)
When a federal law interferes with state sovereignty:
Look for the Record: Did Congress hold hearings? Did they find a "pattern" of unconstitutional behavior by States?
Check the Fit: Is the law a "surgical" remedy for those violations, or a "nationwide ban" on something the Court hasn't yet declared illegal?
The Result: If it's a "nationwide ban" without a record, it fails the Proportionality test.
Shelby County v. Holder
570 U.S. 529, 133 S. Ct. 2612, 186 L. Ed. 2d 651 (2013).
FACTS: The Voting Rights Act of 1965 (VRA) was enacted to address entrenched racial discrimination in voting. Section 5 of the Act required certain "covered" jurisdictions to "preclear" any changes to their voting laws with the federal government. Section 4(b) provided the "coverage formula" used to determine which states were subject to preclearance, based on their history of using discriminatory tests and low voter turnout as of 1964, 1968, or 1972. Congress reauthorized the Act for another 25 years in 2006 without updating the coverage formula. Shelby County, Alabama, a covered jurisdiction, sued the U.S. Attorney General, arguing that the 40-year-old data in the formula was no longer "congruent and proportional" to current conditions.
ISSUE: Does the renewal of the Section 4(b) coverage formula of the Voting Rights Act exceed Congress’s enforcement power under the Fourteenth or Fifteenth Amendments in light of current conditions?
I — ISSUE
Whether Congress's decision to continue using a decades-old coverage formula to subject specific states to federal oversight violates the principle of equal sovereignty and exceeds the prophylactic enforcement power granted by the Reconstruction Amendments.
R — RULE
Equal Sovereignty: The federal government must justify any departure from the "fundamental principle of equal sovereignty" among the States.
Current Needs Test: For a prophylactic remedy to be "appropriate" under the 15th Amendment, it must be justified by current needs and "current burdens" must be justified by "current needs".
Congruence and Proportionality: Per City of Boerne, there must be a fit between the remedy and the injury to be prevented.
A — APPLICATION
Outdated Data: Chief Justice Roberts, writing for the majority, noted that while the VRA was successful, the "coverage formula" in Section 4(b) was based on data that was nearly 50 years old. The "extraordinary measures" of 1965 were justified by "extraordinary conditions," but those conditions no longer exist in the same way.
Equal Sovereignty Violation: By forcing only some states to preclear laws based on 1970s data, Congress treated states unequally without a current justification. The Court found the formula was "no longer responsive to current conditions" and thus could not be considered a "congruent and proportional" remedy.
Ginsburg’s Dissent: Justice Ginsburg argued that "throwing out preclearance when it has worked and is continuing to work to stop discriminatory changes is like throwing away your umbrella in a rainstorm because you are not getting wet". She contended that the "current needs" were evidenced by the high volume of Section 5 preclearance blocks that were still occurring.
C — CONCLUSION
The Supreme Court held that Section 4(b) is unconstitutional. While the Court did not strike down Section 5 itself, without a valid coverage formula, Section 5 is rendered "inoperable". Congress may draft a new formula based on current data, but it can no longer rely on the 1965 framework.
🏛️ Professor Fisher’s "Virtual Auditor" Notes
Fisher’s "Checkmate" Strategy: You must master the Rational Basis vs. Equal Sovereignty distinction. In South Carolina v. Katzenbach (1966), the Court used a deferential McCulloch "rational basis" to uphold the formula. In Shelby County, the Court applied a much "stricter" standard of review by requiring the formula to be justified by "current needs".
The Judicial Supremacy Finality: This case confirms the Boerne transition. The Court—not Congress—will decide when a historical "pattern of discrimination" has sufficiently ended such that federal oversight must cease.
🎓 1L Exam Tip (Short & Happy Method)
When analyzing a Section 5 federal law that targets specific states:
Identify the "Burden": Does the law treat some states differently than others? (The Equal Sovereignty trigger).
Check the "Date": Is the evidence Congress used to justify the law current (last 5-10 years) or historical (20+ years)?.
The Result: If the evidence is historical and the burden is current, the law fails the Congruence and Proportionality test.
The Voting Rights Act After Shelby County
I. The Post-Shelley "Inoperability" Crisis
The Status of § 5: While the Court in Shelby County did not strike down Section 5 (the preclearance requirement), it invalidated Section 4(b) (the coverage formula).
Legislative Gridlock: Without a valid formula to determine which states are "covered," Section 5 is effectively "inoperable". Proposals to update the formula, such as the Voting Rights Amendment Act of 2014, have failed to pass.
Immediate State Action: Following the decision, previously "covered" states (Texas, North Carolina, Alabama, and Mississippi) immediately moved to implement strict voter identification laws that had been previously blocked or stalled by federal preclearance.
The Impact: Critics argue these laws disproportionately burden poor and minority voters, marking a shift from federal proactive prevention to reactive litigation.
II. The Evolution of Section 2: Intent vs. Results
With Section 5 neutralized, Section 2 of the VRA—which applies nationwide—became the primary tool for challenging discriminatory voting practices.
The Intent Requirement (Mobile v. Bolden): A 1980 plurality held that for a facially neutral law to violate § 2 or the 15th Amendment, plaintiffs had to prove discriminatory intent.
The Congressional Override (1982): Congress amended § 2 to explicitly reject the Bolden intent requirement, replacing it with a "results test". Under this test, a violation is established if the "totality of circumstances" shows the political process is not equally open to a protected class.
III. The Modern "Cabining" of Section 2 (Brnovich)
In Brnovich v. Democratic National Committee (2021), the Court significantly limited the reach of the amended Section 2.
The Shift: After Shelby, § 2 liability became the VRA's core strength; however, the Brnovich decision "diluted" that strength by establishing restrictive "guideposts".
The Arizona Regulations: The case specifically evaluated two Arizona laws: one involving out-of-precinct voting and another involving "ballot harvesting" (collection of ballots by third parties).
The Guideposts: While the Court refused to set a single "test," it emphasized factors that make it harder for plaintiffs to win, such as the size of the burden, the degree of disparity, and the state's interest in preventing fraud.
🏛️ Professor Fisher’s "Virtual Auditor" Notes
Fisher’s "Checkmate" Strategy: You must distinguish the Procedural Posture of § 5 vs. § 2.
Under § 5 (Pre-Shelby): The State bore the burden of proving a law was not discriminatory before it could take effect.
Under § 2 (Post-Shelby): The Plaintiff bears the burden of proving a law is discriminatory after it has already taken effect. The Result: This shift drastically increases the cost and time of litigation, effectively returning voting rights to the "case-by-case" frustration that the 1965 Act was designed to end.
🎓 1L Exam Tip (Short & Happy Method)
When analyzing a modern voter ID or "ballot harvesting" hypo:
Check for Preclearance: It doesn't exist anymore. Do not argue § 5 unless the prompt specifies a new coverage formula has been passed.
Apply § 2 Results Test: Look at the "guideposts" from Brnovich. Is the burden "usual" for voting, or does it specifically and significantly disadvantage a minority class?
Identify the State Interest: If the state claims "preventing fraud," the current Court (per Brnovich) will give significant weight to that interest, making a § 2 violation harder to prove.
Brnovich v. Democratic National Committee
594 U.S. 647, 141 S. Ct. 2321, 210 L. Ed. 2d 753 (2021).
Case Brief: Brnovich v. DNC (2021)
FACTS: The Democratic National Committee (DNC) challenged two Arizona voting regulations:
The Out-of-Precinct (OOP) Policy: Required that ballots cast in the wrong precinct be discarded entirely (even for statewide races).
The Ballot Collection Ban (H.B. 2023): Criminalized the collection and return of another person’s early ballot by anyone other than a family member, household member, or caregiver. The DNC argued these policies had a "disparate impact" on minority voters (Latino, African American, and Native American) in violation of Section 2 of the VRA, which prohibits practices that "result in a denial or abridgment" of the right to vote on account of race.
+1
ISSUE: Do Arizona’s OOP policy and ballot collection ban violate Section 2 of the VRA, and what is the proper test for "vote denial" claims under the 1982 "Results Test" amendment?
I — ISSUE
Does Section 2 of the VRA prohibit facially neutral "time, place, and manner" voting rules simply because they result in a statistically significant disparate impact on minority groups?
R — RULE
The "Equally Open" Standard: Section 2(b) focuses on whether the political process is "equally open" to participation. A violation is found only if, under the "totality of circumstances," members of a protected class have "less opportunity" than other members of the electorate.
The Brnovich "Guideposts": The Court established non-exhaustive factors for Section 2 claims:
Size of the burden: Usual inconveniences of voting (e.g., traveling to a precinct) do not count as a denial of equal opportunity.
Departure from 1982 standards: Practices that were common in 1982 (when § 2 was amended) are highly likely to be considered "equally open."
Size of disparities: Small disparities are insufficient to prove a system is not "equally open."
The system as a whole: Courts must look at all available ways to vote (e.g., mail-in, early voting) to see if the system is open.
Strength of State Interests: Legitimate interests, such as preventing voter fraud, are a strong justification for rules.
A — APPLICATION
OOP Policy: The Court held that the burden of voting in the correct precinct is a "modest" and "usual" requirement. The fact that minority voters were slightly more likely to vote out-of-precinct did not mean the system was not "equally open."
+1
Ballot Collection: The Court found the ban served the state's interest in preventing fraud and ensuring ballot integrity. Because Arizona provided many other ways to vote (like a 27-day early voting period), the ban on third-party collection did not result in "less opportunity."
Intent Check: The Court rejected the Ninth Circuit's finding of discriminatory intent, holding that "racially tinged" allegations of fraud by individual legislators did not prove the entire legislature acted with a discriminatory purpose.
C — CONCLUSION
The Supreme Court held (6–3) that neither policy violated Section 2. The VRA does not require states to maximize minority turnout; it only requires that the system be "equally open" to everyone.
🏛️ Professor Fisher’s "Virtual Auditor" Notes
Fisher’s "Checkmate" Strategy: Pay attention to the "Anchor Date" (1982). By making 1982 practices the "baseline" for what is constitutional, the Court has effectively "frozen" a period of voting history. If a state rule was acceptable then, it is almost certainly legal now. This is a Textualist "Checkmate" against the idea that the VRA is an evolving, living statute.
🎓 1L Exam Tip (Short & Happy Method)
When analyzing a Section 2 "Vote Denial" claim (like a new ID law):
Avoid the "Disparate Impact" Trap: Don't just argue that minority groups are hit harder. That is no longer enough.
Apply the "Usual Effort" Filter: Is the burden (e.g., getting an ID or walking to a precinct) greater than the "usual effort" of voting? If not, the claim fails.
Identify the State's Interest: If the state mentions "Fraud Prevention," identify it as a "strong and legitimate" interest per Brnovich that will likely shield the law from Section 2 liability.
Limits of Congressional Enforcement of the Fourteenth Amendment
I. The Sovereign Immunity Barrier: Florida Prepaid (1999)
Florida Prepaid serves as the definitive application of the Boerne test to property rights and state immunity.
The Conflict: Congress tried to abrogate (cancel) states' sovereign immunity for patent infringement via the Patent Remedy Act.
The "Property" Argument: The U.S. argued that patents are "property" under the Due Process Clause, and thus Congress can use § 5 to prevent states from "stealing" that property without compensation.
The "Checkmate" Audit (Rehnquist):
No Pattern of Abuse: Unlike the Voting Rights cases, Congress identified no widespread history of states infringing patents.
State Remedies: Congress failed to check if state courts provided their own remedies. If a state provides a way to sue in state court, there is no "deprivation without due process."
Lack of Proportionality: The Act was a "sledgehammer" targeting all states for all types of infringement (even negligent ones) for an indefinite duration.
The Outcome: The Act failed the congruence and proportionality test. It was "substantive" (changing the law) rather than "remedial" (fixing a proven constitutional wrong).
II. Intellectual Property and the Boerne Standard
The Court has consistently held that federal uniformity in IP law does not override the 11th Amendment.
Copyrights (Allen v. Cooper, 2020): Even with evidence that copyright holders would suffer "immediate harm," the Court struck down the Copyright Remedy Clarification Act.
The Rule: The "imbalance" between the lack of evidence of constitutional harm by states and the "expansive" federal remedy resulted in a failure of the Boerne test.
III. The Role of Congressional Findings
A recurring theme in your notes is the sufficiency of the Legislative Record.
The Question: Can Congress "save" a law by simply compiling more data and findings of state misconduct?
The Warning Shot: As seen in the upcoming United States v. Morrison (VAWA), even a voluminous record of private violence and state indifference may be insufficient if the remedy targets the wrong actors or invades the wrong sphere of power.
🏛️ Professor Fisher’s "Virtual Auditor" Notes
Fisher’s "Checkmate" Strategy: You must distinguish between Private Infringement and State Constitutional Violations.
In Florida Prepaid, it wasn't enough to show that a state infringed a patent (a tort).
To use § 5, Congress had to show the state infringed the patent AND provided no process for a remedy (a constitutional violation). The Fisher Lesson: If the state has a court system that could hear the case, Congress cannot use § 5 to drag the state into Federal Court.
🎓 1L Exam Tip (Short & Happy Method)
When a federal law allows a private person to sue a State for money:
Identify the Power Source: It must be § 5 of the 14th Amendment. (Art. I Commerce Power is a "no-go" for money damages per Seminole Tribe).
Audit the Record: Did Congress find a "pattern of unconstitutional behavior" by the States themselves, or just by private people?
The Proportionality Check: Is the law limited to the "bad" states, or does it sue everyone? If it's a nationwide "sledgehammer," it's unconstitutional.
United States v. Morrison
529 U.S. 598, 120 S. Ct. 1740, 146 L. Ed. 2d 658 (2000).
Case Brief: United States v. Morrison (2000)
FACTS: Christy Brzonkala, a student at Virginia Tech, alleged she was assaulted and raped by two football players, Antonio Morrison and James Crawford. After the university failed to provide a satisfactory remedy, Brzonkala sued the players in federal court under the Violence Against Women Act of 1994 (VAWA). Specifically, she invoked 42 U.S.C. § 13981, which provided a federal civil remedy for victims of gender-motivated violence. Congress had compiled a massive record showing that gender-motivated violence costs the economy billions and that state justice systems often failed to provide adequate protection or prosecution for such crimes due to "discriminatory bias."
ISSUE: Does Section 5 of the Fourteenth Amendment grant Congress the power to provide a federal civil remedy against private individuals for gender-motivated violence?
I — ISSUE
Whether Section 5 of the Fourteenth Amendment authorizes Congress to regulate purely private conduct (violence by one citizen against another) when the "pattern of unconstitutional behavior" is actually the State's failure to prosecute those private actors.
R — RULE
The State Action Barrier: The Fourteenth Amendment "erects no shield against merely private conduct, however discriminatory or wrongful." Civil Rights Cases (1883).
The Remedial Constraint: Section 5 power is remedial and preventive, not substantive. It must be "congruent and proportional" to the injury to be prevented. City of Boerne (1997).
Targeting the Wrongdoer: A Section 5 remedy must be directed at the State actor committing the constitutional violation, not a private actor.
A — APPLICATION
The Private Conduct Problem: Chief Justice Rehnquist, writing for the majority, held that VAWA’s civil remedy was directed at private individuals, not state officials. Even though Congress found that state systems were biased (a state action problem), the law allowed victims to sue the attacker (a private actor).
The Boerne Checkmate: The Court found the remedy lacked congruence and proportionality. Because the law applied nationwide, even in states with no documented bias, it was too broad.
Rejection of the "Failure to Act" Theory: The Court rejected the argument that Congress could regulate private parties to "remedy" the State's failure to provide equal protection. If the State is the one failing, the remedy must be directed at the State (e.g., suing the police or the court system), not the private citizen.
Dissent (Souter/Breyer): Argued that the "State Action" requirement should be viewed more flexibly in light of the massive legislative record showing that state bias effectively denied women the equal protection of the laws.
C — CONCLUSION
The Supreme Court held the civil remedy provision of VAWA unconstitutional. Congress cannot use Section 5 to regulate purely private conduct. The "State Action" requirement remains an unyielding barrier that prevents the Fourteenth Amendment from becoming a general federal "police power" over individual crimes.
🏛️ Professor Fisher’s "Virtual Auditor" Notes
Fisher’s "Checkmate" Strategy: This is a lesson in Constitutional Geography. Congress tried to fix a problem at Point A (State Bias) by regulating Point B (Private Violence). Rehnquist’s logic is a procedural "Checkmate": You cannot remedy a State's omission by punishing a private person's commission.
The Record is Irrelevant: This is the most dangerous part for a 1L. Students think a "voluminous record" saves a statute. Morrison proves that if the Target of the law is constitutionally improper (a private citizen), no amount of data can make it "congruent and proportional."
🎓 1L Exam Tip (Short & Happy Method)
When analyzing a Section 5 law:
Identify the Defendant: Is the person being sued a Government Official or a Private Citizen?
If Private: The law is almost certainly unconstitutional under Morrison and the Civil Rights Cases.
The Only Out: Does the private conduct fall under the Thirteenth Amendment (Race/Badges of Slavery)? If no, the federal government has no business in a "general tort" suit.
Legal Source Note: For the definitive limit on Section 5 as applied to gender-motivated violence, see United States v. Morrison, 529 U.S. 598 (2000).
Would you like me to run a comparative analysis between the commerce clause failure and the 14th Amendment failure in this case to ensure you have the full "Fisher-level" view?
The Meaning and Scope of Morrison
These notes analyze the "Congruence and Proportionality" test in its most complex phase: the application to suspect vs. non-suspect classifications. As Virtual Law Emeritus, I have synthesized these materials to highlight the "Checkmate" distinction between Heightened Scrutiny (easier for Congress to regulate) and Rational Basis (harder for Congress to regulate).
I. The Threshold: Tier of Scrutiny as a Gateway
For Professor Fisher, the tier of scrutiny applied to the classification under § 1 of the 14th Amendment dictates the "thickness" of the legislative record required under § 5.
1. The "Rational Basis" Barrier (Age & Disability)
The Rule: If the Court reviews a classification under Rational Basis (Age/Disability), it is very difficult for Congress to prove a "pattern of unconstitutional behavior" by states. Why? Because most state distinctions in these areas are "rational" and thus constitutional.
Age Discrimination (Kimel v. Board of Regents): The ADEA failed because age is not a suspect class. Congress identified no pattern of unconstitutional age discrimination by states. The law was a "sledgehammer" that prohibited many rational (and therefore legal) state actions.
Disability Discrimination (Board of Trustees v. Garrett): Title I of the ADA failed for similar reasons. The Court found the legislative record was merely "anecdotal" and focused on local governments (who don't have 11th Amendment immunity) rather than the States themselves.
2. The "Heightened Scrutiny" Gateway (Gender)
The Rule: If a classification triggers Heightened Scrutiny (Gender), it is easier for Congress to show a pattern of unconstitutional state behavior.
The Success (Nevada v. Hibbs): The FMLA's family-care provision was upheld. Because gender classifications are "presumptively unconstitutional," Congress had a much lighter burden to show that state leave policies were based on invalid gender stereotypes.
II. The "Fundamental Rights" Exception (Tennessee v. Lane)
Even if a group (like the disabled) only gets Rational Basis review, Congress can still win if the law protects a Fundamental Right (which triggers strict scrutiny).
The Distinction: While Title I (Employment) failed in Garrett, Title II (Public Services) succeeded in Lane—but only as applied to Access to the Courts.
The Logic: Access to courts is a fundamental Due Process right. Therefore, Congress's "prophylactic" requirement for elevators/ramps in courthouses was "congruent and proportional" to the goal of protecting a high-level constitutional right.
III. The "Targeting" Requirement (Morrison & Coleman)
The Court has made it clear that a "suspect class" (Gender) is not a blank check for Congress.
The Private Actor Problem (Morrison): VAWA failed because it allowed victims to sue private individuals. Section 5 only allows Congress to regulate State Actors.
The Indirect Remedy Problem (Coleman v. Maryland): While the Hibbs family-care provision was valid, the self-care provision (sick leave for oneself) was struck down. The Court found that "self-care" leave was too attenuated from the "evil" of gender discrimination to be a proportional remedy.
IV. Comparative Framework: Summary of Section 5 Cases
Export to Sheets
🏛️ Professor Fisher’s "Virtual Auditor" Notes
Fisher’s "Checkmate" Strategy: You must distinguish between Abrogation and Remedy.
If a federal law allows a private citizen to sue a State for Money, the Boerne test is at its peak.
If the plaintiff only seeks Injunctive Relief (making the state stop doing something) via Ex Parte Young, they can often bypass this entire 11th Amendment/Section 5 headache.
🎓 1L Exam Tip (Short & Happy Method)
When a federal statute is challenged on Section 5 grounds:
Identify the Class: Is it Race/Gender (Suspect/Quasi-Suspect) or Age/Disability (Non-Suspect)?
Identify the Right: Is it a Fundamental Right (Voting, Courts) or a non-fundamental one (Employment)?
The Result: If it is a Non-Suspect Class plus a Non-Fundamental Right, the law is almost certainly unconstitutional under Kimel and Garrett because the record of "unconstitutional" state behavior will be too thin.
